Self-Paced Workbook · 30 Days · 60–90 Minutes a Day

30-Day Basic Azure Learning Workshop

A step-by-step, hands-on workshop that takes a complete beginner from “what is the cloud?” to deploying, securing, monitoring, and automating a real workload on Microsoft Azure — no trainer required.

Author: Dr. Sivakumar Madesan
Approach each day in order: Learn → Watch → Practice → Verify → Document → Assess.

License & Usage

Free for personal learning. You may use this workshop for your own self-study at no cost.

Commercial use requires written permission. Using this material in paid training, corporate workshops, bootcamps, or any other commercial offering — or redistributing it for commercial purposes — requires prior written permission from the author.

📧 Contact for commercial licensing: inventsiva@gmail.com

Workshop Overview

Who this workshop is for

Beginners, IT professionals, software developers, infrastructure engineers, DevOps learners, and career changers. You need basic IT knowledge (you can use a browser, install software, and have heard of servers and networks) — no cloud experience is assumed.

What you need before Day 1

  • A computer with a modern browser (Edge or Chrome recommended) and a reliable internet connection.
  • A Microsoft account (a free Outlook.com account works).
  • For the Azure free account (created on Day 1): a phone number and a credit/debit card. The card is used for identity verification only — Azure will not charge it unless you explicitly move to pay-as-you-go. A small temporary verification hold (about $1) may appear and is removed.
  • Optional but recommended: Visual Studio Code (free) for the Bicep and Git days.

Cost safety — read this first

An Azure free account includes a credit (currently $200 for the first 30 days) plus monthly free amounts of selected services. Details change; always confirm the current offer at azure.microsoft.com/free. Three rules keep you safe:

  • Clean up after every lab. Every hands-on section in this workshop ends with cleanup steps — usually deleting the resource group. Do them the same day.
  • A budget is an alarm, not a wall. The budget alerts you set on Day 19 notify you by email; they do not stop resources or cap spending, and usage data can be delayed. Deleting resources is the only reliable stop.
  • Never share secrets. Never paste passwords, access keys, or connection strings into code, chats, screenshots, or GitHub. This workshop teaches Key Vault and managed identities instead.

Learning outcomes — by Day 30 you will be able to

  1. Explain cloud computing and core Azure concepts.
  2. Navigate the Azure portal and identify important Azure services.
  3. Understand subscriptions, resource groups, regions, and resource management.
  4. Understand Microsoft Entra ID, users, groups, roles, and RBAC.
  5. Create and manage basic Azure compute resources.
  6. Understand Azure virtual networks, subnets, DNS, and network security groups.
  7. Create and manage Azure Storage resources.
  8. Deploy a basic web application using Azure App Service.
  9. Understand Azure Functions and serverless computing.
  10. Use Azure Key Vault and understand secure secrets management.
  11. Monitor resources using Azure Monitor, Log Analytics, and alerts.
  12. Understand Azure cost management, budgets, and resource cleanup.
  13. Perform basic infrastructure deployment using Azure CLI and introductory Bicep.
  14. Complete a final practical project and explain its architecture.

This workshop builds practical skills. Completing it does not by itself certify or professionally qualify you — Day 30 maps your next steps toward official Microsoft credentials. Azure portal screens, service names, and prices change over time; where this workbook and the live portal disagree, follow the portal and the official documentation.

Day 1 — Introduction to Cloud Computing and Microsoft Azure60–90 minutes

A. Day 1 — Introduction to Cloud Computing and Microsoft Azure

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain in your own words what cloud computing is and why businesses use it
  • Name the six main benefits of cloud computing (high availability, scalability, elasticity, agility, geo-distribution, disaster recovery)
  • Describe the difference between CapEx (capital expenditure) and OpEx (operational expenditure)
  • Create a free Azure account and sign in to the Azure portal for the first time

C. Prerequisites

  • No Azure account needed — creating it is today's lab
  • A computer with a web browser and internet access
  • An email address you can use for a Microsoft account (or an existing Microsoft account)
  • A phone number and a credit or debit card for identity verification (the card is NOT charged)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Cloud computing is the delivery of computing services — servers, storage, databases, networking, software, and more — over the internet (“the cloud”) on a pay-as-you-go basis. Instead of buying and owning your own physical servers, you rent capacity from a provider such as Microsoft Azure, and you can turn that capacity up or down whenever you want.

Think of it like electricity. A century ago, factories ran their own generators — expensive, hard to maintain, and wasteful when demand was low. Then the power grid arrived: you plug in, use what you need, and get a bill for what you consumed. The cloud is the same idea for computing power. You don't own the power plant; you just turn the switch.

Why does this matter? Because it flips spending from CapEx (capital expenditure — big up-front purchases of hardware) to OpEx (operational expenditure — a monthly bill for what you actually used). No data center to build, no servers to cool, no hardware to replace. It also gives you elasticity (resources grow or shrink automatically with demand), scalability (the ability to handle more work), high availability (your app stays up even if hardware fails), and geo-distribution (you can run your app close to your customers anywhere in the world).

Microsoft Azure is Microsoft's cloud platform: 200+ services across computing, storage, networking, databases, AI, and security, running in data centers in 60+ regions worldwide. This workshop uses Azure because it is the cloud most commonly found in enterprise companies — and the skills transfer to other clouds too.

A note on honesty: portal screens, free-tier limits, and pricing change over time. If a button in this workbook has moved, use the portal's search bar at the top — it is the fastest way to find anything.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create your Azure free account and sign in to the Azure portal for the first time.

Prerequisites: Web browser, an email address, a phone number (for a text/call verification code), and a credit or debit card for identity verification.

Cost note: Free. The card is used for identity verification only and is not charged for the free account. You get $200 in credit for the first 30 days.

Step 1. Open your browser and go to https://azure.microsoft.com/free/ . Read the page so you understand what the free account includes, then click the “Start free” button.

Expected result: You land on the Microsoft sign-in page.

Verify: The page says “Start free” or shows a Microsoft account sign-in prompt.

Troubleshooting: If the page is blank, disable ad-blocker extensions for this site and reload.

Step 2. Sign in with your existing Microsoft account. If you don't have one, click “Create one” and follow the prompts using your email address.

Expected result: You reach the Azure signup form asking for personal details.

Verify: You see fields for country/region, name, and contact information.

Troubleshooting: If you get stuck in a sign-in loop, open a private/incognito window and try again.

Step 3. Fill in your country/region, name, and phone number. Microsoft will send a verification code by text or call — enter it when prompted.

Expected result: Phone verification succeeds.

Verify: The form moves to the payment/identity verification step.

Troubleshooting: If the code doesn't arrive within a few minutes, click “Resend code”; check that your phone can receive international SMS.

Step 4. Enter your credit or debit card details. This is for identity verification only — the free account is not charged. Prepaid cards are often not accepted.

Expected result: Card is accepted and a small temporary authorization may appear.

Verify: The form advances to the agreement step.

Troubleshooting: If your card is rejected, try a different major credit/debit card. Virtual or prepaid cards usually fail verification.

Step 5. Review and accept the subscription agreement, then click “Sign up”.

Expected result: A “Your Azure free account is ready” or similar confirmation page appears.

Verify: You see a confirmation message and a “Go to the portal” button.

Troubleshooting: If signup hangs, wait 2 minutes and reload — do not submit the form twice.

Step 6. Click “Go to the portal” (or go to https://portal.azure.com and sign in with the same account).

Expected result: The Azure portal home page loads, showing a search bar at the top and a left-hand menu.

Verify: Your email address appears in the top-right corner of the portal.

Troubleshooting: If you see an error page, sign out completely and sign back in at portal.azure.com.

Step 7. In the portal search bar at the top, type “Subscriptions” and open it. Confirm you see a subscription named something like “Azure subscription 1” with status Active.

Expected result: One active subscription is listed — this is your free subscription.

Verify: Status shows “Active” and the offer shows your free trial.

Step 8. Take a screenshot or write down your subscription name. You'll need it later. Then sign out and sign back in once to prove to yourself you can get in again.

Expected result: You can sign back in without help.

Verify: Second sign-in works on the first try.

Troubleshooting: Save the portal URL (portal.azure.com) as a browser bookmark.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Nothing to clean up — this lab creates no billable resources, only the free account itself. Keep the account; every remaining day of the workshop builds on it.

G. Practical Assignment

Without looking at your notes, explain to a friend (or write in your journal) the difference between CapEx and OpEx, and give one business reason a company would move a workload to the cloud. Write down their/your one follow-up question.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is cloud computing?
    • A) Buying physical servers and installing them in your office
    • B) Delivering computing services over the internet on a pay-as-you-go basis
    • C) A type of computer hardware sold by Microsoft
    • D) Storing files only on your local hard drive
  2. Q2. A company stops buying its own servers and instead pays a monthly Azure bill based on usage. It has moved from:
    • A) OpEx to CapEx
    • B) CapEx to OpEx
    • C) IaaS to SaaS
    • D) Public cloud to private cloud
  3. Q3. Which term describes resources automatically growing or shrinking to match demand?
    • A) Elasticity
    • B) Geo-distribution
    • C) High availability
    • D) Fault tolerance
  4. Q4. Microsoft Azure is best described as:
    • A) A single data center in Washington state
    • B) Microsoft's cloud platform offering 200+ services across 60+ global regions
    • C) A version of Windows for servers
    • D) A free email service
  5. Q5. Practical check: sign in to portal.azure.com, open Subscriptions, and confirm your free subscription shows status Active. Have you done this?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Cloud computing means renting servers, storage, databases and more over the internet and paying only for what you use — not owning the hardware yourself.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. CapEx is up-front capital spending on owned assets; OpEx is ongoing operational spending. Cloud's pay-as-you-go model shifts cost from CapEx to OpEx.
  3. Q3 — Answer: A. Elasticity is the automatic scaling of resources up or down with demand. Scalability is the ability to handle more work; availability is staying up during failures.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Azure is Microsoft's full cloud platform — compute, storage, networking, databases, AI and more — delivered from data centers worldwide.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Done — I can sign in to portal.azure.com and see my Active free subscription listed.. Practical check — the account from today's lab is the foundation for the rest of the workshop.

Day 2 — IaaS, PaaS, SaaS, Shared Responsibility, and Cloud Deployment Models60–90 minutes

A. Day 2 — IaaS, PaaS, SaaS, Shared Responsibility, and Cloud Deployment Models

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Define IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service), PaaS (Platform as a Service), and SaaS (Software as a Service) with a real example of each
  • Explain the shared responsibility model and state who is ALWAYS responsible for data and access
  • Distinguish the four cloud deployment models: public, private, hybrid, and multi-cloud
  • Use the Azure pricing calculator to produce a monthly cost estimate for a simple workload

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Yesterday you learned the cloud is like the power grid. Today we get precise about how much of the work you rent versus how much you keep. Cloud services come in three flavors, and the pizza analogy is the classic way to remember them.

IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service) is like ordering a pizza kit: the shop delivers the dough, sauce, and cheese, but you assemble and bake it at home. In the cloud, the provider gives you the raw ingredients — VMs (Virtual Machines), storage, and networks — and you install the operating system, patch it, and run your software. Maximum control, maximum work. Example: Azure Virtual Machines.

PaaS (Platform as a Service) is like ordering a ready-made pizza: the shop bakes it, you just eat. The provider manages the operating system, runtime, and patching — you only bring your code and data. Less control, much less maintenance. Example: Azure App Service, where you deploy a website and never touch a server.

SaaS (Software as a Service) is like dining at the restaurant: everything is done for you, you just show up and eat. Finished software delivered over the internet — you configure settings and use it. Example: Microsoft 365, Salesforce, Gmail.

The shared responsibility model describes who does what. In every model Microsoft secures the physical data centers, the network, and the hosts. You ALWAYS own your data, your user accounts, and who can access what. The key rule: the higher up the stack you go (IaaS → PaaS → SaaS), the more the provider manages and the less you do — but your responsibility for data and access never disappears.

Finally, the deployment models: public cloud (shared resources, pay-as-you-go — like Azure itself), private cloud (dedicated to one organization), hybrid cloud (public and private working together), and multi-cloud (using more than one provider, e.g. Azure plus another cloud). Most large companies end up hybrid or multi-cloud.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Use the Azure pricing calculator to estimate the monthly cost of a simple VM plus a managed database, so cost becomes a concrete number instead of an abstract idea.

Prerequisites: Azure free account from Day 1 (sign-in not required for the calculator, but keep your subscription handy for comparison).

Cost note: Free. This lab uses only the pricing calculator and creates zero billable resources.

Step 1. Go to https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/ . Spend one minute looking at the layout: product list on the left, your running estimate at the bottom.

Expected result: The pricing calculator loads with an empty estimate.

Verify: You see a search box or product list and an estimate panel showing $0.00.

Troubleshooting: If the page loads slowly, it may be your ad blocker — allow this page and reload.

Step 2. In the product search, type “Virtual Machines” and add it to the estimate. Leave the defaults for now — this represents one basic IaaS server.

Expected result: Virtual Machines is added to your estimate with a default price.

Verify: Your estimate panel shows a non-zero monthly price for Virtual Machines.

Step 3. Change the VM configuration: set the region to the one nearest you (you'll learn what regions are on Day 3), and change the operating system to Linux. Watch how the price moves.

Expected result: The VM price changes as you adjust the settings.

Verify: The monthly estimate updates when you change region or OS.

Troubleshooting: If your region isn't listed, pick the closest available one — this is only an estimate.

Step 4. Add “Azure SQL Database” to the same estimate. Set it to the Basic tier. This represents a PaaS database — Microsoft manages the server, you just use the database.

Expected result: Azure SQL Database appears in the estimate with its own price line.

Verify: The estimate now shows two lines: Virtual Machines and Azure SQL Database.

Step 5. Scroll to the bottom and read the total monthly estimate. Write it down, along with one line explaining what each item pays for.

Expected result: You can read the total and explain both line items.

Verify: You have a written number and two one-line explanations.

Step 6. Change the SQL Database from Basic tier to General Purpose tier, then back. Notice how PaaS pricing steps up with performance — more power, more cost.

Expected result: The estimate recalculates to a lower or higher number.

Verify: You can describe in one sentence how tier changes affect price.

Step 7. Find the disclaimer text on the calculator page (usually near the estimate) and read it. Calculator numbers are planning figures, not a contract.

Expected result: You see a disclaimer that estimates are not quotes and actual billing may vary.

Verify: You can state in your own words why an estimate is not a final bill.

Step 8. In your journal, write: “My simple VM + database would cost about $X/month in region Y. The two biggest cost drivers I noticed are Z.” Save or export the estimate if you want to compare later.

Expected result: You have your own cost number, not Microsoft's example.

Verify: Your journal entry has X, Y, and Z filled in.

Troubleshooting: Use the calculator's share/export option if you want a link — it contains no personal data.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Nothing to clean up — the pricing calculator is a planning tool and creates no resources. Do not click any “Buy” or “Deploy” button during the lab; the goal is an estimate, not a purchase.

G. Practical Assignment

Open your Azure subscription in the portal and find three services you could recognize from today: one IaaS example, one PaaS example, and one SaaS example Microsoft offers (hint: look at Microsoft's product list). Write down one sentence for each explaining why it belongs in that category.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. You want to deploy a web app's code without installing or patching the operating system or web server yourself. Which model fits?
    • A) IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service)
    • B) SaaS (Software as a Service)
    • C) PaaS (Platform as a Service)
    • D) On-premises
  2. Q2. Which statement about the shared responsibility model is TRUE?
    • A) The cloud provider manages more of the stack as you move from IaaS to PaaS to SaaS
    • B) Microsoft manages everything in all models, including your data
    • C) You manage everything in all models
    • D) The responsibility split is identical for IaaS, PaaS, and SaaS
  3. Q3. A bank runs all workloads on servers it owns in its own data center, managed by its own staff. Which deployment model is this?
    • A) Public cloud
    • B) Private cloud
    • C) Hybrid cloud
    • D) Multi-cloud
  4. Q4. Microsoft 365 (Outlook, Word, Teams in the browser) is an example of:
    • A) IaaS
    • B) PaaS
    • C) Hybrid cloud
    • D) SaaS
  5. Q5. Practical: draw a table with four rows (on-premises, IaaS, PaaS, SaaS) and three columns: who manages the hardware, who manages the operating system, and one real-world example. Have you completed it?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: C. In PaaS (Platform as a Service) the provider manages the operating system, runtime, and patching; you focus on your code and data. IaaS still leaves the OS to you; SaaS needs no code at all.
  2. Q2 — Answer: A. The shared responsibility model always changes with the model: the provider takes over more as you move from on-premises → IaaS → PaaS → SaaS, while you always own your data and access.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. A private cloud runs on infrastructure dedicated to one organization (your own data center or a hosted one). Public cloud resources are shared and pay-as-you-go.
  4. Q4 — Answer: D. SaaS (Software as a Service) is finished software delivered over the internet — the provider runs everything and you just use it.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Done — my table has the four models, who manages what for each, and at least one real example per model.. Practical reinforcement of IaaS vs PaaS vs SaaS vs on-premises and the responsibility split.

Day 3 — Azure Regions, Availability Zones, Subscriptions, and Resource Groups60–90 minutes

A. Day 3 — Azure Regions, Availability Zones, Subscriptions, and Resource Groups

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what an Azure region is and why the region you choose matters (latency and data residency)
  • Describe availability zones and region pairs and how they protect against failures
  • Explain the subscription as the billing and access boundary
  • Create a resource group in the portal and set up a budget alert, understanding that budgets are alerts — not spending caps

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Azure runs in data centers all over the world, but you never deploy “to the internet” — you deploy to a specific place. That place is called a region: a set of data centers within a geographic boundary (for example, “East US” in Virginia or “Central India” in Pune), connected by a low-latency network.

Think of regions like warehouse districts. A retail chain doesn't ship everything from one giant warehouse — it keeps stock in warehouses near its customers so deliveries are fast. Cloud regions work the same way: put your app in a region near your users and responses are faster. Regions also matter for law — some data must legally stay inside a country, and choosing a region in that country keeps you compliant.

Inside many regions, Azure offers Availability Zones: physically separate buildings within the same region, each with independent power, cooling, and networking. If one zone loses power, the other zones keep running. Spreading your app across zones is how you survive a data-center failure without leaving the region. Related idea: region pairs — Azure pairs each region with another region at least 300 miles away, so if a whole region ever goes down, you have a planned place to recover.

Now the money and organization side. A subscription is your billing and access boundary — it's the account Azure bills, and the unit you grant people access to. Inside a subscription you organize resources with resource groups: logical containers that hold related resources (a web app, its database, and its storage account might live in one group called “my-website”). Deleting a resource group deletes everything inside it, which makes cleanup wonderfully simple — one delete, and the lab is gone.

Before you create anything billable in this workshop, learn the golden rule of Azure spending: budgets are ALERTS, not spending caps. A budget watches your spending and emails you when you cross a threshold — but it does not stop spending, and the alert email can arrive hours after the spending happened. Use budgets as an early-warning system, and always delete resources you no longer need.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a resource group in your chosen region and set up a $50 budget alert so you have an early-warning system for spending.

Prerequisites: Azure free account from Day 1, signed in to the Azure portal.

Cost note: Free. Resource groups and budgets cost nothing. The budget is an alert only — it never blocks or caps spending.

Step 1. Open the Azure portal and use the top search bar to find your subscription from Day 1. Before creating anything, search the web or portal for the list of Azure regions and pick the region closest to where you live. Write it down.

Expected result: The region list page or documentation shows region names like “East US” and “Central India”.

Verify: You have one region name written down, e.g. “East US” or “Central India”.

Troubleshooting: If you're unsure which is closest, pick the region in your own country — data-residency laws often require it.

Step 2. In the portal search bar, type “Resource groups” and open it. Click “+ Create”.

Expected result: The “Create a resource group” form opens.

Verify: A form asks for Subscription, Resource group name, and Region.

Troubleshooting: If you don't see + Create, check you're signed in to the right account — the one from Day 1.

Step 3. Select your free subscription. Name the group “lab-03” (lowercase, no spaces). Select the region you chose in step 1. Leave tags empty for now.

Expected result: The form shows your subscription, your group name, and your region.

Verify: All three fields are filled and the Review + create button is enabled.

Troubleshooting: If the name is rejected, it's already taken in your subscription — add your initials, e.g. “lab-03-sk”.

Step 4. Click “Review + create”, then “Create”. Wait for the deployment to finish — it usually takes under a minute.

Expected result: The portal reports “Deployment succeeded” and the group appears in the list.

Verify: “lab-03” appears in your Resource groups list.

Troubleshooting: A failed deployment here is rare; if it happens, note the error message and retry.

Step 5. In the portal search bar, type “Cost Management” and open it. This is where Azure shows what you've spent. Take 30 seconds to look at the overview — with a fresh free account it should be near zero.

Expected result: Cost Management opens showing your subscription's spending views.

Verify: You can see a spending overview for your subscription.

Troubleshooting: If Cost Management shows an error, wait a few minutes — billing data can lag on new accounts.

Step 6. In Cost Management, go to “Budgets” in the left menu and click “+ Add” (or “Create budget”). Remember today's rule: this creates an ALERT, not a spending cap.

Expected result: The budget creation form opens.

Verify: A form asks for budget name, amount, and alert conditions.

Troubleshooting: If Budgets is missing from the menu, make sure your subscription — not a management group — is selected at the top.

Step 7. Name the budget “lab-03-alert”, set the amount to 50 USD, and add an alert condition at 80% of the budget. For the alert email, enter your own email address. Save it.

Expected result: The budget is saved and listed as Active.

Verify: Your budget appears in the Budgets list with status Active.

Troubleshooting: Currency defaults to your billing currency — that's fine; keep the amount equivalent to about $50.

Step 8. Open the budget you just created and confirm: amount ≈ $50, alert at 80%, and your email listed under alert recipients.

Expected result: The alert condition shows 80% with your email as the recipient.

Verify: All three settings are visible and correct.

Troubleshooting: If the email field is empty, edit the budget and add it — otherwise the alert has nowhere to go.

Step 9. In your journal, write one sentence completing this: “If my spending hits 80% of $50, Azure will email me, but the alert might arrive late and it will NOT stop my spending, so I must...” Finish the sentence yourself.

Expected result: You can explain the alert's limits in your own words.

Verify: Your journal has a completed, honest sentence.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete both the lab-03 resource group and (if you made one) the rg-personal resource group from the assignment: Portal → Resource groups → select each group → Delete resource group → type the name to confirm → Delete. Then delete the budget: Cost Management → Budgets → select your budget → Delete. Confirm both groups are gone from the Resource groups list.

G. Practical Assignment

Open the portal, go to Resource groups, and create a second resource group named rg-personal in the same region you used today. Then delete it immediately. Write down in your journal: what did the portal warn you about before deleting, and how long did the deletion take?

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is an Azure region?
    • A) A single physical building
    • B) One availability zone
    • C) A set of data centers in a geographic area connected by a low-latency network
    • D) A billing account
  2. Q2. You need your app to survive a single data-center power failure inside one Azure region. The best design is:
    • A) Two VMs in the same data center rack
    • B) Copies of an application placed in different availability zones within the region
    • C) Backups stored on your laptop
    • D) Running the app in two different subscriptions
  3. Q3. Which statement about subscriptions and resource groups is TRUE?
    • A) Many resource groups can live inside one subscription
    • B) A resource group can span multiple subscriptions
    • C) A subscription can exist inside a resource group
    • D) Resource groups and subscriptions are the same thing
  4. Q4. What happens when you delete a resource group?
    • A) The resources move to another resource group automatically
    • B) The resources keep running but become free
    • C) Only the resource group's name is deleted
    • D) All resources inside it are deleted
  5. Q5. Practical: confirm your lab-03 resource group appears in the portal and your $50 budget alert is listed. Have you done this?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: C. A region is a set of data centers deployed within a geographic boundary. Availability zones are separate buildings WITHIN one region — a finer subdivision, not the same thing.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Each availability zone is a physically separate location within the region with independent power, cooling, and networking — surviving the failure of another zone.
  3. Q3 — Answer: A. Subscriptions are the billing and access boundary; resource groups are organizational containers inside a subscription. You can have many resource groups per subscription.
  4. Q4 — Answer: D. Resource groups are logical containers — they hold metadata about which subscription manages resources. Deleting the container deletes everything in it; the resources do not magically relocate.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Done — my resource group exists in my chosen region and my budget alert is visible under Cost Management → Budgets.. Practical confirmation that the learner can organize resources and set up spending alerts.

Day 4 — Azure Portal, Cloud Shell, Azure CLI, and PowerShell Introduction60–90 minutes

A. Day 4 — Azure Portal, Cloud Shell, Azure CLI, and PowerShell Introduction

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Describe what the Azure portal is and navigate it using the search bar
  • Launch Azure Cloud Shell and explain why it needs no installation
  • Create a resource group with the Azure CLI, explaining each part of the command
  • List resource groups with Azure PowerShell and recognize the verb-noun cmdlet pattern
  • Compare the Azure CLI and Azure PowerShell approaches to the same task

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Day 3 (resource groups, regions)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

So far you've driven Azure with a mouse. Professionals also drive it with typed commands — faster, repeatable, and automatable. Today you meet the four ways to talk to Azure.

The Azure portal (portal.azure.com) is the graphical website: point, click, and create. Think of it as a car's dashboard — dials and buttons, easy to learn, perfect when you're starting out. Its best feature is the search bar at the top: type any service name and jump straight there.

Azure Cloud Shell is a terminal that lives inside the portal — click the >_ icon in the top bar and a command prompt opens in your browser. No installation, nothing to configure; the Azure tools are already there. It gives you two shells: Bash (the Linux world's shell) and PowerShell (Microsoft's shell). Files you save in Cloud Shell persist in a small Azure storage account created for you. Think of Cloud Shell as a workshop terminal bolted to the wall of the Azure factory — always there, always ready.

The Azure CLI is the command-line tool you use inside Bash (and it works in PowerShell and on your own laptop too). Its commands read like sentences: az group create --name lab-04 --location eastus means “Azure, create a resource group named lab-04 in East US.” The pattern is always az <what> <action> --parameters.

Azure PowerShell is Microsoft's alternative, built on PowerShell's verb-noun pattern: Get-AzResourceGroup (Get = the verb, AzResourceGroup = the noun). Same cloud, different dialect. In this workshop you'll mostly use the Azure CLI, but you should recognize PowerShell cmdlets because many companies use them.

Why learn commands at all? Because clicking through the portal 50 times is how mistakes happen. A command can be saved, reviewed, and run identically every time — that is the foundation of automation, which you'll meet later in the workshop.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
What is Azure Cloud ShellDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-shell/overview VerifiedOfficial overview of Cloud Shell: features, the storage it creates, and how files persist between sessions.10 min
Install the Azure CLIDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/install-azure-cli VerifiedReference for later, when you want the CLI on your own computer instead of only in Cloud Shell.10 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Launch Cloud Shell, create a resource group with the Azure CLI, and list resource groups with Azure PowerShell — the same task in two command languages.

Prerequisites: Azure free account from Day 1, signed in to the Azure portal.

Cost note: Free tier covers normal use. Cloud Shell creates a small storage account for your files; typical cost is a few cents per month at most, and deleting it (see cleanup) removes it.

Step 1. In the Azure portal, click the Cloud Shell icon (>_) in the top bar, next to the search box.

Expected result: A terminal pane opens at the bottom of the portal.

Verify: A terminal panel slides up from the bottom of the portal.

Troubleshooting: If the icon is missing, you may be on a narrow screen — widen the window or use the portal menu.

Step 2. When asked to choose an environment, pick Bash. Cloud Shell will ask to create a storage account for your files — click Create. (This storage is tiny; see the cost note.)

Expected result: A Bash prompt appears and Cloud Shell is ready.

Verify: You see a Bash prompt like yourname@Azure:~$.

Troubleshooting: If storage creation fails, try a different region in the prompt — any region works.

Step 3. Type: az account show --output table — then press Enter. Breakdown: `az` = the Azure CLI; `account show` = “show me the current account”; `--output table` = “display it as a table”.

Expected result: A table shows your subscription name, ID, and that it is the default.

Verify: Your subscription name from Day 1 appears in the output.

Troubleshooting: If the command isn't found, reload Cloud Shell — the CLI is preinstalled, so a fresh session fixes it.

Step 4. Type: az group list --output table — then press Enter. `group list` = “list all resource groups”. Confirm you see the lab-03 group from Day 3.

Expected result: A table lists lab-03 and any other groups you have.

Verify: lab-03 appears in the table.

Troubleshooting: If lab-03 is missing, you may be in the wrong subscription — run `az account list --output table` to check.

Step 5. Type: az group create --name lab-04 --location <your-region> — replacing <your-region> with your Day 3 region name with no spaces (e.g. eastus). Breakdown: `group create` = the action; `--name` = what to call it; `--location` = which region.

Expected result: JSON output confirms the group was created, showing its name and location.

Verify: The output shows "name": "lab-04" and your region.

Troubleshooting: If the region name is rejected, run `az account list-locations --output table` and copy the exact Name column value.

Step 6. Type: az group show --name lab-04 --output table — to confirm the group exists exactly as you described it.

Expected result: The same lab-04 group is shown.

Verify: One row appears with name lab-04.

Step 7. In the Cloud Shell toolbar, use the environment dropdown (top-left of the terminal) to switch from Bash to PowerShell. Wait for it to connect — your sign-in carries over automatically.

Expected result: The prompt changes to PowerShell style (PS Azure:\>).

Verify: You see a PS prompt.

Troubleshooting: If PowerShell hangs on connecting, switch back to Bash and then to PowerShell once more.

Step 8. Type: Get-AzResourceGroup — then press Enter. Breakdown: `Get` = the verb (fetch); `AzResourceGroup` = the noun (what to fetch). This is the PowerShell dialect of the same “list my groups” request.

Expected result: A list of your resource groups appears, including lab-04.

Verify: lab-04 is in the list.

Troubleshooting: If you see a red error about not being connected, run Connect-AzAccount and follow the device-login prompt.

Step 9. Type: Get-AzResourceGroup -Name lab-04 — to fetch just the one group, mirroring the `az group show` you ran in Bash.

Expected result: Only the lab-04 group is returned.

Verify: A single result for lab-04 appears.

Step 10. In your journal, write one sentence comparing the two dialects: what felt similar (same cloud, same result) and what felt different (az … … --flags vs Verb-Noun).

Expected result: You can state one similarity and one difference in your own words.

Verify: Your journal has the comparison sentence.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the lab-04 resource group (Portal → Resource groups → lab-04 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete, or run: az group delete --name lab-04 --yes). Cloud Shell also created a small storage account in a resource group named like cloud-shell-storage-<region> — delete that resource group too if you want zero leftover footprint. Note: deleting it means Cloud Shell will recreate storage next time you launch it.

G. Practical Assignment

Without looking at your notes, write the Azure CLI command to create a resource group named rg-test in your region, and the PowerShell cmdlet to list all resource groups. Then open Cloud Shell and check whether you got them right. Fix any mistakes in your journal.

Day 5 — Microsoft Entra ID, Users, Groups, Authentication, and Identity Basics60–90 minutes

A. Day 5 — Microsoft Entra ID, Users, Groups, Authentication, and Identity Basics

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what Microsoft Entra ID is and what a tenant represents
  • Distinguish authentication (who you are) from authorization (what you can do)
  • Explain MFA (Multi-Factor Authentication) and why every account should use it
  • Create a user and a security group in the portal and add the user to the group
  • Verify a new user can sign in, and explain why a new user sees almost nothing

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Every office building has a badge system: it knows who works there, which badge opens which door, and it logs every entry. Microsoft Entra ID (formerly called Azure Active Directory) is the cloud version of that badge system. It is Microsoft's identity service — it stores user accounts and groups, and it handles signing in to Azure, Microsoft 365, and thousands of other apps.

Your badge system lives in a tenant: your organization's dedicated instance of Entra ID. When you created your Azure account on Day 1, Microsoft created a tenant for you automatically (named something like youname.onmicrosoft.com). Everything you do in Azure happens inside that tenant.

Two words you'll use constantly: authentication and authorization. Authentication (“authN”) answers who are you? — proving your identity with a password, a phone code, or biometrics. Authorization (“authZ”) answers what are you allowed to do? — and that decision happens after you're signed in. You'll master authorization tomorrow with RBAC; today is about proving identity.

The single biggest upgrade to authentication is MFA (Multi-Factor Authentication): requiring two or more proofs at sign-in, such as your password plus a code from your phone. Passwords get stolen every day; MFA means a stolen password alone is useless. Turn it on for your own account — it is the cheapest security improvement in all of cloud computing.

Finally, users and groups. A user is one person's account. A security group is a collection of users managed as one unit — instead of granting 200 developers access one by one, you grant it to the “Developers” group once. New hire? Add them to the group and they're set. This one habit — manage access through groups, not individuals — is what separates beginners from professionals.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Microsoft Learn Azure training hubMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/azure/ VerifiedSearch for the free “Microsoft Entra ID” modules here for deeper identity training after today's lab.30 min
Microsoft Entra ID overview (What is Microsoft Entra ID?)Documentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/fundamentals/whatis Needs verificationOfficial documentation explaining tenants, users, groups, and authentication concepts in more depth.20 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a test user and a security group in Microsoft Entra ID, add the user to the group, and verify the user can sign in.

Prerequisites: Azure free account from Day 1, signed in to the Azure portal as the account owner.

Cost note: Free. Entra ID user and group management costs nothing on the free tier.

Expected result: The Users list opens.

Expected result: The “Create new user” form opens.

Verify: You can describe in one sentence what the Lab User could see.

Troubleshooting: Seeing nothing here is not an error — it's proof that authentication (who you are) and authorization (what you can do) are separate.

Step 9. Back in your main browser (signed in as yourself), open Groups → Lab-Readers and confirm Lab User is still listed as a member. In your journal, write what surprised you about the Lab User's limited view.

Verify: Membership confirmed and journal entry written.

Day 6 — Azure RBAC, Least Privilege, and Access Management60–90 minutes

A. Day 6 — Azure RBAC, Least Privilege, and Access Management

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain Azure RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) using its three parts: role, security principal, and scope
  • Distinguish the Owner, Contributor, and Reader built-in roles
  • Explain the principle of least privilege and why narrow scopes matter
  • Assign a role to a group at resource-group scope in the portal
  • Verify effective access with Check access, then remove the assignment

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Day 5 completed (Lab User and Lab-Readers group exist)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Yesterday your Lab User signed in successfully — and could see almost nothing. That gap between “I know who you are” (authentication) and “here's what you may touch” (authorization) is exactly what Azure RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) fills.

Stay with the office-badge analogy: every badge in a building is programmed with permissions. The cleaner's badge opens supply closets, the engineer's opens the server room, the CEO's opens everything. RBAC is the system that programs the badges. An RBAC assignment has three parts: a role (the WHAT — a named set of allowed actions), a security principal (the WHO — a user, group, or application), and a scope (the WHERE — a subscription, a resource group, or a single resource).

Azure has three built-in roles you'll use constantly: Owner — full control, including managing who else has access (the master key). Contributor — can create, change, and delete resources, but cannot grant access to others. Reader — can look, but cannot change anything. (A fourth, User Access Administrator, manages access only.)

Scope matters enormously. Assign Contributor at the subscription scope and that person can change EVERY resource you own. Assign Reader at one resource group and they can only view that group. Permissions also inherit downward: a role given at the subscription level flows into every resource group and resource beneath it — like a master key that also opens every office on every floor.

The professional habit governing all of this is the principle of least privilege: give each person the smallest role, at the narrowest scope, for the shortest time their job requires. It is not about distrust — it's about damage control. If an account with only Reader access is compromised, the attacker can look but cannot delete or steal compute. A compromised Owner account, by contrast, can hand the entire cloud to an attacker in minutes. Least privilege is why you saw so few subscription Owners in real companies — usually two or three, never twenty.

One security habit to carry forward: never store passwords, keys, or connection strings in code or notes. When resources need secrets, Azure's pattern is Key Vault (a dedicated secrets store) and managed identities (Azure-issued identities for apps, so they never handle a password at all). You'll meet Key Vault properly later in the workshop.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure Key Vault overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/general/overview VerifiedBackground for the secrets pattern mentioned today: why secrets live in Key Vault with RBAC-controlled access, not in code or notes.15 min
Azure role-based access control (RBAC) overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/role-based-access-control/overview Needs verificationOfficial RBAC documentation: roles, scopes, inheritance, and how assignments are evaluated.25 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Assign the Reader role to your Lab User at resource-group scope, verify what they can see, then remove the assignment — practicing least privilege end to end.

Prerequisites: Azure free account (Day 1), the Lab User and Lab-Readers group from Day 5 (create them first if you deleted them), and the lab-03 resource group from Day 3.

Cost note: Free. RBAC assignments cost nothing, and this lab changes permissions only — it creates no billable resources.

Step 1. In the portal, go to Resource groups → lab-03 to open the group. In its left-hand menu, click “Access control (IAM)”. This blade is where all role assignments for this scope live.

Expected result: The Access control (IAM) page for lab-03 opens.

Verify: You see tabs like Check access, Role assignments, and Roles.

Troubleshooting: If IAM is missing from the menu, widen the browser window — the menu collapses on narrow screens.

Step 2. Click “+ Add” at the top, then “Add role assignment”.

Expected result: The Add role assignment wizard opens.

Verify: A list of roles appears (Owner, Contributor, Reader, and many more).

Step 3. In the role list, find and select “Reader”. Read its description — view everything, change nothing. Click “Next”.

Expected result: Reader is selected and highlighted.

Verify: Reader is the chosen role before you continue.

Troubleshooting: Use the search box in the wizard if the list is long — type “Reader”.

Step 4. On the Members tab, choose “User, group, or service principal”, click “+ Select members”, search for “Lab-Readers”, select the GROUP (not the individual user — assigning to groups is the professional habit), and click “Select”, then “Next”.

Expected result: Lab-Readers is selected as the assignee.

Verify: The Members tab shows Lab-Readers.

Troubleshooting: If Lab-Readers doesn't appear, confirm it still exists under Microsoft Entra ID → Groups.

Step 5. Click “Review + assign”, review the summary (Role: Reader, Scope: lab-03, Assigned to: Lab-Readers), then click “Review + assign” again to finish.

Expected result: The assignment succeeds and a confirmation appears.

Verify: You see a success notification.

Troubleshooting: If assignment fails with a permissions error, confirm you're signed in as the account owner from Day 1.

Step 6. On the Access control (IAM) page, click the “Role assignments” tab. Find the Reader row and confirm Lab-Readers appears. This is the audit view — who has what, at this scope.

Expected result: Reader is listed with Lab-Readers (and the Lab User through membership).

Verify: Reader → Lab-Readers is visible in the list.

Step 7. Click the “Check access” tab, search for “Lab User”, and open the result. Azure computes the user's effective access — because the group has Reader, the user does too.

Expected result: Check access shows Reader permissions for the Lab User.

Verify: Lab User shows Reader-level access on lab-03.

Troubleshooting: If results are empty, wait 2 minutes and retry — role assignments can take a moment to propagate.

Step 8. Open a private/incognito window, go to https://portal.azure.com, sign in as Lab User, and open Resource groups. Then sign out and close the window.

Expected result: In the incognito window, the Lab User CAN now open the lab-03 resource group and view it — but “+ Create” actions in Azure will fail with an authorization error if they try to add a resource.

Verify: The Lab User sees lab-03 as view-only.

Troubleshooting: If the group isn't visible yet, wait a few minutes — propagation delay is normal.

Step 9. Back as yourself, in your journal complete this sentence: “The Lab User could see lab-03 today because the ___ role was assigned to the ___ group at the ___ scope.”

Expected result: You can state what changed between Day 5 and now, in RBAC terms.

Verify: Your sentence says: Reader role, Lab-Readers group, lab-03 (resource group) scope.

Step 10. Remove the assignment: Access control (IAM) → Role assignments → select the Reader/Lab-Readers row → “Remove” → confirm “Yes”. Then re-run Check access for Lab User to prove it's gone. Least privilege includes taking access AWAY when the task is done.

Expected result: The assignment is removed and Check access shows no Reader access for the Lab User.

Verify: Check access no longer lists Reader for the Lab User on lab-03.

Troubleshooting: If Remove is greyed out, make sure you selected the assignment row's checkbox first.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Remove the role assignment (step 10 does this). Then, if you no longer need them, delete the Lab User and Lab-Readers group: Microsoft Entra ID → Users → Lab User → Delete; Microsoft Entra ID → Groups → Lab-Readers → Delete. Confirm the resource group itself still exists — RBAC changes never delete resources.

G. Practical Assignment

Suppose your team hires an intern who needs to view resources in ONE resource group for a summer project, and a senior engineer who manages (but does not own billing for) the whole subscription. Write down which role and which scope you would assign to each, and one sentence justifying each choice using the principle of least privilege.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. Azure RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) works by:
    • A) Encryption of data at rest
    • B) Granting permissions by assigning roles to users or groups at a scope
    • C) A firewall rule set
    • D) A backup policy
  2. Q2. You want a teammate to view your resource group but never create or delete anything. Which built-in role fits?
    • A) Owner
    • B) Contributor
    • C) Reader
    • D) User Access Administrator
  3. Q3. “Give each person only the permissions they need for their job — no more.” This is:
    • A) The principle of least privilege
    • B) Defense in depth
    • C) Zero trust
    • D) Shared responsibility
  4. Q4. A role assigned at the subscription scope applies to:
    • A) Nowhere — assignments don't inherit
    • B) Only that one resource group
    • C) Only that one resource
    • D) Every resource group and resource inside the subscription
  5. Q5. Practical: in the Access control (IAM) blade of your resource group, use “Check access” to confirm your Lab User's effective permissions, before and after removing the assignment. Did the results change as expected?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) grants permissions by assigning a role (what) to a security principal (who) at a scope (where) — permissions flow from that combination.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. Reader can view resources but cannot create, change, or delete anything — perfect for auditors, observers, and new team members.
  3. Q3 — Answer: A. The principle of least privilege: give only the permissions someone needs to do their job — no more — so mistakes and compromises cause less damage.
  4. Q4 — Answer: D. Role assignments inherit downward: subscription-level roles apply to all resource groups and resources inside it. The broadest scope gives the widest access.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Done — the Lab User appeared in the Check access results with Reader only, and removing the assignment removed them from the list.. Practical proof the learner can grant and revoke scoped access.

Day 7 — Week 1 Review: Consolidate Cloud and Azure Fundamentals60–90 minutes

A. Day 7 — Week 1 Review: Consolidate Cloud and Azure Fundamentals

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Summarize the core cloud and Azure concepts from Days 1–6 in your own words
  • Verify your free account, subscription, budgets, and resource inventory are healthy
  • Complete a guided consolidation exercise in the portal and Azure Cloud Shell
  • Score yourself on a Week 1 self-test and list exactly what to revisit

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Days 1–6 completed, with your daily journal notes handy
  • A browser signed in to the Azure portal

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Think of Week 1 as learning the parts of a workshop before you start building furniture. Days 1–6 gave you the tools: what cloud computing is, how Azure organizes everything, and how to click and type your way around without breaking anything expensive. Today you lay every tool out on the bench, check none are missing, and prove you can pick each one up on demand.

Here is the Week 1 story in one paragraph. Cloud computing means renting computers, storage, and networking over the internet instead of buying hardware. Microsoft Azure is one provider of those rented services. Your subscription is your billing account — the free trial subscription holds your $200 credit. Inside a subscription you create resource groups, which are folders that hold related resources (a virtual machine, a storage account, and so on). Every resource lives in a region, a physical Azure datacenter location such as East US. You manage all of this through the Azure portal (the website), the Azure CLI (typed commands), or Cloud Shell (a free CLI that runs right in your browser with no install). Cost Management + Billing with a budget keeps spending visible — and remember, a budget is an alert, not a spending cap: it emails you when you approach a limit, it does not stop the meter.

Why a revision day matters: cloud skills are built like muscle memory. Reading about a resource group is not the same as creating and deleting one twice. Today’s exercise forces you to touch every Week 1 skill once more, so Week 2 (virtual machines, networks, storage, web apps) starts on solid ground instead of shaky memory.

  • Subscription — your billing container; one free-trial subscription per account.
  • Resource group — a folder for related resources; deleting the folder deletes everything inside, which makes cleanup easy.
  • Region — where your resources physically run; pick one close to you and stick with it for these labs.
  • Cloud Shell — a free, browser-based terminal with the Azure CLI preinstalled.
  • Budget — a Cost Management alert on spending; it warns, it never blocks.

Portal screens, free-tier limits, and prices change over time — if a button moved, use portal search (the top bar) to find it by name. That search habit is itself a Week 1 skill worth keeping.

E. Learning Resources

Microsoft Learn — Azure training hubMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/azure/ VerifiedBrowse free learning paths to reinforce any Week 1 topic you feel shaky on20 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Prove your Week 1 setup is solid: inventory your resources, check your spending, and run basic Cloud Shell commands.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; browser access to the Azure portal.

Cost note: Free. This lab only reads information and opens the free Cloud Shell — it creates nothing billable.

Step 1. Sign in at the Azure portal and confirm which account you are using: click your profile picture (top right) and check the email matches your free-account email.

Expected result: You see your own email address and the portal home page loads.

Verify: The profile menu shows the same email you used to create the free account.

Troubleshooting: If you land in the wrong tenant or directory, use the profile menu → Switch directory to pick the right one.

Step 2. In the top search bar type 'Subscriptions' and open it. Click your subscription (usually named 'Free Trial' or similar) and note the status and any credit balance shown.

Expected result: A subscription overview page showing status Active and your remaining credit or spending.

Verify: Status reads Active; you can see a spending/credit figure.

Troubleshooting: If no subscription appears, you may be signed in with the wrong account — sign out and back in with your free-account email.

Step 3. Search 'Resource groups' and open the list. Write down every resource group name you see — these are leftovers from Days 1–6 practice.

Expected result: A list of resource groups, possibly empty or with a few practice groups.

Verify: You have a written list (paper or notes app) of all group names.

Step 4. Click into one practice resource group from Days 1–6 and open its 'Resources' list. Confirm you can explain what each resource in it is and why you created it.

Expected result: You can say in one sentence per resource what it does.

Verify: No resource in the list is a mystery to you — if one is, that topic goes on your revisit list.

Troubleshooting: If a resource type is unfamiliar, search its name in the Microsoft Learn Azure training hub later.

Step 5. Search 'Cost Management' → open 'Budgets' under your subscription. Confirm the budget alert you set earlier still exists and note its threshold amount and alert percentage.

Expected result: Your budget appears with its amount and alert rules (for example, alert at 80% of $200).

Verify: The budget row is listed and its details open when clicked.

Troubleshooting: If the budget is missing, recreate one now: Budgets → + Add → set amount 200 USD and an 80% alert to your email. Remember: this is an alert, not a spending cap.

Step 6. Click the Cloud Shell icon (&gt;_) in the top toolbar. On first launch choose 'Bash' and create a storage account when prompted (Cloud Shell needs a tiny storage account to keep your files).

Expected result: A terminal opens at the bottom of the portal and connects after a short wait.

Verify: You see a prompt like user@Azure:~$ and can type commands.

Troubleshooting: If Cloud Shell times out, close it, wait a minute, and reopen it. The small storage account it creates costs a few cents per month at most.

Step 7. In Cloud Shell, type: az account show --output table. The 'az' command is the Azure CLI; 'account show' asks 'which subscription am I using?'; '--output table' formats the answer as a readable table.

Expected result: A table showing your subscription name, id, and state.

Verify: The 'name' column matches your Free Trial subscription and 'state' is Enabled.

Troubleshooting: If the command is not found, you are not in Cloud Shell — make sure the terminal says Azure and not your local machine.

Step 8. Type: az group list --output table. This lists every resource group in your subscription, matching what you saw in the portal in step 3.

Expected result: A table of resource groups identical to your written list.

Verify: Portal list and CLI list agree — this proves you can inventory resources two ways.

Step 9. Type: az account list-locations --query "[?name=='eastus'].{Name:name, Display:displayName}" --output table. This asks Azure for the display name of the East US region. Regions are the physical locations from Day 2 — this is how you query them by code.

Expected result: A one-row table showing eastus and 'East US'.

Verify: The Display column reads 'East US'.

Troubleshooting: Mind the quotes: the query uses single quotes inside double quotes. Retype carefully if you get a parsing error.

Step 10. Open the Azure pricing calculator in a new tab and estimate one Standard_B1s virtual machine (the size you will use on Day 8) running full-time for a month in East US. Write the number down.

Expected result: A monthly estimate appears (the exact figure changes over time — what matters is that you can produce one).

Verify: You have a written estimate and you know which inputs produced it.

Troubleshooting: If the B1s size is hard to find, search the calculator for 'Virtual Machines' first, then filter by B-series.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Nothing billable was created by this lab. Optional tidy-up: delete any empty leftover practice resource groups from Days 1–6 you no longer need — Resource groups → click the group → Delete resource group → type the name to confirm. Leave the Cloud Shell storage account alone; deleting it just forces Cloud Shell to recreate it next time.

On paper or in a doc, write a one-page Week 1 summary in your own words: define subscription, resource group, region, resource, portal, CLI, Cloud Shell, and budget, and draw a simple diagram showing how they relate (for example: account → subscription → resource groups → resources, each pinned to a region). No solution is provided — this page becomes your personal cheat sheet for Week 2.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is the correct containment order in Azure?
    • A) Resource → resource group → region → subscription
    • B) Subscription → resource group → resource, with each resource in a region
    • C) Region → subscription → resource → resource group
    • D) Resource group → subscription → region → resource
  2. Q2. You set a $200 budget with an 80% alert in Cost Management. Spending reaches $170. What happens?
    • A) Azure stops all your resources at $160
    • B) You get an alert email; resources keep running
    • C) The subscription is suspended until you approve
    • D) Nothing — budgets only report once a month
  3. Q3. Which statement about Azure Cloud Shell is true?
    • A) It must be installed on your laptop before use
    • B) It runs in the browser with the Azure CLI preinstalled and is free to use
    • C) It only works with Windows containers
    • D) It deletes your resources when the session ends
  4. Q4. Why does every lab in this workshop end with cleanup steps that delete the resource group?
    • A) Deleted resource groups earn free credit
    • B) It keeps the portal tidy and stops any metered resources from charging after the lab
    • C) Azure requires deletion within 24 hours by policy
    • D) It is only needed for virtual machines
  5. Q5. Practical: open Cost Management in the portal and write down (1) your current month-to-date spend, (2) your forecasted spend, and (3) whether your budget alert is configured. What does a healthy Week 1 result look like?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. A subscription holds resource groups, resource groups hold resources, and every resource is deployed into a region. Deleting a resource group deletes everything inside it.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Budgets are alerts, not spending caps. At 80% of $200 ($160) you are notified, but the meter keeps running — which is why cleanup steps in every lab matter.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Cloud Shell is a browser-based terminal in the portal with Azure CLI preinstalled. It keeps a small storage account for your files, which persists between sessions.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Deleting the resource group removes every resource inside it in one action, so nothing keeps consuming credit or free-tier hours after you finish learning.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Month-to-date spend near $0 (or well under the $200 credit), forecast comfortably below the credit limit, and a budget with an 80%-of-limit alert pointing at your email. If spend is unexpectedly high, list which resource groups contain running resources and delete what you no longer need.. This checks that you can actually navigate Cost Management and interpret the numbers — the real Week 1 skill — rather than just reciting definitions.

Weekly Review — Week 1 Review - Cloud and Azure Fundamentals

You have spent a week on cloud concepts, the Azure portal, identity, and access control. Answer these ten questions without looking at your notes, then check the answers and explanations.

  1. Q1. Which statement best describes cloud computing?
    • A) Buying and running your own physical servers in an office closet
    • B) On-demand delivery of IT resources over the internet, where you pay only for what you use
    • C) A type of software you install on your laptop
    • D) A backup of your files on a USB drive
  2. Q2. A company rents virtual hardware from Azure and installs its own operating system and applications on it. Which cloud service model is this?
    • A) SaaS
    • B) PaaS
    • C) IaaS
    • D) DaaS
  3. Q3. Which of the following is a PaaS example?
    • A) Azure Virtual Machines
    • B) Azure App Service
    • C) Microsoft 365
    • D) An on-premises server
  4. Q4. What is an Azure region?
    • A) A billing plan for Azure
    • B) A geographic area containing one or more Azure datacenters
    • C) A type of virtual machine
    • D) A user account in Microsoft Entra ID
  5. Q5. What is the main purpose of a resource group?
    • A) To group related resources so they can be managed, deployed, and deleted together
    • B) To set the price of resources
    • C) To create user accounts
    • D) To connect resources to the internet
  6. Q6. Which built-in RBAC role can create and manage resources but CANNOT grant access to other people?
    • A) Owner
    • B) Contributor
    • C) Reader
    • D) User Access Administrator
  7. Q7. The principle of least privilege means:
    • A) Give every user the Owner role to avoid access errors
    • B) Give each identity only the minimum permissions it needs, and nothing more
    • C) Never assign any roles to anyone
    • D) Share one admin account with the whole team
  8. Q8. Microsoft Entra ID is primarily used to:
    • A) Store blob data
    • B) Manage identities, sign-in, and access to Azure resources
    • C) Monitor virtual machine CPU usage
    • D) Send billing alerts
  9. Q9. Multi-factor authentication (MFA) protects an account by:
    • A) Making the password longer
    • B) Requiring a second verification factor, such as a phone approval, in addition to the password
    • C) Blocking all sign-ins from new devices
    • D) Encrypting the hard drive
  10. Q10. Which scope is the WIDEST when assigning an Azure RBAC role?
    • A) Resource
    • B) Resource group
    • C) Subscription
    • D) Management group
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Cloud computing means renting IT resources (compute, storage, networking) over the internet on a pay-as-you-go basis instead of buying and maintaining hardware.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service) gives you virtualized hardware; you manage the operating system, patches, and applications yourself.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Azure App Service is PaaS: you deploy code and Azure manages the servers, OS patching, and load balancing. Azure Virtual Machines is IaaS; Microsoft 365 is SaaS.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. A region is a set of datacenters in a geographic area (for example, East US). You pick a region when you create resources.
  5. Q5 — Answer: A. A resource group is a logical container. Resources inside it share a lifecycle: deploy them together, manage them together, and delete the group to remove everything.
  6. Q6 — Answer: B. Contributor can create and manage all resource types but cannot assign roles. Owner can do everything, including granting access.
  7. Q7 — Answer: B. Least privilege reduces risk: if an account is compromised, the damage is limited to what that account was allowed to do.
  8. Q8 — Answer: B. Microsoft Entra ID (formerly Azure AD) is the identity service: users, groups, authentication, and single sign-on for Azure and Microsoft 365.
  9. Q9 — Answer: B. MFA combines something you know (password) with something you have (phone/app) or are (fingerprint), so a stolen password alone is not enough.
  10. Q10 — Answer: D. Management groups sit above subscriptions. A role assigned at management-group scope flows down to every subscription, resource group, and resource beneath it.
Day 8 — Azure Virtual Machines and Operating System Basics60–90 minutes

A. Day 8 — Azure Virtual Machines and Operating System Basics

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what a virtual machine and a hypervisor are, using a plain-words analogy
  • Identify the parts of a VM: image, size (SKU), OS disk, data disk, and network interface
  • Create a Linux VM in the portal and choose an appropriate free-tier size
  • Connect to the VM over SSH and run basic operating-system commands
  • Stop (deallocate) a VM and explain what that does to billing

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account with the Day 7 health check done
  • Browser signed in to the Azure portal
  • Comfort opening Cloud Shell (Day 7)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Imagine a large apartment building. The building itself is a powerful physical computer in a Microsoft datacenter. Each apartment is a virtual machine (VM) — a complete computer (CPU, memory, disk, network card) created in software, with its own front door, its own furniture, and no knowledge of the neighbors. The building manager who divides the building’s electricity, water, and space between apartments is called the hypervisor — the software layer that carves one physical server into many isolated virtual computers.

To rent your apartment you make three choices. The image is the move-in template: which operating system (OS) — the software that manages the computer, like Ubuntu Linux or Windows Server — plus any preinstalled software. The size (also called SKU, pronounced “skew”) is the apartment floor plan: how many virtual CPUs and how much RAM you get — for example, Standard_B1s means 1 virtual CPU and 1 GB of RAM, a small, cheap, “burstable” size that is ideal for learning. The disk is your storage: the OS disk holds the operating system, and you can add data disks for your files, in flavors from slower Standard HDD to fast Premium SSD.

Two more ideas complete the picture. Every VM gets a virtual network interface (NIC) — its network plug — which is how it joins the virtual network you will build on Day 9. And you reach into the machine remotely: SSH (Secure Shell) is the standard way to type commands into a Linux VM, while RDP (Remote Desktop Protocol) gives you a graphical screen on a Windows VM. This lab uses Linux and SSH because it is cheaper (no Windows license cost) and SSH from Cloud Shell needs no extra software.

  • VM — a software-defined computer rented by the hour.
  • Hypervisor — the layer that splits physical servers into VMs.
  • Image — the OS + software template a VM is built from.
  • Size / SKU — the VM’s CPU and RAM specification (B1s = 1 vCPU, 1 GB RAM).
  • OS disk / data disk — storage for the operating system vs. your files.
  • SSH — encrypted command-line access to Linux; RDP — graphical access to Windows.

A cost warning before you start: the compute meter runs only while the VM is allocated (running or merely shut down from inside the OS). Choosing Stop (deallocate) releases the hardware and stops that meter; the small disk-storage meter keeps running until you delete the resource group. Budgets are alerts, not spending caps — the alert can also arrive late — so the cleanup step at the end of this lab is your real protection. Portal screens and free-tier limits change over time; if a label moved, use portal search to find it.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
