Azure virtual machines documentation overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/overview Needs verificationOfficial reference on VM concepts, sizes, images, and disks — read after the lab to go deeper20 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a small Linux virtual machine in the portal, connect to it over SSH, run basic operating-system commands, and then delete everything.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; a browser signed in to the portal. No software to install — you will connect from Cloud Shell.

Cost note: The Standard_B1s size is covered by the free account’s included VM hours (750 B1s hours per month for the first 12 months; limits change over time). If you exceed that, the $200 free credit absorbs it. Budgets are alerts, not caps, and alerts can be delayed — always run the cleanup step. Estimated lab runtime: 20–30 minutes of VM time if you clean up promptly.

Step 1. Portal → top search bar → type 'Virtual machines' → open it → click '+ Create' → 'Azure virtual machine'.

Expected result: The 'Create a virtual machine' form opens.

Verify: The Basics tab of the VM creation form is displayed.

Step 2. On the Basics tab: Subscription = your free-trial subscription. Resource group → 'Create new' → name it rg-day08 → OK. Virtual machine name → vm-day08.

Expected result: Subscription is your free trial; the new resource group name is accepted.

Verify: The resource group dropdown shows rg-day08 (new).

Troubleshooting: Resource group names must be unique within your subscription — if rg-day08 is taken from an earlier attempt, use rg-day08b.

Step 3. Region → choose the region closest to you (for example, East US). You will use this same region for the rest of Week 2 so your resources sit near each other.

Expected result: The region dropdown shows a list of Azure regions.

Verify: A region is selected and shown next to the VM name.

Troubleshooting: If VM creation later fails with a quota/capacity error, repeat this lab in a different large region such as East US or West US 2.

Step 4. Image → select 'Ubuntu Server 22.04 LTS - x64 Gen2' (or the closest Ubuntu LTS offered). Then click 'See all sizes' → filter for 'B1s' → select Standard_B1s → Select.

Expected result: The image shows an Ubuntu version; the size link shows available sizes.

Verify: Image reads Ubuntu and Size reads Standard_B1s.

Troubleshooting: If B1s does not appear, clear the size filter or pick B1ms — any small B-series size works for this lab.

Step 5. Authentication type → Password. Username → azureuser. Password → invent a strong password (12+ characters, mixed case, numbers, symbols) and save it in a password manager — you will need it once, in step 9.

Expected result: The form accepts the username and password without errors.

Verify: No red validation errors under the password fields.

Troubleshooting: The portal rejects short or simple passwords. Longer and more mixed always passes.

Step 6. Public inbound ports → 'Allow selected ports'. Select inbound ports → check 'SSH (22)'. This opens the door for your step-9 connection; everything else stays closed.

Expected result: 'SSH (22)' is selected under inbound ports.

Verify: The inbound-ports dropdown reads 'SSH (22)'.

Step 7. Click through the 'Disks' and 'Networking' tabs without changing anything — accept the defaults (they create a managed OS disk, a new virtual network, and a public IP for you). Then go to 'Review + create'.

Expected result: Defaults are shown for disk type, virtual network, subnet, and public IP.

Verify: Validation passes with a green check: 'Validation passed'.

Step 8. Click 'Create'. Wait for the deployment to finish — do not close the tab. When it completes, click 'Go to resource'.

Expected result: A 'Deployment is in progress' notification, then 'Your deployment is complete' after 2–5 minutes.

Verify: The notification says deployment succeeded and the VM Overview page opens.

Troubleshooting: If deployment fails with a quota error ('Operation could not be completed as it results in exceeding quota'), delete the failed VM and retry in another region.

Step 9. On the VM Overview page, note the 'Public IP address' and confirm 'Status' is Running. Copy the public IP address.

Expected result: The Overview page shows Status: Running and a Public IP address like 20.x.x.x.

Verify: You have a public IP written down and Status = Running.

Troubleshooting: If the public IP is blank, wait a minute and refresh — it is assigned shortly after boot.

Step 10. Open Cloud Shell (Bash) from the top toolbar and type: ssh azureuser@<paste-your-public-IP>. Type 'yes' if asked about the host key, then enter your password when prompted.

Expected result: A Linux welcome banner, then a prompt like azureuser@vm-day08:~$.

Verify: You see the Ubuntu welcome text and a command prompt ending in $.

Troubleshooting: 'Connection timed out' usually means the VM is still booting (wait 2 minutes) or the SSH inbound rule is missing — check the VM’s Networking settings for port 22. 'Permission denied' means a wrong password — retype it carefully.

Step 11. Run these four commands one by one, reading each output: uname -a (OS and kernel version), df -h (disk space), uptime (how long the VM has been running), then echo 'Hello from Day 8' > hello.txt && ls -l (creates a file and lists it). Each command is typed after the $ prompt; press Enter after each.

Expected result: System info, disk usage, and uptime print out; the file listing shows hello.txt.

Verify: hello.txt appears in the ls -l output.

Troubleshooting: 'command not found' means a typo — Linux commands are case-sensitive and exact. Retype slowly.

Step 12. Type exit to close the SSH session. You are now back in Cloud Shell, no longer inside the VM.

Expected result: Your SSH session closes and you are back at the Cloud Shell prompt.

Verify: The prompt no longer shows azureuser@vm-day08.

Step 13. Back in the portal on the VM Overview page, click 'Stop' and check the box if it asks about deallocation (choose Stop/Deallocate, not just shutdown). This releases the hardware and stops the compute meter while keeping the VM for now.

Expected result: Status changes to 'Stopped (deallocated)' after a minute.

Verify: Status reads 'Stopped (deallocated)'.

Troubleshooting: If the status stays 'Running', wait a minute and refresh the page.

Cleanup (prevent charges): This is the most important step of the day. Portal → search 'Resource groups' → click rg-day08 → 'Delete resource group' → type rg-day08 → Delete. Wait until the notification says the deletion succeeded, then confirm rg-day08 no longer appears in the list. A deleted resource group takes the VM, disks, network, and public IP with it, so no meter keeps running.

G. Practical Assignment

Create (then delete) one more Linux VM on your own, but change two things from the lab: pick a different region and a different username. Write down the public IP, the region, and the SSH command you used. Then delete its resource group. No solution is provided — the goal is to prove you can repeat the process without the steps in front of you.

Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. A VM is software pretending to be a computer, sharing one physical server with other VMs. The hypervisor divides the physical CPU, memory, and disk between them.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. The image is the template (OS + software), the size is the hardware spec (vCPU/RAM), and the disk type sets storage speed. The region just decides where it runs.

Success looks like a welcome banner and a prompt such as azureuser@vm-day08:~$. If it times out, check the VM is Running and the inbound SSH (22) rule allows your IP, then retry.. This proves you can actually reach the VM over the network — the skill every later day assumes — not just create one.

Day 9 — Azure Virtual Network, Subnets, IP Addresses, and Network Security Groups60–90 minutes

A. Day 9 — Azure Virtual Network, Subnets, IP Addresses, and Network Security Groups

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain virtual networks, subnets, CIDR notation, and IP addressing with a plain-words analogy
  • Describe the difference between private and public IP addresses
  • Create a VNet with two subnets in the portal
  • Create a network security group, add a custom inbound rule, and attach it to a subnet
  • Read NSG rules in priority order and explain least-privilege networking

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account; Days 7–8 completed
  • Browser signed in to the Azure portal
  • Your Day 8 region noted (reuse it today)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

On Day 8 your VM got a network automatically, like a hotel assigning you a room without asking. Today you become the architect: you will design the building yourself. An Azure Virtual Network (VNet) is your private, isolated network space in Azure — think of it as renting an entire office building where only your company has keys. Nothing in your VNet can be reached from the internet unless you explicitly open a door.

Floors of the building are subnets: smaller segments of the VNet for different purposes, such as one subnet for web servers and another for databases. Each floor and building is sized with CIDR notation — an address block written like 10.0.0.0/16. The number after the slash sets the size: /16 is a big building (65,536 addresses), /24 is one floor (256 addresses). Addresses come in two kinds: private IP addresses (ranges like 10.x.x.x) are room numbers that only work inside your building, while public IP addresses are the building’s street address — globally unique and reachable from the internet.

Every VM connects through a virtual network interface (NIC) — its network plug — which sits in one subnet. Guarding each floor is a network security group (NSG): a list of allow/deny rules checked top-down by priority number (lower numbers are evaluated first). A rule says: allow or deny traffic, from some source, to some destination, on some port — for example, “allow inbound TCP port 22 from my home IP.” Azure also ships default rules that allow all traffic inside the VNet and deny everything inbound from the internet, so a fresh VNet is closed by default and you open only what you need — that is least privilege in networking form.

  • VNet — your isolated private network in Azure.
  • Subnet — a segment of a VNet; CIDR (e.g., 10.0.1.0/24) defines its address block.
  • Private IP — internal-only address; public IP — internet-reachable address.
  • NIC — the virtual network card attaching a VM to a subnet.
  • NSG — ordered allow/deny rules (lowest priority number first) guarding a subnet or NIC.

Cost note: VNets, subnets, and NSGs themselves cost nothing — you pay only for things like public IPs and data transfer, and this lab’s public IP lives only for the duration of the lab. Portal labels and free-tier limits change over time; use portal search if a button moved.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Microsoft Learn — Azure training hubMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/azure/ VerifiedFind the free networking modules for a second explanation of VNets and subnets20 min
Azure Virtual Network documentation overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-network/overview Needs verificationOfficial reference on VNets, subnets, IP addressing, and peering — read after the lab20 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Build a virtual network with two subnets, create a network security group with a custom rule, attach it to a subnet, and inspect the effective rules.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; portal access. No VM is created today — you work purely with networking objects.

Cost note: Free for this lab’s duration. VNets, subnets, and NSGs have no hourly charge; the public IP created here accrues a tiny charge only while it exists, covered by free credit. Delete the resource group when done.

Step 1. Portal → top search bar → type 'Virtual networks' → open it → click '+ Create'.

Expected result: The 'Create virtual network' form opens.

Verify: The Basics tab of the virtual network form is displayed.

Step 2. Basics tab: Subscription = your free-trial subscription. Resource group → 'Create new' → rg-day09 → OK. Name → vnet-day09. Region → the same region you used on Day 8 (for example, East US).

Expected result: The new resource group is selected.

Verify: Name reads vnet-day09 and the region matches your Day 8 region.

Step 3. Go to the 'IP Addresses' tab. Notice the IPv4 address space 10.0.0.0/16 — this is the whole 'building' (65,536 addresses) written in CIDR notation. Leave it as is.

Expected result: The IP Addresses tab shows the default address space 10.0.0.0/16.

Verify: Address space shows 10.0.0.0/16.

Step 4. Delete the default subnet (select it → Delete), then click '+ Add a subnet'. Name it snet-web, set its address range to 10.0.1.0/24 (one 'floor' of 256 addresses), and add it.

Expected result: The default subnet is listed; you can edit or delete it.

Verify: Subnets list shows snet-web with 10.0.1.0/24.

Troubleshooting: If the portal says the range overlaps, you typed it wrong — it must start with 10.0.1 and end with /24.

Step 5. Add a second subnet: name snet-db, address range 10.0.2.0/24. Then click 'Review + create' → 'Create' and wait for deployment to finish.

Expected result: Two subnets are listed with their CIDR blocks.

Verify: Deployment succeeds; vnet-day09 shows two subnets.

Step 6. Top search bar → type 'Network security groups' → open → '+ Create'. Resource group → rg-day09. Name → nsg-day09. Region → same region as the VNet. Click 'Review + create' → 'Create'.

Expected result: The 'Create network security group' form opens.

Verify: nsg-day09 appears in the network security groups list.

Troubleshooting: The region must match the VNet’s region — an NSG in another region cannot attach to this VNet’s subnets.

Step 7. Open nsg-day09 → 'Inbound security rules' (under Settings) → '+ Add'. Fill in: Source → 'IP Addresses', Source IP addresses → type 0.0.0.0/0 for this lab (meaning 'from anywhere'; in real life you would put your own IP), Destination port ranges → 443, Protocol → TCP, Action → Allow, Priority → 120, Name → allow-https. Click Add.

Expected result: The 'Add inbound security rule' form opens.

Verify: The inbound rules list now shows allow-https with priority 120 above the default deny rule.

Troubleshooting: Priority must be unique — if 120 is taken by a default rule, use 130. Port 443 is HTTPS, the standard web port.

Step 8. Still in nsg-day09, go to 'Subnets' (under Settings) → '+ Associate' → Virtual network → vnet-day09 → Subnet → snet-web → OK. This posts your 'security guard' at the door of the web floor.

Expected result: A rule form where you can pick the subnet.

Verify: The Subnets tab lists snet-web as associated.

Troubleshooting: If vnet-day09 is not listed, the regions do not match — both must be in the same region.

Step 9. Go to 'Inbound security rules' again and read the list top to bottom: rules are evaluated by priority number, lowest first. Confirm allow-https (120) sits above the default 'DenyAllInBound' rule (priority 65500). Say out loud what happens to inbound web traffic on port 443 — it is allowed — and to inbound traffic on any other port — it hits the deny rule.

Expected result: A table of rules in evaluation order, including your allow-https rule and the defaults.

Verify: You can explain, in your own words, which traffic is allowed and which is denied, and why order matters.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Portal → search 'Resource groups' → click rg-day09 → 'Delete resource group' → type rg-day09 → Delete. Wait for the success notification and confirm rg-day09 is gone. This removes the VNet, both subnets, the NSG, and the public IP together.

G. Practical Assignment

In the portal, open the Day 8 VM’s virtual network (before you deleted it — if you already deleted it, just do this on paper): find its address space, its subnets, and the NSG on the VM. Write down which inbound ports are open and decide whether you would keep them open for a production web server. No solution is provided.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. Which statement correctly describes a virtual network, subnets, and CIDR notation?
    • A) A VNet replaces the need for virtual machines
    • B) A VNet is a physical cable between datacenters
    • C) A VNet is your private network; subnets segment it; CIDR notation defines the size of each address block
    • D) Subnets are public and VNets are private
  2. Q2. What is the difference between a private IP address and a public IP address?
    • A) Private IPs work over the internet and public IPs do not
    • B) Private IPs are for internal traffic; public IPs are globally reachable and needed only where the internet must reach in
    • C) Every resource must have a public IP
    • D) Public IPs are free and unlimited
  3. Q3. An NSG has an allow-SSH rule at priority 100 and a deny-all-inbound rule at priority 200. SSH traffic arrives. What happens, and why?
    • A) The deny rule wins because deny always beats allow
    • B) The allow rule wins for SSH because lower priority numbers are evaluated first
    • C) Both rules apply and the connection is randomly allowed or blocked
    • D) The last-created rule wins
  4. Q4. Put these in the right relationship: VM, network interface (NIC), subnet, virtual network (VNet), network security group (NSG).
    • A) VM → network interface → subnet → virtual network, with the NSG filtering traffic at the subnet or NIC
    • B) NSG → VM → VNet → internet, with the subnet unused
    • C) VNet → public IP → NSG → VM, with no subnet involved
    • D) Subnet → NSG → image → VM
  5. Q5. Practical: design (on paper) a VNet for a simple app with web, application, and database tiers. Give the VNet CIDR, one subnet CIDR per tier, and state in one line per subnet what its NSG should allow. What principle does this design follow?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: C. A VNet is your private network space; subnets divide it into segments (for example, web servers vs. databases). CIDR notation like 10.0.0.0/16 simply describes how big the address block is — 10.0.0.0/16 holds 65,536 addresses, 10.0.1.0/24 holds 256.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Private IPs (10.x, 172.16.x, 192.168.x) work only inside your network. A public IP is a globally unique address that lets the internet reach a resource — that is why only the VM that needs inbound access gets one.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. NSG rules are evaluated by priority number, lowest first: rule 100 is checked before rule 200. So the allow rule (100) wins for SSH traffic and the deny rule (200) catches everything else.
  4. Q4 — Answer: A. The NIC plugs the VM into the subnet; the subnet lives inside the VNet; the NSG attached to the subnet filters the traffic. It is one chain: VM → NIC → subnet → VNet, with the NSG guarding the door.
  5. Q5 — Answer: The web subnet’s NSG should allow inbound 443 (HTTPS) from the internet and the app subnet needs no internet inbound; the database subnet should allow its database port only from the app subnet’s address range (10.0.2.0/24), denying everything else. Sketch: VNet 10.1.0.0/16 → web 10.1.1.0/24 (NSG: allow 443 in), app 10.1.2.0/24 (NSG: allow from web subnet), db 10.1.3.0/24 (NSG: allow DB port only from 10.1.2.0/24). The principle is least privilege — each tier only talks to the tier next to it.. This is the classic three-tier design. The key insight is that subnets plus NSGs let you isolate layers so a compromised web server still cannot reach the database directly.

Day 10 — DNS, Connectivity, and Basic Network Troubleshooting60–90 minutes

A. Day 10 — DNS, Connectivity, and Basic Network Troubleshooting

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain DNS, FQDN, A records, and CNAME records with a plain-words analogy
  • Describe the difference between public DNS zones and Azure Private DNS zones
  • Create a Private DNS zone, link it to a VNet, and add an A record
  • Use nslookup from Cloud Shell to resolve public names and observe private-zone scoping
  • Apply a five-step ladder for basic network connectivity troubleshooting

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account; Days 7–9 completed
  • Browser signed in to the Azure portal; Cloud Shell (Bash)
  • Your usual region noted (reuse it today)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

The internet runs on numbers, but humans remember names. DNS (Domain Name System) is the phone book that translates a name like www.example.com into an IP address like 93.184.216.34. A full name such as web1.lab.internal is called an FQDN (fully qualified domain name). Inside the phone book, records are the entries: an A record maps a name to an IPv4 address (web1 → 10.0.1.4), and a CNAME record maps a name to another name (www → web1, an alias).

Azure offers two kinds of phone books. A public DNS zone answers queries from the whole internet — this is what serves real websites, and it requires a domain you own. An Azure Private DNS zone answers queries only from inside the virtual networks (VNets) you link it to — perfect for lab.internal names that should never leak to the internet. That scoping is the day’s key insight: the same name can resolve for a VM inside your VNet and be invisible everywhere else.

When connectivity breaks, beginners usually guess. Professionals check layers in order — a habit that fixes most problems in minutes. Memorize this ladder: (1) Is the VM running? (2) Am I using the right IP address or DNS name? (3) Does the NSG allow this port from my location? (4) Does the OS firewall inside the VM allow it? (5) Is the application actually listening on that port? Two Azure quirks to know: ICMP ping to Azure public IPs is blocked by design, so a failed ping means nothing — test the real TCP port instead. And in the portal, effective security rules show you the final allow/deny decision for any VM after all NSGs are combined.

  • DNS — the name-to-IP phone book; FQDN — a complete domain name.
  • A record — name → IPv4 address; CNAME — name → another name.
  • Private DNS zone — resolves only inside linked VNets; public DNS zone — serves the internet.
  • ICMP/ping — blocked to Azure public IPs; test TCP ports instead.

Today’s lab creates a small private DNS zone and a VNet purely to practice records and linking — no VM, so nothing to connect to and no compute charges. Portal screens and pricing change over time; use portal search if a label moved.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
What is Azure Cloud ShellDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-shell/overview VerifiedRe-read how Cloud Shell networking works — it explains why Cloud Shell cannot resolve your private zone10 min
Azure DNS documentation overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/dns/dns-overview Needs verificationOfficial reference on DNS zones, record types, and private DNS — read after the lab20 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a Private DNS zone, link it to a virtual network, add an A record, and practice public DNS lookups and the connectivity troubleshooting ladder from Cloud Shell.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; portal access; Cloud Shell (Bash).

Cost note: Negligible. A private DNS zone and VNet cost a few cents per month at most while they exist, covered by free credit. No VM is created, so there is no compute charge. Delete the resource group when done.

Step 1. Portal → top search bar → type 'Private DNS zones' → open it → click '+ Create'.

Expected result: The 'Create private DNS zone' form opens.

Verify: The Basics tab of the private DNS zone form is displayed.

Step 2. Basics tab: Subscription = your free-trial subscription. Resource group → 'Create new' → rg-day10 → OK. Name → lab.internal. (This is a private name — it does not need to be a domain you own.)

Expected result: The new resource group is selected and the zone name is accepted.

Verify: Name reads lab.internal with no validation errors.

Troubleshooting: Zone names must be globally unique among private zones in your subscription only — if lab.internal is taken, use lab10.internal.

Step 3. Region: private DNS zones are global — accept the default location. Click 'Review + create' → 'Create', then 'Go to resource'.

Expected result: Deployment succeeds quickly.

Verify: The lab.internal overview page opens.

Step 4. Top search bar → 'Virtual networks' → '+ Create'. Resource group → rg-day10. Name → vnet-day10. Region → your usual region (for example, East US). On the IP Addresses tab, accept the defaults. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The 'Create virtual network' form opens.

Verify: vnet-day10 is deployed in rg-day10.

Step 5. Back in the lab.internal DNS zone, go to 'Virtual network links' (under Settings) → '+ Add'. Link name → link-day10. Virtual network → vnet-day10. Leave 'Enable auto registration' unchecked. Click Create.

Expected result: The 'Add virtual network link' form opens with vnet-day10 selectable.

Verify: The links list shows link-day10 with status Completed.

Troubleshooting: If vnet-day10 is not listed, the link form may need a refresh — close and reopen it.

Step 6. In the DNS zone, go to 'Record sets' (under Settings) → '+ Add'. Name → web1. Type → A. IP address → 10.0.1.4. TTL → accept the default (TTL = how long resolvers cache the answer). Click OK.

Expected result: The 'Add record set' form opens.

Verify: The record-sets list shows web1, type A, value 10.0.1.4.

Step 7. Open Cloud Shell (Bash) and type: nslookup microsoft.com. Read the output: the 'Address' lines are the public A records — the phone book answering from the internet’s public DNS.

Expected result: An answer section showing an IP address for microsoft.com.

Verify: You see one or more IP addresses in the answer.

Troubleshooting: If nslookup is missing, use: dig microsoft.com +short — both query DNS.

Step 8. Type: nslookup www.microsoft.com. Compare with the previous answer — large sites often return a CNAME (an alias) first, then the A records it points to. This is the A-vs-CNAME difference made visible.

Expected result: A CNAME chain or A records pointing at a content-delivery network.

Verify: You can point at which line is the alias and which lines are the addresses.

Step 9. Type: nslookup web1.lab.internal. It will fail. That failure is the lesson: Cloud Shell is NOT inside vnet-day10, and private zones only answer queries from linked VNets. A VM inside vnet-day10 would resolve it to 10.0.1.4.

Expected result: nslookup fails or times out for web1.lab.internal — this is the expected, correct result.

Verify: You can explain why the public lookup worked and the private one did not.

Troubleshooting: If it somehow resolves, double-check you typed the private name — public DNS must never know lab.internal.

Step 10. Say the troubleshooting ladder out loud once: (1) VM running? Check the VM Overview status. (2) Right IP or name? Compare with the Overview page. (3) NSG allows the port? Check effective security rules. (4) OS firewall allows it? (5) App listening on the port? Write any you keep forgetting into your journal.

Expected result: You can recite all five checks without looking.

Verify: All five checks are written in your journal in order.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Portal → search 'Resource groups' → click rg-day10 → 'Delete resource group' → type rg-day10 → Delete. Wait for the success notification and confirm rg-day10 is gone. This removes the VNet and the private DNS zone together.

G. Practical Assignment

Write a one-page 'network down' runbook for yourself: list the five troubleshooting checks from today in order, and under each write the exact portal page or command you would use. Keep it somewhere you will find it on Day 12 when you deploy a web app. No solution is provided.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is the difference between an A record and a CNAME record?
    • A) A records store passwords; CNAME records store usernames
    • B) A records are for email; CNAME records are for websites
    • C) An A record maps a name to an IPv4 address; a CNAME maps a name to another name
    • D) There is no difference
  2. Q2. When should you use an Azure Private DNS zone instead of a public DNS zone?
    • A) Private zones are free and public zones are expensive
    • B) Private zones resolve names only inside linked virtual networks; public zones serve names to the whole internet
    • C) Private zones cannot hold A records
    • D) Public zones do not support subdomains
  3. Q3. You cannot ping your VM’s public IP. What is the correct next troubleshooting step?
    • A) Ping is blocked to Azure public IPs, so test the real TCP port instead (e.g., curl or Test-NetConnection)
    • B) Ping harder by adding the -t flag
    • C) Disable the NSG to let ping through
    • D) Restart the VM three times
  4. Q4. What is the right order for troubleshooting 'I cannot reach my VM on port X'?
    • A) Delete everything and start over
    • B) Check in order: VM running → right IP → NSG allows the port → OS firewall → application listening
    • C) Open all ports in the NSG immediately
    • D) Email support before checking anything
  5. Q5. Practical: after the lab, how would you verify the A record web1.lab.internal exists, and why does querying it from Cloud Shell fail while querying it from a VM in the VNet would succeed?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: C. DNS translates names to IPs. An A record maps a name directly to an IPv4 address; a CNAME maps a name to another name (an alias). So web1 → 10.0.1.4 is an A record, and www → web1 is a CNAME.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. A private DNS zone resolves names only inside the linked VNets — perfect for web1.lab.internal. Public DNS zones serve the whole internet and need a real registered domain.
  3. Q3 — Answer: A. Azure blocks ICMP ping to public IPs by design, so ping failing proves nothing. Test the actual TCP port with a tool like curl or Test-NetConnection instead — a refused or timed-out TCP connection tells you where the block really is.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. The layered order — VM running, correct IP, NSG, OS firewall, app listening — finds the fault fast instead of guessing. Most beginner blocks are an NSG rule or a stopped VM.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Query the name from Cloud Shell with: nslookup web1.lab.internal (or dig web1.lab.internal +short). Success returns 10.0.1.4 — but only from a machine inside the linked VNet; Cloud Shell is not in your VNet, so it will NOT resolve this private name, which itself proves the zone is private. In the portal, the zone’s record-set list showing web1 → 10.0.1.4 is your verification that the record exists.. This teaches the key insight: private DNS is scoped to the VNet, so where you query from determines what you get.

Day 11 — Azure Storage Accounts, Blobs, Containers, and File Shares60–90 minutes

A. Day 11 — Azure Storage Accounts, Blobs, Containers, and File Shares

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain storage accounts, containers, blobs, and file shares with a plain-words analogy
  • Choose sensible performance, redundancy, and access-tier settings, and handle access keys securely by never copying them anywhere
  • Create a storage account and a container in the portal
  • Upload a blob, change its access tier, and explain Hot vs Cool
  • Create a file share, upload a file, and describe how VMs mount it

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account; Days 7–10 completed
  • Browser signed in to the Azure portal
  • A small file on your computer to upload (any text file or image under 1 MB)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Picture a warehouse. The storage account is the warehouse building itself — it has a name, an address, and building-wide settings like how many backup copies to keep. Inside, blob storage is the shelving system: containers are the aisles (named sections like “day11files”), and blobs are the individual boxes on the shelves (your files). You never place a box on the warehouse floor — every blob lives inside a container. In a separate wing, Azure Files offers file shares: shared network drives that multiple computers can mount at once, like the shared drive in an office.

Three settings control cost and durability, and they are worth understanding before you click. Performance (Standard vs. Premium) sets the speed class — Standard is the economical default. Redundancy sets how many copies Azure keeps: LRS (locally redundant storage) keeps three copies in one datacenter and is the cheapest; ZRS spreads copies across zones, GRS across regions, each costing more. The access tier prices how often you touch the data: Hot for files you read often, Cool for files you rarely read (cheaper to store, pricier to access), Archive for deep storage you almost never open.

A security habit starts today and never leaves: storage accounts have access keys — master passwords for all the data inside. Treat them like house keys: never paste them into chat, email, or code. In this lab you will use the portal (which signs in as you) and the CLI with --auth-mode login, so no keys are copied anywhere. Later in your career you will meet managed identities and Key Vault, the grown-up ways to handle secrets.

  • Storage account — the container for all storage services; settings apply building-wide.
  • Container / blob — a named section / an individual file in blob storage.
  • File share — SMB shared drive mountable by multiple VMs.
  • LRS / ZRS / GRS — how many copies and where; Hot / Cool / Archive — pricing by access frequency.

Storage is pay-for-what-you-use down to the gigabyte, so a lab that uploads a few small files costs fractions of a cent — the free credit covers it many times over. Portal screens and prices change over time; use portal search if a label moved.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a storage account, upload a file to a blob container, change its access tier, create a file share, and upload a file there — all through the portal.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; portal access. Optional: Cloud Shell for the assessment’s practical question.

Cost note: Fractions of a cent. A few small files on Standard/LRS/Hot cost almost nothing; the free credit covers it. Delete the resource group when done so the account does not linger.

Step 1. Portal → top search bar → type 'Storage accounts' → open it → click '+ Create'.

Expected result: The 'Create a storage account' form opens.

Verify: The Basics tab of the storage account form is displayed.

Step 2. Basics tab: Subscription = your free-trial subscription. Resource group → 'Create new' → rg-day11 → OK. Storage account name → type a globally unique lowercase name, 3–24 characters, letters and numbers only — for example stday11 followed by your initials and a number, like stday11abc1.

Expected result: The name is accepted (no red error) and the new resource group is selected.

Verify: A green check confirms the name is available.

Troubleshooting: 'Name not available' means someone else took it — add more characters until the green check appears. No uppercase, no dashes.

Step 3. Region → your usual region (for example, East US). Performance → Standard. Redundancy → 'Locally-redundant storage (LRS)' — the cheapest option, three copies in one datacenter.

Expected result: The region dropdown lists Azure regions.

Verify: Performance reads Standard and Redundancy reads LRS.

Step 4. Click 'Review + create' → 'Create'. Wait for deployment, then 'Go to resource'.

Expected result: Deployment succeeds after about a minute.

Verify: The storage account overview page opens showing the account name.

Step 5. In the left menu, under 'Data storage', click 'Containers' → '+ Container'. Name → day11files. Public access level → Private (the default — no anonymous access). Click Create.

Expected result: The 'New container' panel opens on the right.

Verify: day11files appears in the containers list.

Step 6. Click into day11files → 'Upload' → select a small file from your computer (any text file or image under 1 MB) → click Upload. Wait for the 'Successfully uploaded' notification.

Expected result: The upload panel opens.

Verify: Your file is listed inside day11files.

Troubleshooting: If the upload stalls, try a smaller file — large files are unnecessary for this lab.

Step 7. Click your uploaded blob → 'Change tier' (or open its Properties). Change the access tier from Hot to Cool, save, then change it back to Hot. Say out loud when you would use Cool: data you keep but rarely read, like old backups.

Expected result: The blob’s properties show Access tier: Hot (or Cool after change).

Verify: You saw the tier change in both directions.

Troubleshooting: Tier changes can take a moment to reflect — refresh the properties if it still shows the old tier.

Step 8. Back in the storage account left menu, under 'Data storage', click 'File shares' → '+ File share'. Name → shared. Tier → Transaction optimized (the default). Click Create.

Expected result: The 'New file share' panel opens.

Verify: The 'shared' file share appears in the list.

Step 9. Click into the 'shared' file share → 'Upload' → choose a small file → Upload. Then click 'Connect' at the top and read the Windows/Linux/macOS connection info — notice it shows how multiple machines could mount this same share as a drive. You do not need to mount it today; understanding the concept is the goal.

Expected result: The upload panel for the file share opens.

Verify: The file is listed in the share and you can explain in one sentence how a VM would mount it.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Portal → search 'Resource groups' → click rg-day11 → 'Delete resource group' → type rg-day11 → Delete. Wait for the success notification and confirm rg-day11 is gone. Deleting the account deletes every container, blob, and file share inside it.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is the correct hierarchy in Azure Blob Storage?
    • A) Account → blob → container, and containers are optional
    • B) Storage account → container → blob; every blob lives inside a container
    • C) Blob → file share → account
    • D) Container → account → blob
  2. Q2. Which storage account settings are the sensible, cheapest choice for learning labs?
    • A) Premium SSD with ultra disks
    • B) Standard performance with LRS (locally redundant storage)
    • C) Geo-redundant with read access plus a private endpoint
    • D) Storage accounts have no redundancy setting
  3. Q3. When should data use the Cool access tier instead of Hot?
    • A) Hot is cheapest for everything
    • B) Hot for frequently accessed data; Cool for rarely accessed data (cheaper storage, higher access cost)
    • C) Cool deletes data after 30 days
    • D) Tiers only affect the account name
  4. Q4. Why would you pick an Azure file share over blob storage?
    • A) File shares are faster than local disks
    • B) Blobs can be mounted as drive letters
    • C) File shares use the SMB protocol and mount as shared drives on multiple VMs; blobs are object storage accessed by API/URL
    • D) There is no difference
  5. Q5. Practical: from Cloud Shell, upload a local file to your container using the Azure CLI with your own login (not an account key). What is the exact command, and what does success look like?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. The account is the front door (name + settings like redundancy); containers are the aisles; blobs are the boxes on the shelves. You never upload a blob without putting it in a container.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. LRS keeps three copies in one datacenter — the cheapest option and fine for learning. GRS/ZRS add geographic or zone resilience at higher cost, which you do not need for lab files.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Hot is for data you touch often; Cool is cheaper to store but costs more to read — right for backups and archives you rarely open. Archive is the cheapest shelf but takes hours to retrieve from.
  4. Q4 — Answer: C. A file share speaks the SMB file-sharing protocol, so it mounts as a drive letter (Windows) or mount point (Linux) on multiple VMs at once — exactly like a shared office drive. Blobs are object storage accessed via APIs and URLs, not mounted drives.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Upload the file with: az storage blob upload --account-name <your-account> --container-name day11files --name notes.txt --file notes.txt --auth-mode login. Success prints a JSON block with the blob’s URL and properties and no error. (The --auth-mode login flag uses your Azure sign-in instead of a key — least privilege in action: no keys copied around.). This proves you can drive storage from the CLI too, and the --auth-mode login detail reinforces the no-keys habit.

Day 12 — Azure App Service and Basic Web Application Deployment60–90 minutes

A. Day 12 — Azure App Service and Basic Web Application Deployment

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain PaaS vs IaaS and App Service vs App Service plan with a plain-words analogy
  • Create a Free-tier App Service plan and a web app in the portal
  • Browse the default page of a new web app and explain what the default domain is
  • Deploy a custom HTML page using ZIP deploy from Cloud Shell
  • Describe deployment slots and CI/CD as next steps without doing them yet

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account; Days 7–11 completed
  • Browser signed in to the Azure portal; Cloud Shell (Bash)
  • Your usual region noted (reuse it today)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

On Day 8 you built a computer and installed everything yourself — that is IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service): maximum control, maximum chores (patching, scaling, uptime are all yours). Azure App Service is PaaS (Platform as a Service): the restaurant-kitchen analogy. Instead of constructing the building, you rent a professional kitchen — ovens, gas, health inspections handled — and you just bring your recipes (code). Azure runs the servers, patches the OS, and offers scaling; you deploy a web app and get a public URL in minutes.

Two objects work together. The App Service plan is the kitchen itself: it defines the region, the size of the workers, and how many of them run — including the Free (F1) tier, which costs nothing but has strict quotas (limited daily compute, no custom domains, no always-on). The web app is your restaurant running in that kitchen: it gets a globally unique name that becomes your URL, like myday12app.azurewebsites.net, and a runtime stack (Node, Python, .NET, Java, PHP) that must match your code.

Getting code onto the app is called deployment. Today you will use ZIP deploy: zip your files and hand the package to Azure, which unpacks and restarts the app. Professionals later graduate to deployment slots (a staging copy of the app you can swap into production) and CI/CD pipelines (automatic deploy on every code change) — know the names today, master them later.

  • IaaS / PaaS — rent the computer vs. rent the managed platform.
  • App Service — the PaaS for hosting web apps and APIs.
  • App Service plan — the compute behind the app (region, size, scale, pricing tier).
  • Runtime stack — the language platform your code runs on; ZIP deploy — publish code as a zip package.

Cost note: the F1 Free tier used here costs nothing, but its quotas are real — if the app sleeps or refuses connections after heavy use, you likely hit the daily compute limit; wait or redeploy tomorrow. Free-tier availability varies by region, so if F1 is missing, pick another large region. Portal screens and limits change over time; use portal search if a label moved.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a Free-tier web app in the portal, view its default page, then deploy your own HTML page with ZIP deploy from Cloud Shell.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; portal access; Cloud Shell (Bash).

Cost note: Free. The F1 (Free) App Service plan has no hourly charge; quotas (daily compute minutes) apply instead of billing. Delete the resource group when done.

Step 1. Portal → top search bar → type 'App Services' → open it → click '+ Create' → 'Web App'.

Expected result: The 'Create Web App' form opens.

Verify: The Basics tab of the web app form is displayed.

Step 2. Basics tab: Subscription = your free-trial subscription. Resource group → 'Create new' → rg-day12 → OK. Name → a globally unique name, lowercase letters and numbers, like webday12 followed by your initials and a number (for example webday12abc1) — this becomes your URL: webday12abc1.azurewebsites.net.

Expected result: The new resource group and a unique app name are accepted.

Verify: A green check confirms the name is available.

Troubleshooting: 'Name not available' means it is taken worldwide — add more characters until the check passes.

Step 3. Publish → Code. Runtime stack → Node 20 LTS (any stack works for a plain HTML page; Node is a common default). Operating System → Linux.

Expected result: Publish = Code and the runtime dropdown lists language stacks.

Verify: Publish shows Code and a runtime stack is selected.

Step 4. Region → your usual region (for example, East US). Keep your Week 2 resources together.

Expected result: Your usual region is selected.

Verify: The region matches the one you used on Days 8–11.

Step 5. Pricing plans → click 'Explore pricing plans' (or the plan dropdown) → select the 'Free F1' tier → Select/Apply. This is the zero-cost tier with quotas — exactly what a lab needs.

Expected result: The pricing plan selector opens.

Verify: The plan summary reads Free F1 (or similar free-tier wording).

Troubleshooting: If F1 is not offered in your region, go back and choose a different large region such as East US, then try again.

Step 6. Click 'Review + create' → 'Create'. Wait for 'Your deployment is complete', then 'Go to resource'.

Expected result: Deployment completes after 1–3 minutes.

Verify: The web app overview page opens.

Troubleshooting: Name-taken errors at this stage are rare since the name was validated — if deployment fails, note the error and retry once.

Step 7. On the overview page, click the 'Default domain' URL (your-name.azurewebsites.net). A Microsoft default page appears — this proves the app is live before you change anything.

Expected result: A default Azure landing page loads in the browser.

Verify: The default page loads with HTTP 200 (no error screen).

Troubleshooting: If the page shows an error, wait 2–3 minutes — the app can need a moment after first creation — then refresh.

Step 8. Open Cloud Shell (Bash) from the top toolbar. You will build the deployment package here.

Expected result: A Cloud Shell terminal opens.

Verify: You see the Cloud Shell prompt.

Step 9. Type: echo '<h1>Hello from Day 12</h1><p>My first Azure deployment.</p>' > index.html. This creates a tiny web page. Then type: ls -l to confirm index.html is there.

Expected result: An index.html file exists in the current directory.

Verify: ls -l lists index.html.

Troubleshooting: Mind the quotes — single quotes around the HTML, double quotes are not needed here.

Step 10. Type: zip app.zip index.html. (If zip is missing, install it first: sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y zip — this installs inside Cloud Shell, which is fine.)

Expected result: app.zip is created containing index.html.

Verify: The output says 'adding: index.html' and ls -l shows app.zip.

Troubleshooting: 'command not found' means the install step was skipped — run it, then retry zip.

Step 11. Type: az webapp deploy --resource-group rg-day12 --name <your-app-name> --src-path app.zip --type zip. Replace <your-app-name> with the unique name from step 2. This uploads the zip; Azure unpacks it and restarts the app. Wait 1–2 minutes.

Expected result: A JSON response describing the deployment, ending without errors.

Verify: The command finishes with a JSON block and no error message.

Troubleshooting: 'Resource not found' means the app name or resource group is mistyped — compare letter by letter with the portal. Deployment taking a while is normal; wait before retrying.

Step 12. Back in the browser, hard-refresh your app’s URL (Ctrl+Shift+R or Cmd+Shift+R). Your custom page should now load.

Expected result: Your heading 'Hello from Day 12' appears instead of the default page.

Verify: Your own HTML is visible at your azurewebsites.net URL.

Troubleshooting: Still seeing the default page? Wait another 2 minutes and hard-refresh again — browsers cache the old page aggressively.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Portal → search 'Resource groups' → click rg-day12 → 'Delete resource group' → type rg-day12 → Delete. Wait for the success notification and confirm rg-day12 is gone. This removes the web app and its Free plan together.

G. Practical Assignment

On your own, change your index.html to include today’s date and one line about what you learned in Week 2, re-zip, and redeploy with the same az webapp deploy command. Browse the site and confirm the change appears. Then delete rg-day12. No solution is provided — repetition is what makes deployment feel routine.

Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)

Then hard-refresh https://<your-app-name>.azurewebsites.net. Success looks like your heading replacing the default page; a deployment-log entry appears under the app’s Deployment Center.. This is the smallest real deployment loop: edit → zip → deploy → browse. The same shape scales to professional CI/CD pipelines later.

Day 13 — Azure Functions and Serverless Computing Fundamentals60–90 minutes

A. Day 13 — Azure Functions and Serverless Computing Fundamentals

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain serverless computing and when it fits better than VMs or App Service
  • Describe the anatomy of an Azure Functions app: triggers, bindings, and code
  • Identify common trigger types (HTTP, timer, queue) and what each is used for
  • Compare the Functions hosting plans (Flex Consumption, Premium, Dedicated) at a beginner level
  • Create, run, and delete an HTTP-triggered function using the Azure portal

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completion of Days 1–12, especially Day 10 (App Service), so you can compare hosting styles

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Serverless computing means you write code and run it without provisioning or managing any servers yourself. You still "use" servers — you just don't see, patch, or pay for them when idle. The analogy: ordering food at a restaurant versus renting a whole kitchen. With a VM (Virtual Machine) you rent the kitchen (always paying). With App Service you rent a kitchen with a chef who runs the shifts. With serverless, you order individual dishes: the restaurant appears when you order, cooks exactly your dish, and you pay per dish.

Azure Functions is Microsoft's serverless platform for running small units of code that respond to events. You write one function — a single piece of code with one job, like "when this file arrives, resize it" or "every hour, clean up old data." A Function App is the container that groups related functions together so they share settings, like an app shares a name and address.

Every function has a trigger (what wakes it up) and bindings (the inputs and outputs it can connect to without boilerplate code). Common triggers: HTTP trigger (runs when a web request hits a URL), timer trigger (runs on a schedule), queue trigger (runs when a message lands in a queue). Bindings let your function read a blob, write to a database row, or send a queue message by just declaring it — no connection code required.

Hosting plans control how your functions scale and what you pay. The Flex Consumption plan (recommended for new apps) scales from zero automatically and you pay only for what runs — true serverless billing. The Premium plan keeps always-warm instances ready for instant responses. The Dedicated plan runs functions inside an existing App Service plan with predictable costs. Beginners on a free account should use Flex Consumption.

Cold start is a term worth knowing: when a function hasn't run in a while, the platform may take a few seconds to wake it up before it responds. This matters for user-facing APIs (which is why Premium offers always-warm), but not for background jobs. Portal UI, free-tier limits, and billing details change over time — check the current pricing calculator before any real-world use. This workshop is not sponsored, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft.

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure Functions overview (Microsoft Learn)Documentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-functions/functions-overview VerifiedOfficial explanation of triggers, bindings, scenarios, and hosting plans — the reference behind today's lab15 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a serverless Function App with an HTTP-triggered function in the Azure portal, call it from a browser, then clean it up.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. A web browser. You do not need any code editor — the portal has one built in.

Cost note: Free tier: the Flex Consumption plan's first executions each month are free. Watch the free-tier limits; delete the resource group when done.

Step 1. Create a resource group. Portal top search bar → type "Resource groups" → open Resource groups → + Create. Subscription: your free subscription. Name: rg-serverless-day13. Region: East US (or your Day-4 region). Click Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The rg-serverless-day13 resource group appears in the resource group list.

Verify: Refresh the Resource groups blade; rg-serverless-day13 is listed with your subscription and region.

Troubleshooting: If Create is greyed out, check your subscription dropdown shows your free subscription and all required fields are filled.

Step 2. Create the Function App. Portal top search bar → type "Function App" → open Function App → + Create. On the Basics tab: Subscription: your free subscription. Resource Group: rg-serverless-day13. Function App name: func-day13-<yourname> (must be globally unique, letters/numbers only — if taken, add digits). Runtime stack: Node.js (or Python). Version: latest stable offered. Region: same region as your resource group. Hosting: Flex Consumption plan.

Expected result: The create form validates and the Review + create button becomes available.

Verify: Portal shows no red validation errors on the Basics tab; your app name has a green check.

Troubleshooting: Name already taken: add more digits. Region mismatch error: pick the same region as the resource group.

Step 3. Finish creation. Click Review + create → Create. Wait for the deployment spinner to finish (1–2 minutes). Click "Go to resource" when it appears.

Expected result: A green "Your deployment is complete" banner; the Function App overview blade opens.

Verify: The overview blade shows your function app name, runtime stack, and status Running.

Troubleshooting: Deployment stuck over 10 minutes: check Notifications (bell icon) for the error, then delete and recreate with a different app name.

Step 4. Create a function. Inside the Function App blade, left menu → Functions → + Create (or + Add). In the template list choose "HTTP trigger". Template details: Function name: greet. Authorization level: Anonymous (this makes the URL callable without a key — fine for this lab, never use this for production). Click Create.

Expected result: The greet function appears in the Functions list.

Verify: Functions list shows greet with trigger type "Http" and status Enabled.

Troubleshooting: Template list empty or fails to load: wait a minute and refresh; the portal sometimes lags on a fresh Function App.

Step 5. Look at the code. Select the greet function → Code + Test (left menu). Read the sample code: notice the parts that read the HTTP request, and notice you never wrote any server code — the platform provides the web server. Do not change anything yet.

Expected result: Sample code for your runtime stack loads in the editor pane.

Verify: You can see a line that builds the response body (e.g. returning a message with the name query parameter).

Troubleshooting: Code pane blank: refresh the blade; the editor can take a few seconds on first open.

Step 6. Get the URL and test it. Select the greet function → Get function URL (top of the blade or in Code + Test) → copy the URL. Paste it into a new browser tab, then add ?name=Siva to the end and press Enter.

Expected result: The browser shows a greeting response containing "Siva" (or the default response if your stack differs).

Verify: The page text includes the name you passed in the query string — your function ran in the cloud, from that URL, with no server you managed.

Troubleshooting: 404 or function not found: you copied a URL for a different function or the app name; re-copy via Get function URL.

Step 7. Check the trigger in the portal. Back in the greet blade, open Integration (left menu). Notice the trigger section says httpTrigger and shows how bindings declare inputs and outputs without extra code.

Expected result: The Integration blade lists one trigger (httpTrigger) with its binding configuration.

Verify: You can point to the trigger name and explain in one sentence what wakes your function up.

Step 8. Clean up. Portal top search bar → "Resource groups" → open rg-serverless-day13 → Delete resource group (top toolbar). Type the resource group name exactly to confirm → Delete. Wait for the deletion notification.

Expected result: The resource group disappears from the list after a few minutes.

Verify: Resource groups list no longer shows rg-serverless-day13; Function App is gone (billing stopped).

Troubleshooting: Delete fails: some resources take minutes to release; retry deletion after 5 minutes.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the resource group rg-serverless-day13: Portal → Resource groups → rg-serverless-day13 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Confirm it is gone from the list. This removes the Function App, its storage account, and the plan in one step.

Do not build them.

  2. Q2. In Azure Functions, what is a trigger?

  3. Q3. Which hosting plan should a beginner on the free account choose for true serverless, pay-for-what-runs billing?

  5. Q5. Practical: You need a task that runs every night at 2 AM to archive old database records. Which trigger fits, and why?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Serverless hides server management from you; the provider still runs servers and you pay for actual executions (scale-to-zero). A is wrong — servers exist, you just don't manage them.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. The trigger is the wake-up event for a function. Bindings are the declared inputs/outputs; the trigger is specifically what starts execution.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. Flex Consumption is the recommended serverless plan: automatic scale-from-zero and pay-as-you-go billing. Premium is for always-warm speed; Dedicated is predictable-cost App Service hosting.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Cold start is the warm-up delay when the platform loads an idle function. It matters for latency-sensitive APIs, not background jobs.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Timer trigger, because the job is time-based and runs on a fixed schedule regardless of user activity. An HTTP trigger would require someone to call a URL, and a queue trigger would require a message to be posted — neither matches a nightly schedule.. Match the trigger to the event: scheduled work → timer trigger; web requests → HTTP trigger; background messages → queue trigger.

Day 14 — Week 2 Practical Lab and Assessment60–90 minutes

A. Day 14 — Week 2 Practical Lab and Assessment

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Combine the Week 2 building blocks (VNet, VM, storage, App Service) into one working system
  • Apply integration thinking: trace a request across multiple Azure services
  • Practice a repeatable integration-debugging method (isolate, follow the path, check the locks, read the logs)
  • Explain why budgets are alerts, not spending caps, and why deleting resource groups is the real cost control
  • Pass a self-test that checks the full Week 2 skill set

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account
  • Completion of Days 1–13 (VMs, networking, storage, App Service)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Week 2 taught you compute (the VMs from Days 5–6), networking (virtual networks, subnets, NSGs from Days 7–8), storage (Days 9–10-era storage accounts and blob storage), and App Service (hosted web apps). Today you assemble all four into one working system — the way real cloud systems look. The analogy: a restaurant (your whole application) has a dining room (App Service, what the public sees), a kitchen (the VM running the database), a pantry (storage), and hallways between them (the virtual network) with locked doors (NSGs).

The key skill this week: integration thinking. A VM alone is a box. A web app alone is a website. Connected together with correct network paths (which subnet can reach which), identity and access (who is allowed in), and connection strings (secret addresses apps use to find databases — never publish these), they become a real system. Most real outages are integration failures, not single broken parts.

Debugging integration has a repeatable pattern: (1) Isolate — test each piece alone. Does the web app load? Does the VM respond? (2) Follow the path — trace the traffic from browser → web app → VNet → VM, and ask where it stops. (3) Check the locks — NSG rules, firewalls, and connection strings are the usual suspects. (4) Read the logs — the portal's Diagnose and Metrics blades tell you what the service itself sees.

Budgets are ALERTS, not spending caps. This is the most important safety fact of the workshop: setting a budget in Cost Management sends you an email when spending crosses a threshold — it does NOT stop Azure from spending, and the alert can arrive hours late. The only way to stop costs in this lab is to delete the resource group at the end, which you will do today. VMs bill by the hour while they exist (even stopped, they still reserve the VM), so clean up promptly.

Portal UI, pricing, and free-tier limits change over time — always verify current prices in the pricing calculator before building real systems. This workshop is not sponsored, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure pricing calculatorToolhttps://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/ VerifiedEstimate costs before building — shows how a VM, storage, and App Service plan add up15 min
Azure free accountDocumentationhttps://azure.microsoft.com/free/ VerifiedReview what is free versus pay-as-you-go before running a billable VM lab10 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Build a small four-part system (VNet + storage account + App Service web app + VM), verify each piece, practice one controlled failure, then delete everything.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. Completion of Days 1–13. About 60 minutes of uninterrupted time.

Cost note: A small (B1s/B2s) VM bills per hour while it exists, even when stopped — delete the resource group when finished. Storage and App Service use free tiers. Build, verify, then delete promptly.

Step 1. Create a resource group. Portal → search "Resource groups" → + Create. Name: rg-week2-build. Region: your usual region (e.g. East US). Review + create → Create.

Expected result: rg-week2-build appears in the resource group list.

Verify: Refresh the Resource groups blade; rg-week2-build is listed.

Step 2. Create the virtual network. Portal → search "Virtual networks" → + Create. Resource group: rg-week2-build. Name: vnet-week2. Region: same region. IPv4 address space: 10.1.0.0/16. Add two subnets: subnet-app (10.1.1.0/24) and subnet-db (10.1.2.0/24). Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The VNet validation passes; the overview blade shows address space 10.1.0.0/16 with two subnets.

Verify: Open vnet-week2 → Subnets; both subnet-app and subnet-db are listed.

Troubleshooting: Address space overlap error: keep the default 10.x ranges shown and do not reuse another lab's range.

Step 3. Create the storage account. Portal → search "Storage accounts" → + Create. Resource group: rg-week2-build. Storage account name: stweek2<randomdigits> (must be globally unique, lowercase). Performance: Standard. Redundancy: LRS. Region: same region. Create. After deployment, open the account → left menu Data management → Static website → Enabled → set index document name index.html → Save.

Expected result: Storage account deploys; static website shows an Enabled toggle.

Verify: Static website blade shows Enabled and a Primary endpoint URL.

Troubleshooting: Name taken: add more random digits — storage names must be globally unique.

Step 4. Upload a test page. In the storage account → left menu Data storage → Containers → $web container → Upload → choose a simple index.html file from your computer (any file with a hello message; create one in Notepad/TextEdit if needed) → Upload. Copy the Primary endpoint from the Static website blade and open it in a new browser tab.

Expected result: The Primary endpoint URL loads your page text in a browser.

Verify: Your browser shows the contents of your index.html — your storage is serving web content.

Troubleshooting: 404 on the page: the file name must be exactly index.html and must live inside the $web container.

Step 5. Create the App Service web app. Portal → search "App Service" → + Create → Web App. Resource group: rg-week2-build. Name: web-week2-<randomdigits> (globally unique). Publish: Code. Runtime stack: Node 20 LTS (or .NET). Operating System: Linux. Region: same region. Pricing plan: create new, SKU: Free F1. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The App Service plan and web app deploy; the overview blade shows a default URL.

Verify: Open the web app → Overview; the URL loads a default welcome page.

Troubleshooting: Free F1 unavailable in a region: switch to the Basic B1 SKU (billable) and delete promptly at the end, or retry another region.

Step 6. Create the VM. Portal → search "Virtual machines" → + Create → Azure virtual machine. Resource group: rg-week2-build. Name: vm-db-week2. Region: same region. Image: Ubuntu Server. Size: Standard_B1s. Authentication: password (write it in your journal only — never publish it). Virtual network: vnet-week2, subnet: subnet-db. Public inbound ports: None. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The VM deploys; its overview blade shows Running status and a public IP.

Verify: VM overview shows Running and the virtual network vnet-week2 attached.

Troubleshooting: Size unavailable: try Standard_B2s or another region; availability varies.

Step 7. Draw the system and verify the pieces. On paper, draw: browser → web app (public URL) → VNet → VM in subnet-db; storage static site (public URL). Then open each resource's overview blade and confirm it matches your drawing: web app URL works, storage static URL works, VM is Running in vnet-week2.

Expected result: You can explain, in your own words, how traffic flows from the browser to each part.

Verify: All four resources exist in rg-week2-build and you can point at each on your drawing.

Step 8. Practice a controlled failure. Stop the VM: open vm-db-week2 → Stop → confirm. Observe: the web app and storage still work — the system degrades, but parts are independent. Then start the VM again.

Expected result: The web app shows its default page (or an error you recognize); stopping the VM does not take the web app down, because they are separate pieces.

Verify: You observed and wrote down which pieces kept working while the VM was stopped.

Step 9. Clean up everything. Portal → Resource groups → rg-week2-build → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Confirm deletion in the list.

Expected result: The resource group disappears; Cost Management shows no running resources in this lab.

Verify: Resource groups no longer lists rg-week2-build.

Troubleshooting: Deletion hangs: retry after a few minutes; VMs sometimes take time to release.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the resource group rg-week2-build: Portal → Resource groups → rg-week2-build → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Verify it is gone. Double-check the Billing/Cost Management view shows no new ongoing costs.

G. Practical Assignment

Run the troubleshooting checklist from memory on one of the resources in your Week 2 build (without looking at the lab): find the resource, open its Diagnose/Metrics blade, and describe in your journal what you checked and what you found. Then delete everything.

  1. Q1. Your Week 2 build has a VM that must not be reachable from the public internet, but its subnet needs to reach a storage account. Which control do you adjust FIRST?
    • A) Network security group (NSG) rules on the VM's subnet or NIC
    • B) Storage account private endpoint settings
    • C) Web app application settings
    • D) The Azure firewall inside the VM's operating system only
  2. Q2. Your App Service web app shows a database connection error even though the database VM is running in your virtual network. What is the MOST likely cause?
    • A) The web app needs more CPU
    • B) The storage account region is wrong
    • C) App Service by default cannot reach resources inside a VNet — the architecture needs redesign, e.g. VNet integration or a different path
    • D) The budget alert is misconfigured
  3. Q3. Why does every lab in this workshop tell you to create one resource group per lab?
    • A) The resource group would delete my subscription
    • B) Everything in the lab is deleted at once, in one step
    • C) Resources are renamed to their old values
    • D) Nothing happens; resource groups cannot be deleted
  4. Q4. What does the Azure pricing calculator (and Cost Management) actually do?
    • A) It puts a hard spending cap on your subscription
    • B) It emails you a bill every hour
    • C) It estimates before you build and shows actual spend after — it never blocks spending by itself
    • D) It deletes resources that cost money
  5. Q5. Practical: On paper, redesign your Week 2 build so the web app reaches the database but the database is NOT reachable from the public internet. Draw the VNet, subnets, NSG rules, and traffic arrows, then explain each choice.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: A. NSG rules at the subnet/NIC level are the correct way to limit inbound traffic. Storage private endpoints and app settings don't open VM ports; firewall settings refer to different products.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. App Service's sandbox normally blocks outbound access to Azure VM virtual networks by default. The design itself needs correcting (e.g., expose the DB over a private network path), not just retries.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Resource groups are a management boundary: deleting it removes everything the lab created in one action. That's exactly why the safety policy requires one resource group per lab.
  4. Q4 — Answer: C. Cost management is estimation — the calculator estimates and Cost Management shows actuals; nothing hard-blocks spending. Only a spending limit on the free account does that, and that's a different setting.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Redesign step-by-step, explaining each change: move the database into the same VNet, add VNet integration to the web app, and write NSG rules that allow the web-app subnet to reach the database subnet but block direct internet access to the database. The answer is judged on the learner's reasoning about network paths and least privilege.. Tests whether the learner can think in network topology and least privilege across services, not just click through a portal.
