Weekly Review — Week 2 Review - Compute, Networking, and Storage

Week 2 covered virtual machines, App Service, Functions, virtual networks, and storage. Ten questions below. Try them closed-book first.

  1. Q1. You need a full server where you control the operating system, patches, and installed software. Which Azure service do you choose?
    • A) Azure App Service
    • B) Azure Virtual Machines
    • C) Azure Functions
    • D) Azure DNS
  2. Q2. Availability Zones protect your application against:
    • A) A hacker attack
    • B) Failure of an entire datacenter within a region
    • C) A typo in your application code
    • D) An expired user password
  3. Q3. A network security group (NSG) is used to:
    • A) Store passwords securely
    • B) Allow or deny inbound and outbound network traffic using rules
    • C) Back up virtual machines
    • D) Translate domain names to IP addresses
  4. Q4. Two NSG rules conflict with each other. Which rule is applied?
    • A) The rule created most recently
    • B) The rule with the lower priority number
    • C) The rule with the higher priority number
    • D) Both rules are applied at once
  5. Q5. Which storage redundancy option keeps a copy of your data in a second, paired region?
    • A) LRS
    • B) ZRS
    • C) GRS
    • D) Premium SSD
  6. Q6. You need to store unstructured files such as images, videos, and backups. Which Azure Storage service fits best?
    • A) Azure Files
    • B) Blob Storage
    • C) Azure Queues
    • D) Azure Tables
  7. Q7. What is the key difference between the Hot, Cool, and Archive blob access tiers?
    • A) The file formats they support
    • B) The trade-off between storage cost and retrieval speed and cost
    • C) The maximum file size allowed
    • D) The Azure regions they are available in
  8. Q8. You want code to run automatically whenever a new file is uploaded to Blob Storage. Which Azure Functions trigger do you use?
    • A) HTTP trigger
    • B) Timer trigger
    • C) Blob trigger
    • D) Queue trigger
  9. Q9. A subnet is:
    • A) A billing category
    • B) A range of IP addresses carved out of a virtual network to group resources
    • C) A type of storage account
    • D) A user role in Microsoft Entra ID
  10. Q10. An App Service plan defines:
    • A) The domain name of your app
    • B) The region, pricing tier, and compute size shared by the apps running on it
    • C) The programming language your app uses
    • D) The backup schedule for your app
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Azure Virtual Machines is IaaS: you get a full VM and manage the OS yourself. App Service and Functions abstract the OS away.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Each Availability Zone is a physically separate datacenter (independent power, cooling, networking) inside a region. Spreading VMs across zones survives a datacenter failure.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. An NSG holds allow/deny rules based on source, destination, port, and protocol. Attach it to a subnet or a network interface.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. NSG rules are evaluated in priority order and the first match wins. Lower number means higher priority (for example, 100 beats 1000).
  5. Q5 — Answer: C. GRS (geo-redundant storage) replicates data to a paired secondary region. LRS keeps copies in one datacenter; ZRS spreads them across zones in one region.
  6. Q6 — Answer: B. Blob Storage is the object store for unstructured data. Azure Files is for SMB/NFS file shares; Queues is for messages; Tables is for NoSQL key-value data.
  7. Q7 — Answer: B. Hot costs more to store but is cheapest to access; Cool and Archive cost less to store but more (and slower) to retrieve. Pick the tier that matches how often you read the data.
  8. Q8 — Answer: C. A trigger defines the event that starts a function. The Blob trigger fires when a blob is created or updated in the watched container.
  9. Q9 — Answer: B. You divide a VNet's address space (for example, 10.0.0.0/16) into subnets (for example, 10.0.1.0/24) to organize resources and control traffic.
  10. Q10 — Answer: B. The plan is the compute behind your web apps: region, tier (Free, Basic, Standard, and so on), and instance size/count. You pay for the plan; multiple apps can share it.
Day 15 — Azure Key Vault and Secrets Management60–90 minutes

A. Day 15 — Azure Key Vault and Secrets Management

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what Azure Key Vault is and why secrets must not live in code or config files
  • Distinguish secrets, keys, and certificates at a beginner level
  • Create a key vault and store/read a secret using the Azure portal
  • Apply RBAC roles and access policies with least privilege
  • Describe soft-delete and audit logging as safety nets for secrets

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account
  • Completion of Days 1–14, especially the RBAC concepts from Day 4

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Azure Key Vault is a managed service for storing and controlling access to secrets (passwords, API keys, connection strings), keys (cryptographic keys used by Azure services for encryption), and certificates (files that prove a server's identity for TLS/SSL — the "https" padlock). The analogy: a bank's safe-deposit room. The vault is the room, each box is a secret, and the bank's guard — RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) — checks your identity before opening your box.

Why a vault instead of a config file? Because code and configuration travel: they get copied to laptops, committed to Git, shared in chat. Secrets must live in ONE guarded place where access is granted to identities, not copied around. The golden rule: never put a secret in code, app settings in screenshots, or chat. If a secret ever appears outside the vault, treat it as compromised and rotate it (generate a new one and replace the old).

Access control works in two layers. RBAC roles like "Key Vault Secrets User" and "Key Vault Secrets Officer" decide WHO can read or manage secrets. Access policies (the older model, still visible in the portal) decide WHAT operations each identity can do. Least privilege means: grant only the Secrets User role, to only the identities that truly need it, and remove it when they're done.

Two more concepts worth knowing: soft-delete keeps a deleted secret recoverable for 90 days, protecting against accidents; diagnostic logging records every access attempt so you have an audit trail. Azure encrypts your data at rest with Microsoft-managed keys by default — Key Vault becomes essential when you want customer-managed keys, where YOU control the key and can revoke it.

Portal UI, free-tier limits, and feature names change over time — verify against current Azure docs. Never share, screenshot, or publish real secrets. This workshop is not sponsored, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure Key Vault overview (Microsoft Learn)Documentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/general/overview VerifiedOfficial explanation of vaults, secrets, keys, certificates, and access control20 min
Azure free accountDocumentationhttps://azure.microsoft.com/free/ VerifiedCheck free-tier limits before creating any service, including Key Vault operations10 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a key vault, store a secret in it, read it back, control access with RBAC, and clean up.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. No code editor needed.

Cost note: Free tier: Key Vault operations are free for typical learning volumes. No billable resources in this lab.

Step 1. Create a resource group. Portal → search "Resource groups" → + Create. Name: rg-keyvault-day15. Region: your usual region (e.g. East US). Review + create → Create.

Expected result: rg-keyvault-day15 appears in the resource group list.

Verify: Refresh the list; rg-keyvault-day15 is present.

Step 2. Create the key vault. Portal → search "Key vaults" → + Create. Basics tab: Resource group: rg-keyvault-day15. Key vault name: kv-day15-<randomdigits> (must be globally unique, 3–24 letters/numbers). Region: same region. Pricing tier: Standard. Leave recovery options at defaults. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The key vault deploys; its overview blade shows the vault URI like https://kv-day15-xxx.vault.azure.net/.

Verify: Overview blade shows the vault name and its vault URI.

Troubleshooting: Name taken: add more digits. Region mismatch with policy: use the same region as your resource group.

Step 3. Create a secret. Open the key vault → left menu Objects → Secrets → + Generate/Import. Upload options: Manual. Name: db-password. Secret value: type a FAKE password like TrainingDemo-1234! (never use a real password in a lab). Leave activation/expiry empty. Create.

Expected result: The secret appears in the Secrets list with your name.

Verify: Secrets list shows db-password with the current version.

Troubleshooting: Generate/Import greyed out: you may not have the Secrets Officer role; wait a few minutes for RBAC to propagate, then retry.

Step 4. Read the secret back. Click db-password → click the current version → Show Secret Value (eye icon). Notice the portal makes you click to reveal — this is the least-privilege UI pattern. Copy nothing; just confirm you can see it.

Expected result: The secret value displays; you have confirmed read access works.

Verify: You saw the fake value displayed once.

Troubleshooting: 403 / access denied: your account needs Key Vault Secrets User or Officer role; wait a few minutes and retry.

Step 5. Check your own access. Key vault → left menu Access control (IAM) → Role assignments. Find your account listed with "Key Vault Secrets Officer" (granted by default to the creator). Notice you could add or remove people here.

Expected result: Your account is listed with the Secrets User role on the vault.

Verify: You can name which role your account holds on this vault.

Step 6. Turn on audit logging. Key vault → left menu Monitoring → Diagnostic settings → + Add diagnostic setting. Name: kv-audit. Select the "AuditEvent" log category. Destination: send to your Log Analytics workspace if you have one, or "Send to Log Analytics" later on Day 17. Save. Wait a few minutes, read your secret once more, then check where the log would land.

Expected result: The vault shows at least one logged access entry.

Verify: The diagnostic setting is listed; you know that every secret read is now recorded.

Troubleshooting: No Log Analytics workspace yet: that's expected — you create one on Day 17. The point today is knowing where the switch lives.

Step 7. Look at the safety nets. Key vault → Overview → Properties (or Settings → Properties). Find "Soft delete" and the retention days. Understand: deleting a secret or vault does not immediately destroy it — it is recoverable for the retention period.

Expected result: Soft-delete and purge settings are visible on the vault's Properties blade.

Verify: You can state in one sentence what soft-delete protects you from (accidental or malicious deletion).

Step 8. Clean up. Portal → Resource groups → rg-keyvault-day15 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Optional: to reuse the vault name, open Key vaults → Manage deleted vaults → select your vault → Purge.

Expected result: The resource group disappears; the deleted vault is listed under managed deleted vaults if not purged.

Verify: Resource groups no longer lists rg-keyvault-day15.

Troubleshooting: Purge option unavailable: your account needs the right permissions; leaving the vault in soft-delete is acceptable for a lab.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the resource group rg-keyvault-day15: Portal → Resource groups → rg-keyvault-day15 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Key vaults have soft-delete protection; after deleting the resource group, if you want the vault name reusable, go to the vault's "Managed deleted vaults" view in Key Vault and purge it — otherwise it is held for the retention period.

G. Practical Assignment

Find one place in your own notes or projects where a password or connection string is written in plain text. Describe how you would move it to Key Vault: what the secret would be named, who would get the Secrets User role, and what you would delete from your notes afterward.

  1. Q1. What is the primary job of Azure Key Vault?
    • A) To run serverless code
    • B) To store passwords, keys, connection strings, and certificates in one centrally guarded place
    • C) To store large files and videos
    • D) To scan application logs for errors
  2. Q2. What is the difference between secrets, keys, and certificates in Key Vault?
    • A) Secrets and certificates are the same thing
    • B) Keys are passwords for virtual machines
    • C) Secrets store credentials, certificates prove identity for TLS/SSL, and keys are cryptographic keys used by Azure services
    • D) All three are stored only in App Service
  3. Q3. In the safe-deposit analogy for Key Vault, what do the vault and the bank guard represent?
    • A) The billing currency
    • B) The region where it lives
    • C) What it is (the store) and how it is guarded (RBAC roles and access policies)
    • D) The name of the vault
  4. Q4. When do customer-managed keys in Key Vault matter versus Microsoft-managed encryption keys?
    • A) Key Vault is required for all Azure storage
    • B) Encryption is on by default; Key Vault matters when you want to control or revoke the keys yourself
    • C) Customer-managed keys make data unencrypted
    • D) You must bring your own hardware to use encryption
  5. Q5. Practical: Your team suspects a former contractor's account still had access to a production database password stored as a Key Vault secret. What is the FIRST thing you do in the vault, and which capability proves who accessed it?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Key Vault's job is to keep secrets out of code and configs in one guarded store. It doesn't run apps, store blobs, or inspect logs.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. Secrets are API keys, connection strings, and passwords. Certificates hold cryptographic identity material for TLS/SSL. Keys (in the KMS sense) are cryptographic keys used by Azure services for encryption at rest.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. A vault is the logical store; access is controlled by RBAC roles plus per-vault access policies. That's the governance boundary.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Azure encrypts with Microsoft-managed keys by default. Customer-managed keys in Key Vault give you control and revocation rights — the classic least-privilege trade: more control, more responsibility.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Audit trail, because the requirement is to PROVE who accessed a secret, not just to stop unauthorized access. RBAC stops it; logging proves it.. Tests that the learner separates enforcement (RBAC/policy) from evidence (logging/audit).

Day 16 — Azure Monitor, Metrics, Logs, and Alerts60–90 minutes

A. Day 16 — Azure Monitor, Metrics, Logs, and Alerts

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what Azure Monitor does, using the patient-monitor analogy
  • Distinguish metrics, logs, the Activity log, and insights
  • Read a metric chart for a real resource in the Metrics explorer
  • Build an alert rule with a threshold and attach an action group
  • Describe static vs dynamic thresholds at a beginner level

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account
  • Completion of Days 1–15, especially Day 5–6 (VMs) and Day 14 (the multi-resource build)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Azure Monitor is Microsoft's unified service for collecting, analyzing, and acting on telemetry — measurements about your cloud resources. Think of a hospital patient monitor: the metrics are the live numbers on the screen (heart rate 72 — for Azure, CPU 45%, requests per second 120), and the logs are the nurse's written diary ("at 2:04 PM the patient received medication" — for Azure, "at 2:04 PM a deployment happened"). Metrics are numbers over time; logs are timestamped records of events.

Metrics are stored automatically for nearly every Azure resource at no extra setup. You view them in Metrics (the Metrics explorer blade), where you pick a resource, a metric (like Percentage CPU), and a time range, and get a chart. Many services also ship insights — prebuilt dashboards like VM insights that show CPU, disk, memory, and top processes without you building charts.

The Activity log is a special log: it records control-plane events — WHO did WHAT to WHICH resource and WHEN ("Siva deleted vm-db at 9:41 PM"). It answers accountability questions and is the first place to look when something changed unexpectedly. Resource logs (diagnostic logs) record what happened INSIDE a service — data-plane events like failed login attempts on a database.

Alerts turn watching into action. An alert rule says WHEN to fire ("CPU above 80% for 5 minutes"). An action group says WHO gets told and HOW (email Siva, SMS the on-call, call a webhook). You can use static thresholds (a fixed number) or dynamic thresholds (machine learning that learns the normal pattern and fires on unusual deviation — good for spiky workloads).

Portal UI, metric names, and free-tier limits change over time — verify against current docs. Alerting on a deleted or idle lab resource wastes noise; always clean up. This workshop is not sponsored, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Point Azure Monitor at a small VM, graph its CPU metric, build an alert rule with an action group, and clean up.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. No extra tools needed.

Cost note: Free tier: viewing metrics and creating alert rules costs nothing. The small VM used as a signal source bills per hour while it exists — delete it promptly. Action groups with email/SMS notifications are free at learning scale, but test with email only.

Step 1. Create a resource group. Portal → search "Resource groups" → + Create. Name: rg-monitor-day16. Region: your usual region. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: rg-monitor-day16 appears in the resource group list.

Verify: Refresh; rg-monitor-day16 is listed.

Step 2. Create a small signal-source VM. Portal → search "Virtual machines" → + Create → Azure virtual machine. Resource group: rg-monitor-day16. Name: vm-monitor-16. Image: Ubuntu Server. Size: Standard_B1s. Authentication: password (journal only, never publish). Public inbound ports: None. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The VM deploys and shows Running status.

Verify: VM overview shows Running.

Troubleshooting: Size unavailable: try Standard_B2s.

Step 3. Open Metrics for the VM. Open vm-monitor-16 → left menu Monitoring → Metrics. Scope should already be your VM. Metric: choose "Percentage CPU". Aggregation: Avg. Time range: Last 1 hour. Click Apply (or it auto-applies). Watch the chart.

Expected result: The Metrics blade shows a chart with at least one line (even near zero).

Verify: You see a CPU chart for vm-monitor-16 and can read a value off it.

Troubleshooting: Chart empty/flat at zero: that's normal for an idle VM — a flat line near zero IS data.

Step 4. Look at VM insights. Still in the VM blade → Monitoring → Insights. Browse the tabs (Performance, Map). Notice how much analysis ships prebuilt — this is the "insights" experience for VMs.

Expected result: The Insights view loads with prebuilt charts (CPU, disk, memory may show limited data on a fresh VM).

Verify: You can describe two charts you saw in VM insights.

Troubleshooting: Insights asks to enable: click Enable if offered (uses the free tier of data collection at learning scale).

Step 5. Start an alert rule. Portal top search → "Alerts" → open Monitor Alerts → + Create → Alert rule. Scope: select vm-monitor-16 (Resource groups → rg-monitor-day16 → the VM). Condition tab → See all signals → search "Percentage CPU" → select it.

Expected result: The alert rule wizard opens with your VM pre-selected as the scope.

Verify: The condition pane shows the Percentage CPU signal for vm-monitor-16.

Troubleshooting: Signal list empty: confirm the scope is the VM itself, not the resource group.

Step 6. Set a deliberately low threshold. Operator: Greater than. Threshold value: 1 (percent). Aggregation: Average. Evaluation: check every 1 minute, look back 5 minutes. We use 1% so the rule will fire on an idle VM — this is a TEST threshold, not a production one. Click Next.

Expected result: The condition summary reads like "Greater than 1, Avg Percentage CPU, evaluated every 1 minute over 5 minutes" (your exact numbers may differ).

Verify: You can read the condition back in plain English.

Step 7. Create an action group. Actions tab → + Create action group. Resource group: rg-monitor-day16. Action group name: ag-day16-test. Region: Global. Notification type: Email/SMS message/Push/Voice → Email → enter YOUR email address → OK. Review + create → Create. Then select this action group in the alert wizard.

Expected result: The action group appears in the list with your email shown.

Verify: The alert rule wizard shows ag-day16-test selected under Actions.

Troubleshooting: Email not arriving later: check spam/junk; Azure alert emails sometimes land there the first time.

Step 8. Finish the alert rule. Details tab: name it alert-cpu-test-day16. Severity: 3 (or 4 — this is a test). Description: "Lab test alert — will be deleted." Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The alert rule is listed with status Enabled and severity of your choice.

Verify: Monitor → Alerts → Alert rules lists alert-cpu-test-day16 as Enabled.

Troubleshooting: Create blocked: every alert rule needs at least one action group — go back and attach ag-day16-test.

Step 9. Check the Activity log. Portal → search "Activity log" (or the VM's Activity log blade). Filter to the last hour. Find the entries for creating the VM and the alert rule — WHO did WHAT and WHEN.

Expected result: You can explain in one sentence what the Activity log recorded (e.g. the alert rule creation event).

Verify: You found at least one entry naming your alert rule creation.

Step 10. Clean up. Portal → Resource groups → rg-monitor-day16 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Then open Monitor → Alerts → Alert rules and confirm alert-cpu-test-day16 is gone (if it was in a shared group, delete it manually).

Expected result: The resource group disappears and the alert rule no longer exists.

Verify: No resource group, no VM, no alert rule remains.

Troubleshooting: Stray alert emails after deletion: action groups fire on old evaluations for a short while, then stop.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the resource group rg-monitor-day16: Portal → Resource groups → rg-monitor-day16 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Deleting the VM also stops its metrics flow, so no alert will fire afterward. Also delete the alert rule explicitly if you created it in a shared resource group: Monitor → Alerts → Alert rules → select it → Delete.

Day 17 — Log Analytics Workspace and Introductory KQL60–90 minutes

A. Day 17 — Log Analytics Workspace and Introductory KQL

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what a Log Analytics workspace is and how logs are organized into tables
  • Describe the KQL pipeline model (table → pipe → operator)
  • Write basic KQL queries using take, where, project, summarize, and sort/top
  • Explain ingestion cost and the Analytics vs Basic table plans
  • Run queries against sample data without ingesting anything (no cost)

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account
  • Completion of Day 16 (Azure Monitor, metrics, logs, alerts)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

A Log Analytics workspace is Azure Monitor's data store for logs: one big house where log records from many resources are collected into tables (like Heartbeat, Perf, Usage, SecurityEvent). Think of it as a library: each shelf is a table, each book is a record, and every book has a timestamp shelf-tag called TimeGenerated. You don't browse this library by hand — you query it.

The query language is KQL (Kusto Query Language). KQL reads like a pipeline flowing left to right: start with a table, then each pipe (|) passes the rows to the next operator. SecurityEvent | take 10 means "give me 10 rows from SecurityEvent, just to look at them." Learning KQL is learning a handful of operators well.

The six operators that cover 90% of beginner queries: take (grab a small sample), where (filter rows, e.g. | where TimeGenerated > ago(1h)), project (keep only the columns you want), summarize (group and count/average, the heart of analysis), sort by / top (order results), extend (add a computed column). Case matters in KQL — table and column names must match exactly, and keywords are lowercase.

Cost reality: you pay to ingest (write) data into a workspace and to retain it. Two table plans exist: Analytics logs (full features — alerts, all operators) and Basic logs (much cheaper to ingest, query-only with limits, no alerts). Choosing per-table plans is how real teams control log bills. Always filter by time (ago()) early in a query — scanning a month of data when you need an hour is slow and wasteful.

Portal UI, table names, and pricing change over time — verify against current docs. This workshop is not sponsored, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft.

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Log Analytics workspace overview (Microsoft Learn)Documentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/logs/log-analytics-workspace-overview VerifiedHow workspaces, tables, retention, and access work — the reference for today's concepts20 min
Get started with log queries in Azure Monitor Logs (Microsoft Learn)Documentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/logs/get-started-queries VerifiedMicrosoft's own KQL walkthrough with the demo environment link — follow it alongside today's lab25 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a Log Analytics workspace, learn where its tables live, then practice five KQL queries against Microsoft's demo environment with real sample data.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. Completion of Day 16 (Azure Monitor basics).

Cost note: Free tier: a fresh workspace with no ingestion costs nothing. You ingest NO data in this lab (queries run against Microsoft's demo workspace) — no billable ingestion.

Step 1. Create a resource group. Portal → search "Resource groups" → + Create. Name: rg-loganalytics-day17. Region: your usual region. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: rg-loganalytics-day17 appears in the resource group list.

Verify: Refresh; the group is listed.

Step 2. Create a Log Analytics workspace. Portal → search "Log Analytics workspaces" → + Create. Resource group: rg-loganalytics-day17. Name: law-day17-<randomdigits>. Region: same region. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The workspace deploys; its overview blade shows the workspace name and region.

Verify: Overview blade shows your workspace name and a green provisioning state.

Troubleshooting: Name taken: add more digits — workspace names must be unique per resource group; digits make collisions unlikely.

Step 3. Open the Logs blade and look at the schema. Open your workspace → left menu General → Logs. On the left is the schema pane listing tables (Heartbeat, Perf, Usage, and more). Notice your fresh workspace has tables defined but almost no data yet — that's why the next steps use the demo workspace for queries.

Expected result: The Logs blade opens (possibly with a welcome dialog); the left schema pane lists tables.

Verify: You can name three tables from the schema pane.

Troubleshooting: Logs blade asks about a query mode: choose KQL mode or Simple mode — pick KQL mode for this workshop.

Step 4. Open the demo environment. In a new browser tab, go to the Microsoft Learn "Get started with log queries" article (linked in Resources) and follow its link to the demo environment, or search the portal help for "Log Analytics demo". Sign in with your Azure account if prompted — the demo workspace is read-only sample data.

Expected result: The demo Log Analytics environment opens with sample data available to query.

Verify: The demo Logs blade shows populated tables (e.g. SecurityEvent has data).

Troubleshooting: Demo link hard to find: the article linked in today's Resources contains it; the demo is the standard way to practice without ingesting data.

Step 5. Query 1 — take a sample. In the demo Logs blade, clear the editor and type: SecurityEvent | take 10 — then click Run. This is the universal first move when facing any new table: look at a few rows.

Expected result: Exactly 10 rows are returned from the SecurityEvent table.

Verify: The results grid shows 10 rows with columns like TimeGenerated and Computer.

Troubleshooting: Syntax error highlight: KQL is case-sensitive; the table name must be exactly SecurityEvent and keywords lowercase.

Step 6. Query 2 — filter by time and sort. Type: SecurityEvent | where TimeGenerated > ago(1h) | top 10 by TimeGenerated desc — Run. Notice the pipeline: filter first (cheap), then sort the small remainder.

Expected result: Rows are returned, all within the last hour, newest first.

Verify: All returned TimeGenerated values are within the window you chose.

Troubleshooting: No results: the demo data may be sparse in the last hour; widen to ago(24h).

Step 7. Query 3 — group and count. Type: SecurityEvent | summarize Count = count() by Computer | top 10 by Count desc — Run. summarize collapses thousands of rows into one row per computer — this is the operator that turns raw logs into answers.

Expected result: A two-column result: Computer and a count column, highest counts first.

Verify: Each Computer appears exactly once, with its event count.

Troubleshooting: Column name mismatch: use the exact name shown in the schema pane; in the demo, Computer exists on SecurityEvent.

Step 8. Query 4 — chart performance over time. Type: Perf | where TimeGenerated > ago(6h) | where CounterName == "% Processor Time" | summarize avg(CounterValue) by bin(TimeGenerated, 30m) — Run, then switch the results view to Chart. bin() groups continuous time into 30-minute buckets.

Expected result: Each row shows a timestamp bucket and an average CPU value.

Verify: A line or bar chart of average CPU over time renders.

Troubleshooting: Perf empty in your window: widen to ago(7d); demo data has gaps.

Step 9. Query 5 — find what costs money. Type: Usage | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d) | summarize TotalMB = sum(Quantity) by DataType | top 5 by TotalMB desc — Run. This is the query real teams run to find their most expensive log sources.

Expected result: A small table of data types with their ingestion volumes, biggest first.

Verify: You can name the top data type by volume in the demo.

Step 10. Clean up. Portal → Resource groups → rg-loganalytics-day17 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Confirm deletion.

Expected result: The resource group and workspace are gone.

Verify: Resource groups no longer lists rg-loganalytics-day17.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the resource group rg-loganalytics-day17: Portal → Resource groups → rg-loganalytics-day17 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Confirm the workspace is gone. The demo workspace belongs to Microsoft and needs no cleanup.

G. Practical Assignment

In your journal, write two KQL queries that would answer questions about YOUR Day-16 VM: (1) average CPU per hour over the last day, (2) the 20 most recent heartbeat records. Then run both against the demo workspace and note what the results look like.

  1. Q1. What is a Log Analytics workspace?
    • A) A database server you manage yourself
    • B) A data store (workspace) where logs from many Azure resources are collected and queried
    • C) A single text file on your VM
    • D) The same thing as a storage account
  2. Q2. What does the KQL query `SecurityEvent | take 10` mean?
    • A) SecurityEvent is a column name
    • B) The query returns 10 random rows from SecurityEvent, piped step by step left to right
    • C) The pipe character means OR
    • D) Random is actually fine here — rows come back in no particular order; the core idea is pipeline + limit
  3. Q3. Which KQL operator counts events grouped by computer?
    • A) where Computer == "web01"
    • B) extend Rows = count()
    • C) summarize count() by Computer
    • D) project Computer
  4. Q4. What is the difference between Analytics logs and Basic logs tables?
    • A) Basic logs are encrypted; Analytics logs are not
    • B) Basic logs are cheaper to ingest but can't be used for alerts and have limited features — a per-table cost choice
    • C) Basic logs expire after 1 hour
    • D) Analytics logs must be queried with SQL
  5. Q5. Practical: Write a KQL query that returns the top 3 data types by total ingested volume in the last 24 hours, using the Usage table.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. A Log Analytics workspace is the data store for logs; querying happens in Log Analytics using KQL written in the Logs blade.
  2. Q2 — Answer: D. KQL is read left to right as a pipeline — table, then each | operator transforms the flowing rows. take limits rows to a sample.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. summarize creates groups and aggregates them — count() by Computer gives per-computer totals. where filters rows; project keeps columns; extend adds one.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Basic logs are cheaper to ingest but are query-only (no alerts/analytics features, limited retention). It's a budget choice per table, not a different language.
  5. Q5 — Answer: KQL: Usage | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d) | summarize TotalGB = sum(Quantity) / 1000 by DataType | top 3 by TotalGB desc. Judged on correct pipeline logic (filter time → summarize sum → sort/top), not exact syntax.. Tests pipeline thinking: narrow, aggregate, then rank.

Day 18 — Azure Backup, Recovery, and Resilience Fundamentals60–90 minutes

A. Day 18 — Azure Backup, Recovery, and Resilience Fundamentals

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what Azure Backup does and when to use it
  • Distinguish backup/restore from disaster recovery (Azure Site Recovery)
  • Define RPO and RTO and relate them to backup policy design
  • Enable VM backup to a Recovery Services vault with a daily policy
  • Run an on-demand backup, verify the recovery point, and clean up completely

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account
  • Completion of Days 1–17, especially Days 5–6 (VMs) and Day 16 (monitoring)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Azure Backup is Azure's native backup service: it protects your data and restores it when needed, without you deploying or managing any backup servers. The analogy: the spare key to your house kept at a friend's place. The house (your production VM) can burn down, but the spare key — and a copy of everything you need — survives because it lives somewhere else. That "somewhere else" is the Recovery Services vault, which stores backup data and recovery points and acts as a security boundary isolated from production.

Backup answers the question "can I get my data back?" — it is about backup and restore. Azure Site Recovery answers a different question: "can my service keep running if a whole region goes down?" — that is disaster recovery (failover orchestration of live systems to another region). They are complementary, not substitutes: Backup restores data to a point in time; Site Recovery keeps systems running during a regional disaster.

Two numbers define every recovery plan. RPO (Recovery Point Objective) = how much data loss is acceptable — if backups run daily, your RPO is roughly 24 hours. RTO (Recovery Time Objective) = how fast you must be back online — 30 minutes means your restore procedure must actually be practiced and that fast. Backup policies turn these numbers into schedules: how often backups run and how long recovery points are kept.

Protection against the worst case matters too: soft delete keeps deleted backup data for a retention period, defending against accidental or malicious deletion; immutable backups lock recovery points so they cannot be modified or deleted; RBAC restricts who can touch the vault. The vault's storage replication — LRS (Locally Redundant Storage) (three copies in one region), GRS (Geo-Redundant Storage) (copied to a distant secondary region — the default), ZRS (Zone-Redundant Storage) (spread across zones in the region) — decides how far your backups survive.

Note: backup of a real VM can take a long time (hours) and incurs small storage costs — this lab uses a small VM and on-demand backup; delete the vault promptly. Portal UI, pricing, and vault features change over time. This workshop is not sponsored, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft.

What is Azure Backup? (Microsoft Learn)Documentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/backup/backup-overview VerifiedOfficial overview: when to use Backup, what it protects, vaults, policies, and security features20 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create a Recovery Services vault, enable backup on a small VM with a daily policy, run an on-demand backup, verify the recovery point, then clean up fully.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. Completion of Day 16 (VM creation) — you will create a small VM.

Cost note: A small B1s VM bills per hour while it exists; vault storage for a small VM's recovery points is small but not zero. Run the backup, verify one recovery point, then stop backup and delete the resource group promptly.

Step 1. Create a resource group. Portal → search "Resource groups" → + Create. Name: rg-backup-day18. Region: your usual region (e.g. East US). Review + create → Create.

Expected result: rg-backup-day18 appears in the resource group list.

Verify: Refresh; rg-backup-day18 is listed.

Step 2. Create a small VM to protect. Portal → search "Virtual machines" → + Create → Azure virtual machine. Resource group: rg-backup-day18. Name: vm-backup-18. Image: Ubuntu Server. Size: Standard_B1s. Authentication: password (journal only, never publish). Public inbound ports: None. Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The VM deploys and shows Running status.

Verify: VM overview shows Running.

Troubleshooting: Size unavailable: try Standard_B2s.

Step 3. Create a Recovery Services vault. Portal → search "Recovery Services vaults" → + Create. Resource group: rg-backup-day18. Vault name: rsv-day18-<randomdigits>. Region: same region. Backup storage redundancy: Locally-redundant (LRS) for this lab (cheaper). Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The vault deploys; its overview blade shows the vault name and backup/storage replication settings.

Verify: Vault overview shows the name and LRS storage replication.

Step 4. Start the backup setup. Open the vault → left menu Getting Started → Backup. Where is your workload running? Azure. What do you want to back up? Virtual machine. Click Backup (or Continue). Select vm-backup-18 from the list.

Expected result: The VM appears in the backup configuration list and can be selected.

Verify: vm-backup-18 is selected as the item to protect.

Troubleshooting: VM not listed: it must be in the same subscription; the vault region and VM region may need to match — keep them the same.

Step 5. Choose a backup policy. Select the DefaultPolicy (daily backup, 30-day retention) — or create a custom policy if offered: daily at a set time, retain recovery points for 30 days. Click Enable backup. Wait for the enable-backup job to complete (check the bell Notifications icon).

Expected result: A policy is attached to the VM; backup is "enabled" on the VM.

Verify: Vault → Backup items → Azure Virtual Machine shows vm-backup-18 with protection status (e.g. Healthy or Backup in progress).

Troubleshooting: Enable fails with an extension error: retry once; the backup extension install can take several minutes on first run.

Step 6. Run an on-demand backup. Vault → Backup items → Azure Virtual Machine → select vm-backup-18 → Backup now → set "Retain backup till" 30 days from today → OK. This queues a backup job. Watch it under Monitoring → Backup jobs. The first backup of a VM can take 30+ minutes — start it, work on the assignment, and check back.

Expected result: A "Backup now" job appears in Backup jobs with status Completed after some time.

Verify: Backup jobs shows a completed "Backup" job for vm-backup-18.

Troubleshooting: Job stuck at "In progress" for a long time: normal for a first VM backup; only worry if it fails outright — then check Backup jobs → the failed job's error message.

Step 7. Verify the recovery point. Vault → Backup items → Azure Virtual Machine → select vm-backup-18 → Backup → the Recovery points section shows your completed backup with date and time. This is your spare key — proof you could restore.

Expected result: At least one recovery point with a timestamp is listed.

Verify: You can read out the recovery point's date and time.

Troubleshooting: Recovery points empty though the job completed: wait a few minutes and refresh; the list can lag behind the job.

Step 8. Walk through a restore WITHOUT completing it. Click Restore VM → observe the steps: choose recovery point, choose restore type (Create new virtual machine vs Replace existing). Do NOT click through the final restore — a real restore creates billable resources. Close the blade after studying the options.

Expected result: The Restore blade lists recovery-point types and restore options (e.g. create a new VM) — you understand the path without paying for a real restore.

Verify: You can describe in one sentence the difference between restoring to a new VM vs replacing the existing one.

Step 9. Clean up fully. Vault → Backup items → Azure Virtual Machine → select vm-backup-18 → Stop backup → choose "Delete backup data" → confirm. Then Portal → Resource groups → rg-backup-day18 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Confirm deletion.

Expected result: The resource group and vault are gone; no backup data remains.

Verify: Resource groups no longer lists rg-backup-day18; vault is gone.

Troubleshooting: Delete blocked by soft-delete: stopping backup with "Delete backup data" first is the required order; then retry the resource group deletion.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete the resource group rg-backup-day18: Portal → Resource groups → rg-backup-day18 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. The Recovery Services vault has soft-delete protection: to fully stop it, first stop backup on the VM (vault → Backup items → Azure Virtual Machine → select → Stop backup → Delete backup data), then delete the resource group. Confirm everything is gone.

Check one real thing in your life that has no backup (a phone photo library, a school project, a USB drive). Write the RPO/RTO for it in plain words and the concrete change you will make this week so losing it costs you at most one day.

  4. Q4. A business says "we can lose at most 1 hour of data, and we must be back online within 30 minutes." Which statement uses RPO and RTO correctly?

    • B) RPO is the cost of backup; RTO is the cost of restore
    • C) RPO = how much data loss is acceptable (backup frequency); RTO = how quickly service must be restored (restore speed)
    • D) RPO applies to VMs; RTO applies to databases
  5. Q5. Practical: In your journal, write a one-page backup plan for the Day-14 web app + VM build: choose an RPO and RTO with reasons, a backup policy (frequency + retention), storage replication (LRS/GRS/ZRS), and say which restore path (file, disk, or full VM) each of two scenarios needs: (1) an app config file is corrupted, (2) the whole VM is deleted.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Backup (recovery of data to a point in time) and Site Recovery (failover of running systems to another region) are complementary: Backup restores data, Site Recovery keeps services running during a regional disaster.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. Soft delete holds deleted backup data for a retention period, so accidental or malicious deletion can be reversed. Immutability locks recovery points from being changed at all.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. The Recovery Services vault stores backup data and recovery points and acts as a security boundary — isolated from the production environment so a production incident doesn't take backups with it.
  4. Q4 — Answer: C. RPO is about DATA loss (how recent must the backup be); RTO is about TIME (how fast must you be back). A 1-hour RPO needs frequent backups; a 30-minute RTO needs a fast-tested restore path.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Practical plan, judged on: realistic RPO/RTO numbers with reasons; policy frequency vs RPO (e.g. daily backup supports ~24h RPO); retention aligned to a stated need; LRS vs GRS justified by disaster scope; which restore path each incident uses; note that restore speed is verified by a drill, not assumed.. Tests realistic recovery planning rather than memorized definitions.

Day 19 — Azure Cost Management, Pricing, Budgets, and Cleanup60–90 minutes

A. Day 19 — Azure Cost Management, Pricing, Budgets, and Cleanup

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Read Cost Analysis to find exactly which resource groups and services are costing you money
  • Estimate a monthly bill with the Azure Pricing Calculator before you deploy anything
  • Create a monthly budget with actual-cost and forecasted-cost alerts at your own subscription scope
  • Explain why a budget is an alert, not a spending cap, and why alerts can arrive hours late
  • Delete leftover lab resources so they stop charging you

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completion of Days 1–18, or equivalent portal experience with resource groups and storage accounts

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Think of your Azure bill like an electricity meter, not a rent cheque. Rent is a fixed number you agree to in advance. An electricity meter runs every minute something is plugged in — and you find out the total at the end of the month. Azure works the same way: every resource you leave running adds up, whether you are using it or not. Today's lesson is about reading that meter, estimating it before you switch things on, and setting an alarm for when the meter runs too fast.

Cost Management is the dashboard where you read the meter. Its Cost Analysis view shows spending broken down by resource group, service, region, or tag (a name/value label you attach to resources, which you will master tomorrow on Day 20). The Pricing Calculator is the opposite end: it estimates the monthly cost of a configuration before you create it. Always estimate first, deploy second.

A budget sets a spending target for a scope — a subscription, a resource group, or a service — over a monthly, quarterly, or annual period. Here is the critical fact, and it is the most expensive misunderstanding in Azure: a budget does not stop spending. Crossing 100% of a budget does nothing to your resources. A budget only alerts — it sends email notifications when your actual cost or your forecasted cost passes thresholds you choose, such as 50%, 80%, or 100% of the budget amount.

Two more facts about timing. Cost data typically takes 8–24 hours to show up, and budgets are re-evaluated about every 24 hours. An alert email normally goes out within an hour of the evaluation. So a budget alert is never instant — it can lag a day behind reality. That is why the habit that saves real money is not the budget itself, but cleanup: deleting resource groups you no longer need. A virtual machine you deleted last week can still cost you money if its managed disk or public IP (Internet Protocol) address was left behind — these are called orphaned resources, and Cost Analysis grouped by resource is how you hunt them down.

One last note: portal screens, prices, and free-tier limits change over time. The exact dollar amounts you see today may differ from what is in any screenshot — the steps and the concepts stay the same.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure Pricing CalculatorToolhttps://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/ VerifiedEstimate the monthly cost of a VM, storage account, or database before you create it15 min
Tutorial: Create and manage budgetsDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cost-management-billing/costs/tutorial-acm-create-budgets VerifiedStep-by-step reference for creating budgets, actual vs forecasted alerts, and alert timing20 min
Describe the Microsoft Cost Management toolMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/describe-cost-management-azure/6-describe-azure-tool VerifiedShort training unit on Cost Analysis, cost alerts, and budgets10 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Inspect your real spending in Cost Analysis, estimate a future bill with the Pricing Calculator, and create a budget with alerts at your subscription scope.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. Sign in to portal.azure.com as the subscription owner or contributor.

Cost note: Free. Cost Management, Cost Analysis, budgets, and the Pricing Calculator cost nothing to use. This lab creates no billable resources.

Step 1. In the Azure portal top search bar, type 'Cost Management' and select 'Cost Management + Billing'. On the left menu, select 'Cost Management', then 'Cost analysis'. Set the scope dropdown to your subscription.

Expected result: A chart of your accumulated cost for the current billing period, with totals in your currency.

Verify: You can see a total cost figure and a breakdown chart. If your subscription is brand new, the total may be $0 — that is fine.

Troubleshooting: If Cost Management shows nothing or says data is unavailable, your subscription may be too new — cost data can take up to 48 hours to appear on a brand-new subscription. Continue with the budget steps and come back tomorrow.

Step 2. Above the chart, change 'Group by' from 'Service name' to 'Resource group'. Write down (on paper or in your journal) the two resource groups with the highest cost.

Expected result: The chart re-renders, showing one bar or segment per resource group from your earlier lab days.

Verify: You can read the names of at least two resource groups and their cost shares.

Step 3. Change 'Group by' to 'Resource'. Expand the top items. Look for any resource that belongs to a lab you thought was finished — for example a storage account or disk in a lab resource group you no longer need.

Expected result: Individual resources appear with their cost. This is how you spot orphaned resources like leftover disks.

Verify: You can name one resource you no longer need, if any exist.

Step 4. Open the Azure Pricing Calculator in a new tab (azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator). Add a Virtual Machines product: choose Windows, region East US, B2s size, 1 VM running 730 hours/month. Note the monthly estimate.

Expected result: The calculator shows an estimated monthly price for that VM configuration.

Verify: You have a dollar figure written down for the VM alone, before storage and bandwidth.

Troubleshooting: If the calculator page layout changed, use its search box for 'Virtual Machines' — the estimate logic is the same.

Step 5. Back in the portal, go to 'Subscriptions', select your subscription, and select 'Budgets' in the left menu. Select '+ Add' to create a budget.

Expected result: The 'Create budget' window opens with the subscription pre-selected as the scope.

Verify: The scope shown matches your subscription name.

Troubleshooting: If 'Add' is greyed out, you need contributor-level access on the subscription. Free-account owners have this by default.

Step 6. Name the budget 'workshop-monthly-budget'. Set reset period to 'Monthly'. Set the budget amount to 10 (ten dollars or your currency equivalent — this is a learning budget, not your real limit). Set an expiration date one year in the future. Select 'Next'.

Expected result: You move to the alert configuration page, possibly with a spending graph suggesting a threshold.

Verify: Budget name, Monthly period, amount 10, and a future expiration date are all visible.

Step 7. Under 'Set alerts', create actual-cost alerts at 50%, 80%, and 100% of the budget, all sending email to your own email address. Add one forecasted-cost alert at 100% to your email. Select 'Create'.

Expected result: The budget appears in the Budgets list with 0% or low usage against it.

Verify: Open the budget — you see four alert rules (50/80/100 actual, 100 forecasted) with your email as the recipient.

Troubleshooting: Remember: these alerts notify you. They do not stop or delete anything. If your email provider filters them, add azure-noreply@microsoft.com to your safe senders.

Step 8. Return to Cost Analysis. Open the 'Budgets' list inside Cost Analysis (or the Budgets page) and confirm your new budget is shown against your spending trend.

Expected result: The budget line appears alongside your actual spending trend.

Verify: You can see the budget name and its current usage percentage.

Step 9. Cleanup review: in the portal, search 'Resource groups'. Open each lab resource group from Days 1–18 that you are completely finished with. If it contains no resources you need, delete the resource group: open it, select 'Delete resource group', type the name to confirm, and select Delete.

Expected result: The deleted resource groups disappear from the list within a few minutes.

Verify: The resource group is gone from the Resource groups list. Repeat Cost Analysis tomorrow to confirm its cost stops growing.

Troubleshooting: Deletion can take a few minutes. Do NOT delete a resource group if you are unsure what is inside — open it and read the resource list first.

Step 10. Write in your journal: your current month-to-date total from Cost Analysis, the Pricing Calculator estimate from step 4, and which resource groups you deleted. Keep the budget in place — it will watch your spending for the rest of the workshop.

Expected result: Three facts recorded in your own words.

Verify: You can state your spend, your estimate, and your cleanup actions without re-opening the portal.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Nothing billable was created in this lab. If you want to remove the learning budget later: portal → Subscriptions → your subscription → Budgets → select 'workshop-monthly-budget' → Delete. The resource groups you deleted in step 9 are already gone — deletion is permanent, so only delete groups you have finished with.

G. Practical Assignment

Pick one resource you still have running from an earlier lab day (for example a storage account). Use the Pricing Calculator to estimate its monthly cost, then decide: keep it, stop it, or delete it — and write one sentence explaining your decision.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. Your spending crosses 100% of your Azure budget. What happens to your resources?
    • A) Azure automatically stops all resources in the budget scope until the next month
    • B) Azure deletes the most expensive resources to bring spending back under budget
    • C) Nothing happens to the resources — you only receive alert notifications
    • D) Your subscription is suspended immediately
  2. Q2. Why can a budget alert arrive a day or more after the spending actually happened?
    • A) Budget alerts are sent by physical mail once a month
    • B) Cost and usage data typically takes 8–24 hours to appear, and budgets are re-evaluated about every 24 hours
    • C) Alerts only fire on the first day of each month
    • D) The alert system is paused on weekends
  3. Q3. You want to know what a new virtual machine will cost per month BEFORE you create it. Which tool should you use?
    • A) Cost Analysis
    • B) Azure Advisor
    • C) The Azure Pricing Calculator
    • D) The Budgets page
  4. Q4. You deleted a virtual machine last week, but Cost Analysis grouped by Resource still shows charges from its resource group. What is the most likely cause?
    • A) The budget you created is too low
    • B) Orphaned resources — such as the VM's managed disk or public IP address — were left behind and are still billing
    • C) Cost Analysis shows data from other subscriptions
    • D) Deleted resources keep billing for exactly 30 more days by design
  5. Q5. Open Cost Analysis in your own subscription, group by Resource group, and answer in your own words: which two resource groups cost you the most this month, and name one resource you can delete today to stop future charges.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: C. Budgets are alerts, not spending caps. Crossing a budget threshold only triggers the alert rules you configured (emails, action groups). Resources keep running and charges continue. This is the single most important cost fact in Azure.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Cost data lags reality by 8–24 hours, and budget evaluation happens roughly once a day, with emails normally sent within an hour of the evaluation. Alerts are therefore never instant.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. The Pricing Calculator estimates future cost before deployment. Cost Analysis shows money already spent, budgets watch spending against a target, and Advisor recommends optimizations.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Deleting a VM does not delete its disk, NIC, or public IP. Those orphaned resources keep accruing charges. Grouping Cost Analysis by Resource is how you find them; deleting the whole resource group removes them all.
  5. Q5 — Answer: A correct answer names two real resource groups from the learner's Cost Analysis (for example, a lab group from an earlier day) and identifies at least one deletable resource — such as a leftover storage account, disk, or an entire finished lab resource group — with the reasoning that deleting the group removes all its billable resources at once.. This is a practical check that you can read your own bill and act on it. The habit of grouping by resource group and deleting finished labs is what keeps a free account free.

Day 20 — Azure Policy, Governance, and Resource Tagging60–90 minutes

A. Day 20 — Azure Policy, Governance, and Resource Tagging

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain the difference between a policy definition, an initiative, and an assignment
  • Describe the four assignment scopes and how policies inherit down the hierarchy
  • Assign the built-in 'Allowed locations' policy and observe the Deny effect blocking a deployment
  • Apply standard name/value tags to a resource group and a resource
  • Read the Policy compliance dashboard and distinguish compliant from non-compliant resources

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Day 19 completed (Cost Analysis grouping by tag was introduced there)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Yesterday you learned to read the meter. Today you learn to write the building code. Governance means setting rules so that your Azure environment stays organized, compliant, and cheap — without you having to check everything by hand. Azure Policy is the rule engine; tags are the labels that make the rules useful.

Azure Policy compares every resource against business rules and reports compliance. The vocabulary is four words: a policy definition is one rule written in JSON (JavaScript Object Notation), a machine-readable format; an initiative is a bundle of definitions for one goal (for example, every rule needed for a security standard); an assignment applies a definition or initiative to a scope; and the scope is where the rule applies — a management group (a container for subscriptions), a subscription, a resource group, or a single resource. Rules cascade down: a policy assigned at the subscription applies to every resource group inside it.

Each rule also has an effect — what the policy actually does when it evaluates a resource. The common effects: Deny blocks the deployment, Audit allows it but flags it as non-compliant in the dashboard, Append adds properties (like tags) to the resource, and DeployIfNotExists creates something missing. Important subtlety: Deny only blocks new or updated resources. It never deletes existing ones — those simply show as non-compliant until you fix them.

Tags are name/value pairs — sticky notes for resources — such as environment=production or owner=siva. You can attach up to 50 tags to a resource, resource group, or subscription. Tags power the cost reports from Day 19: group Cost Analysis by tag and you can report spending per project or per team. Two honest limitations to remember: a tag you add today does not re-label yesterday's costs, and a resource does not automatically inherit its resource group's tags — but Azure Policy can enforce tagging and even inherit tags from a resource group down to its resources.

Portal screens and policy names can change over time. If a built-in policy name in this lab looks slightly different in your portal, use the Policy search box to find the closest match.

E. Learning Resources

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Assign the built-in 'Allowed locations' policy to a lab resource group, watch it block a wrong-region deployment, tag resources, and read the compliance dashboard.

Prerequisites: Azure free account with owner or contributor access on the subscription.

Cost note: Free. Policy assignments and tagging cost nothing. The storage account in this lab uses the cheapest SKU and is deleted in cleanup — expected cost is $0.

Step 1. In the portal, search 'Resource groups' → '+ Create'. Create a resource group named rg-policy-lab in region East US. Leave tags empty for now. Select 'Review + create', then 'Create'.

Expected result: A new empty resource group rg-policy-lab in East US appears in the Resource groups list.

Verify: rg-policy-lab appears in the Resource groups list with Location 'East US'.

Step 2. In the portal top search bar, type 'Policy' and select the 'Policy' service. Take one minute to look at the layout: 'Compliance' on the left is the dashboard you will check later.

Expected result: The Policy service page opens showing Compliance, Authoring, and Remediation sections.

Verify: You are on the Policy page and can see 'Compliance' in the left menu.

Step 3. Select 'Assignments' in the left menu, then '+ Assign policy' at the top. Confirm the Scope shows your subscription and the resource group rg-policy-lab. If not, open the scope picker and select rg-policy-lab.

Expected result: The assignment form opens with Scope already set to your resource group.

Verify: Scope reads like '/subscriptions/.../resourceGroups/rg-policy-lab'.

Step 4. Under 'Policy definition', select the browse button (…). In the search box type 'Allowed locations' and select the built-in definition 'Allowed locations'. Name the assignment 'policy-lab-allowed-locations'.

Expected result: The policy appears selected with its parameters below.

Verify: The definition field shows 'Allowed locations'.

Troubleshooting: If you see several similar names, pick the plain 'Allowed locations' definition. Built-in definition names can change slightly over time.

Step 5. Select the 'Parameters' tab. Check 'Only show parameters that need input'. Set 'listOfAllowedLocations' to East US only (remove any others). Leave the effect as the default. Select 'Review + create', then 'Create'.

Expected result: The parameter list shows the effect and an empty locations list.

Verify: The assignment 'policy-lab-allowed-locations' appears in the Assignments list for scope rg-policy-lab.

Step 6. Wait 2–3 minutes for the assignment to take effect. Then try to break the rule: create a storage account inside rg-policy-lab but choose region West US (not East US). Attempt to create it.

Expected result: You should see an error mentioning the policy, e.g. 'RequestDisallowedByPolicy'. This is the policy doing its job — a failed deployment is the success signal here.

Verify: The creation is blocked with a policy denial error.

Troubleshooting: If the deployment succeeds anyway, the assignment may not have propagated yet — wait 5 more minutes and retry. Do not proceed until you see the denial.

Step 7. Now follow the rule: create the storage account again in rg-policy-lab, this time in East US, with a globally unique lowercase name starting with 'policylab' and SKU Standard_LRS. Create it.

Expected result: The storage account is created successfully in East US.

Verify: The storage account appears inside rg-policy-lab.

Troubleshooting: If the name is taken, add random digits — storage account names must be globally unique, 3–24 lowercase letters and numbers.

Step 8. Tag both levels. Open rg-policy-lab → 'Tags' in the left menu → add environment=learning and owner=<your name> → Apply. Then open the storage account → 'Tags' → add the same two tags → Apply.

Expected result: Tags are saved and shown on both the resource group and the storage account overviews.

Verify: The Tags blade of each shows both name/value pairs.

Step 9. Go back to Policy → 'Compliance'. Select your subscription scope if needed, and open the assignment 'policy-lab-allowed-locations'. Read the compliant vs non-compliant counts.

Expected result: The compliance page lists your assignment and shows 1 compliant resource (the East US storage account) and 0 non-compliant.

Verify: You can state the compliant and non-compliant counts for your assignment.

Troubleshooting: Compliance data can take 15–30 minutes to refresh. If the counts look empty, wait and refresh.

Step 10. Search 'Tags' in the portal top search bar and open the 'Tags' service. Select the tag name 'environment' and then the value 'learning' to see every resource carrying that tag.

Expected result: The tag name and its resources are listed.

Verify: Your resource group and storage account both appear under environment=learning.

Cleanup (prevent charges): 1. Portal → search 'Policy' → 'Assignments'. Open the assignment named 'policy-lab-allowed-locations', select 'Delete assignment', confirm. 2. Portal → 'Resource groups' → open 'rg-policy-lab' → 'Delete resource group', type rg-policy-lab to confirm, select Delete. This removes the assignment, the storage account, and the resource group. Confirm the group is gone from the list.

G. Practical Assignment

In the portal, list three built-in policy definitions whose names contain 'tag' (use the Policy search box). For each, write one sentence saying what it enforces and whether its default effect is Audit, Deny, or something else.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. A colleague says Azure Policy and Azure RBAC do the same job. How do you correct them?
    • A) They are the same thing with different names
    • B) Policy controls who can sign in; RBAC controls resource rules
    • C) RBAC is free and Policy is a paid feature
    • D) RBAC controls who is allowed to act; Policy controls what rules resources must follow
  2. Q2. You assign the built-in 'Allowed locations' policy with the Deny effect at subscription scope, allowing only eastus. There are already VMs in westus. What happens?
    • A) All existing resources outside eastus are deleted immediately
    • B) Existing resources are flagged non-compliant, but only new or updated resources are blocked
    • C) The subscription is moved to eastus automatically
    • D) Existing resources keep running with no compliance impact at all
  3. Q3. What is an Azure Policy initiative?
    • A) A bundle of policy definitions grouped for one compliance goal, assigned as a single unit
    • B) A single policy definition written in JSON
    • C) A scope where policies are assigned, such as a subscription
    • D) A type of budget alert for policy violations
  4. Q4. Which statement about Azure tags is true?
    • A) Tags are automatic system labels you cannot change
    • B) A tag can only be a single word with no value
    • C) Tags are name/value pairs you attach to resources, used for cost grouping and organization
    • D) Tags only work on virtual machines
  5. Q5. In your own subscription, tag a storage account with costcenter=1001, then describe in your own words where that tag appears in the portal and how you would use it to report costs for cost center 1001.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: D. RBAC answers 'who can do what' (permissions); Policy answers 'what rules must resources follow' (compliance). One controls people, the other controls resources.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Deny applies to new deployments and updates — the portal blocks the request. Existing non-compliant resources are flagged in the compliance dashboard, never deleted. Policy never destroys what already exists.
  3. Q3 — Answer: A. An initiative bundles multiple policy definitions under one assignment — for example a regulatory standard like ISO 27001. A single definition is one rule; a scope is where an assignment applies; an alert is a notification, not a policy concept.
  4. Q4 — Answer: C. Tags are arbitrary name/value pairs (up to 50 per resource) you attach to resources, resource groups, or subscriptions. They are the standard way to group costs by project, team, or environment in Cost Analysis.
  5. Q5 — Answer: A correct answer shows the tag name costcenter=1001 on the storage account's Tags blade, plus the description that tags appear under the tag name in the portal 'Tags' service and become a 'Group by: Tag' option in Cost Analysis once cost data refreshes.. This proves you can apply a real tag in the portal and know where tags surface: the resource overview, the Tags service, and Cost Analysis grouping.

Day 21 — Azure CLI and PowerShell Hands-On Automation60–90 minutes

A. Day 21 — Azure CLI and PowerShell Hands-On Automation

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Open Azure Cloud Shell and confirm your signed-in subscription with az account show
  • Create a resource group and a storage account using Azure CLI commands
  • Switch Cloud Shell to PowerShell and manage the same resources with Az cmdlets
  • Explain when to prefer the CLI (JSON output, cross-platform scripts) vs PowerShell (objects, Verb-Noun cmdlets)
  • Delete a resource group from the terminal and verify it is gone

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Days 1–20 completed, or comfort with resource groups and storage accounts in the portal

D. Concepts Explained Simply

So far you have driven Azure with a steering wheel — the portal, clicking one screen at a time. Today you switch to autopilot scripts. Automation means writing instructions once and running them a hundred times, identically. When you can create ten identical environments with one command, you stop making the small manual mistakes that cause outages and surprise bills.

Azure gives you two command-line tools that both talk to the same engine, Azure Resource Manager (ARM), the management layer that creates, updates, and deletes everything. The Azure CLI (Command-Line Interface) is a cross-platform tool with short commands like az group create; it prints JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) text that is easy to read and filter. Azure PowerShell is a set of cmdlets (pronounced 'command-lets', small single-purpose commands) with a Verb-Noun pattern like New-AzResourceGroup; it returns .NET objects you can filter, sort, and pipe into other commands.

You do not need to install anything today. Azure Cloud Shell is a browser-based terminal built into the portal: it comes pre-authenticated to your subscription with the CLI, PowerShell, Bicep, and Git already installed, and it keeps a small persistent storage drive for your files. The first time you open it, it asks you to create a storage account for that drive — that storage is part of the free tier and costs essentially nothing.

One safety rule before you type anything that creates or deletes: always check where you are signed in first — az account show in the CLI or Get-AzContext in PowerShell. And never paste passwords or keys into scripts or chat windows; the workshop's standing rule is interactive sign-in and Key Vault patterns only. Portal screens and CLI versions change over time — if a command's output looks slightly different, the parameters and ideas are the same.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Install the Azure CLIDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/install-azure-cli VerifiedInstall the CLI on your own computer later so you can script without the portal15 min
What is Azure Cloud ShellDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-shell/overview VerifiedHow Cloud Shell works, its persistent storage, and Bash vs PowerShell environments10 min
Install Azure PowerShell on WindowsDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/azure/install-azps-windows?view=azps-14.1.0 VerifiedInstall the Az module locally with Install-Module when you outgrow Cloud Shell15 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Create and delete a resource group and a storage account using Azure CLI in Cloud Shell Bash, then read the same resources with Az PowerShell in Cloud Shell.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. A modern browser — no local installs needed.

Cost note: Tiny. A Standard_LRS storage account existing for under an hour costs a fraction of a cent; the free credit covers it. Cloud Shell's own storage is free-tier. Everything is deleted in cleanup.

Step 1. In the portal, select the Cloud Shell icon (>_) in the top toolbar. When asked, choose 'Bash'. If this is your first time, accept the prompt to create a storage account for Cloud Shell persistence.

Expected result: A terminal panel opens at the bottom of the portal. First launch asks you to create a storage account for Cloud Shell files — accept the defaults.

Verify: You see a Bash prompt like 'user@Azure:~$' in the terminal.

Troubleshooting: If Cloud Shell fails to start, sign out and back in to the portal, then retry. It needs a fresh authentication token.

Step 2. Run 'az version' to confirm the CLI works, then run 'az account show'. Read the 'name' and 'id' fields — this is the subscription every command below will act on.

Expected result: Version info and your subscription details: name, id, tenantId, and 'state: Enabled'.

Verify: The subscription name matches your free Azure account.

Troubleshooting: If you have multiple subscriptions, run 'az account set --subscription <subscription-id>' with the ID from the output to select your free subscription explicitly.

Step 3. Create a lab resource group: az group create --name rg-cli-lab --location eastus. The --name flag sets the name; --location sets the Azure region.

Expected result: JSON output showing the new group with provisioningState 'Succeeded'.

Verify: The output's provisioningState is 'Succeeded'.

Troubleshooting: Names must be 1–90 characters. If you get an authorization error, your sign-in expired — run 'az login' and follow the browser prompts.

Step 4. Run 'az group list --output table' to see all your resource groups as a simple table. Notice --output table changes the JSON into readable columns.

Expected result: A table listing rg-cli-lab and any other groups in your subscription.

Verify: rg-cli-lab appears as a row in the table.

Step 5. Create a storage account: az storage account create --name clilab<random-digits> --resource-group rg-cli-lab --location eastus --sku Standard_LRS. Replace <random-digits> with 6 digits (for example clilab482913). Storage names must be globally unique, 3–24 lowercase letters and numbers.

Expected result: JSON output ending with provisioningState 'Succeeded'. The storage account now exists.

Verify: The command completes with provisioningState 'Succeeded'.

Troubleshooting: 'StorageAccountAlreadyTaken' means someone else has that name — change the digits and retry. Do not reuse a name from an earlier lab.

Step 6. Read it back: az storage account show --name clilab<your-digits> --resource-group rg-cli-lab --output table. This proves create and read both work from the terminal.

Expected result: The account details print, confirming the CLI created it.

Verify: You see the storage account's name, location, and SKU in the table.

Step 7. Switch languages: in the Cloud Shell toolbar, change the environment dropdown from 'Bash' to 'PowerShell'. Confirm the restart when prompted. You are still signed in to the same subscription.

Expected result: The terminal restarts in PowerShell mode with a 'PS' prompt.

Verify: The prompt changes to 'PS /home/user>'.

Step 8. Run 'Get-AzContext' to see your current Azure context, then run 'Get-AzResourceGroup' to list resource groups — the PowerShell twin of 'az group list'.

Expected result: Your subscription context prints, matching what 'az account show' showed in Bash.

Verify: rg-cli-lab appears in the Get-AzResourceGroup output.

Troubleshooting: If Get-AzContext shows a different subscription, run 'Set-AzContext -SubscriptionId <your-id>'.

Step 9. Read the CLI-created storage account with PowerShell: Get-AzStorageAccount -ResourceGroupName rg-cli-lab. Same resource, different tool — that is the whole point of today.

Expected result: The storage account details print, showing the account the CLI created.

Verify: Your clilab storage account appears in the output.

Troubleshooting: Cmdlet names are Verb-Noun with the Az prefix: Get-AzStorageAccount, not Get-StorageAccount.

Step 10. Switch back to Bash in the Cloud Shell dropdown. Delete everything with one command: az group delete --name rg-cli-lab --yes. Deleting the group deletes the storage account inside it.

Expected result: No output — in Bash, a successful delete is silent.

Verify: Run 'az group list --output table' and confirm rg-cli-lab is no longer listed.

Troubleshooting: The command may take a minute. Do not close Cloud Shell until the prompt returns.

Cleanup (prevent charges): In Cloud Shell Bash, run: az group delete --name rg-cli-lab --yes. Wait for it to finish, then run 'az group list --output table' and confirm rg-cli-lab is gone. The storage account inside it is deleted automatically with the group. No local files need cleanup — Cloud Shell keeps only the shell storage account.

G. Practical Assignment

In Cloud Shell Bash, run 'az account list --output table' and write down how many subscriptions you have and which one is marked as the default (IsDefault=true). Then run 'az account set --subscription' with your subscription ID to practice selecting it explicitly.

  1. Q1. Before running a script that creates resources, which Azure CLI command tells you exactly which subscription you are signed in to?
