    • B) az group create
    • C) Create-AzResourceGroup
    • D) New-ResourceGroup
  3. Q3. What is the practical difference between Azure CLI output and Az PowerShell output?
    • A) The CLI only works on Linux and PowerShell only on Windows
    • B) The CLI is free but PowerShell requires a license
    • C) The CLI returns JSON text; PowerShell returns objects you can filter and pipe
    • D) They manage completely different sets of Azure resources
  4. Q4. Why does this workshop use Azure Cloud Shell for the automation labs?
    • A) It is pre-authenticated and has the CLI, PowerShell, Bicep, and Git already installed
    • B) It is the only place Azure CLI commands work
    • C) It automatically deletes resources after one hour
    • D) It gives you a free virtual machine with admin access
  5. Q5. Write the exact command (your choice: Azure CLI or Az PowerShell) that lists all resource groups in your subscription in a simple table, and say which tool you used.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. 'az account show' prints the subscription you are currently signed in to. Running it before any script that creates or deletes resources is the cheapest safety habit in cloud automation.
  2. Q2 — Answer: A. Az PowerShell cmdlets follow the Verb-Noun pattern: New-AzResourceGroup creates a resource group. Get- reads, Remove- deletes, Set- updates.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. The CLI prints JSON text you parse with queries; PowerShell returns .NET objects you can filter and pipe (Get-AzResourceGroup | Where-Object). Same Azure underneath, different handling on your side.
  4. Q4 — Answer: A. Cloud Shell runs in the browser with the CLI, PowerShell, Bicep, Git, and your credentials pre-installed — nothing to install locally. It is the fastest zero-setup path for this workshop.
  5. Q5 — Answer: CLI: az group list --output table. PowerShell: Get-AzResourceGroup (or Get-AzResourceGroup | Format-Table Name, Location). Both commands list the same resource groups, proving the two tools manage the same Azure.. The point of the question is that both tools talk to the same Azure Resource Manager — the tool is a choice, the cloud is the same.

Week 3 covered Key Vault, monitoring and KQL, backup, cost management, policy, and the Azure CLI. Ten questions to close out the week.

    • D) Backup files

    • B) Protect VMs, databases, and file shares and restore them to a point in time

Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Key Vault is the managed vault for passwords, API keys, connection strings, certificates, and encryption keys, with full audit logging.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. The managed identity is an Entra ID identity tied to the Azure resource. Azure handles the credential automatically, so there is nothing to leak or rotate.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Metrics are numbers over time (CPU, request count, disk IOPS). They are cheap to store and ideal for charts and threshold alerts. Logs carry the details.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. KQL is the read-only query language for Log Analytics and Application Insights. You write queries like: requests | where success == false.
  5. Q5 — Answer: B. where keeps only rows matching a condition. summarize aggregates, project picks columns, take returns the first N rows.
  6. Q6 — Answer: B. Azure Backup stores recovery points in a Recovery Services vault so you can restore files, disks, databases, or whole VMs after deletion, corruption, or ransomware.
  7. Q7 — Answer: B. False. Budgets send alerts (email or action group) when thresholds are crossed, but they do NOT stop resources or cap spending. This is the single most misunderstood cost concept.
  8. Q8 — Answer: A. Policy evaluates resources against rules (allowed SKUs, required tags, allowed locations) and can deny non-compliant deployments. RBAC controls WHO can act; Policy controls WHAT is allowed.
  9. Q9 — Answer: B. az group list returns every resource group. az group show needs a --name. az account list shows subscriptions, not resource groups.
  10. Q10 — Answer: B. Tags are name/value labels (for example, env=prod, owner=team-a). Cost Management can group spending by tag, which is how teams answer who spent what.
Day 22 — Infrastructure as Code Concepts and Introduction to Bicep60–90 minutes

A. Day 22 — Infrastructure as Code Concepts and Introduction to Bicep

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Define Infrastructure as Code and explain declarative vs imperative approaches
  • Describe what Bicep is, what a DSL is, and how Bicep relates to ARM templates
  • Read a Bicep file and identify params, resources, and outputs
  • Compile a Bicep file to ARM JSON with az bicep build
  • Validate a Bicep template against Azure without deploying anything

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Day 21 completed — you are comfortable in Cloud Shell Bash

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Imagine you build furniture two ways. The imperative way is a friend on the phone telling you each step: 'pick up screw A, turn it clockwise, now pick up panel B…' — if you miss one step, the chair wobbles. The declarative way is handing a factory a blueprint: 'a chair, oak, these dimensions.' The factory figures out the steps, and every chair comes out identical. Infrastructure as Code (IaC) is the blueprint approach for cloud resources: you write a file describing what you want, and the cloud builds it the same way every time.

Why does this matter? Three reasons. Repeatability: the tenth deployment looks exactly like the first. Reviewability: a blueprint is a file, so teammates can review it like code before anything is built. Recoverability: if a resource is accidentally deleted, you redeploy the same file and get it back. Manual clicking in the portal gives you none of these.

Bicep is Microsoft's IaC language for Azure — a DSL (Domain-Specific Language), meaning a small language built for one job: describing Azure resources. It uses declarative syntax. A Bicep file has params (values supplied at deployment time, like a name), vars (values computed inside the file), resources (the Azure things to create, each with a type like Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts and an API version), and outputs (values the deployment hands back to you, like a resource ID). Behind the scenes, Bicep compiles — transpiles — into a standard ARM (Azure Resource Manager) template in JSON, which is what Azure actually executes.

Two habits start today and never stop: keep infrastructure files in version control (Git — Day 24), and validate before you deploy. Today you only author and validate a Bicep file — nothing is deployed to Azure, so today costs nothing. Tomorrow, on Day 23, you deploy it for real.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Bicep documentationDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/bicep/ VerifiedThe hub page: what Bicep is, install guides, authoring references, and deployment how-tos20 min
Fundamentals of Bicep learning pathMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/fundamentals-bicep/ VerifiedFree Microsoft Learn path that goes deeper: parameters, conditions, loops, and modules60 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Install the Bicep CLI, write a Bicep file that declares a storage account, compile it to ARM JSON, and validate it against a resource group without deploying.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. Cloud Shell Bash from Day 21 — Bicep CLI installs on demand through the Azure CLI.

Cost note: $0. This lab only compiles files locally in Cloud Shell. The validate step checks the template against Azure without creating any resources.

Step 1. Open Cloud Shell in Bash. Check the Bicep tooling: run 'az bicep version'. If it is not installed, run 'az bicep install' and then check the version again.

Expected result: The Bicep version prints, e.g. 'Bicep CLI version 0.x.x'.

Verify: A Bicep CLI version number is displayed.

Troubleshooting: If the install is slow, wait — it downloads the Bicep binary on first use only.

Step 2. Create a working folder: mkdir bicep-lab && cd bicep-lab. Everything for Days 22–23 lives here.

Expected result: A folder named bicep-lab exists in your Cloud Shell home directory.

Verify: Running 'pwd' shows a path ending in bicep-lab.

Step 3. Open the editor: run 'code main.bicep'. Cloud Shell opens an in-browser editor for the new file.

Expected result: A file named main.bicep opens in the Cloud Shell editor.

Verify: You see an empty editor tab titled main.bicep.

Troubleshooting: If 'code' does nothing, type it once more after a few seconds — the editor sometimes needs a moment to load.

Step 4. Type (do not paste blindly — read each line) the following Bicep file, then save (Ctrl+S or the editor's save): param location string = resourceGroup().location param storageAccountName string = 'st${uniqueString(resourceGroup().id)}' resource storageAccount 'Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts@2023-05-01' = { name: storageAccountName location: location sku: { name: 'Standard_LRS' } kind: 'StorageV2' } output storageAccountId string = storageAccount.id Read it once more: two params, one resource (a storage account), one output (its ID).

Expected result: The file saves with the param, resource, and output blocks.

Verify: The file contains the words param, resource, and output.

Troubleshooting: Bicep is indentation-sensitive in blocks. If you get a syntax error later, compare your braces { } line by line.

Step 5. Compile the file: az bicep build --file main.bicep. This transpiles your Bicep into ARM JSON locally. Nothing is sent to Azure.

Expected result: A new file main.json appears in the folder — this is the compiled ARM template.

Verify: 'ls' shows both main.bicep and main.json.

Troubleshooting: A syntax error here points at a line number — fix the Bicep and rebuild.

Step 6. Open main.json and search for 'Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts'. Compare the JSON block with your Bicep resource block — same information, far more verbose. This is why Bicep exists.

Expected result: You find a 'resources' array containing one entry of type Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts.

Verify: You can point to the storage account resource inside the JSON.

Step 7. Validate without deploying. First create an empty group: az group create --name rg-bicep-lab --location eastus. Then run: az deployment group validate --resource-group rg-bicep-lab --template-file main.bicep. Validation checks the template against Azure rules but creates nothing.

Expected result: The validate command returns without errors (it may print warnings about the unused location param default — that is fine).

Verify: The validate command completes with no error output.

Troubleshooting: 'ResourceGroupNotFound' means the group creation did not finish — wait a few seconds and retry the validate command.

Step 8. Experiment safely: change the sku name in main.bicep from 'Standard_LRS' to 'Standard_GRS', rebuild, and notice the JSON changes. Then change it back to 'Standard_LRS' and rebuild once more. You are practicing the edit-build loop with zero risk.

Expected result: 'az bicep build' shows no errors, and you can explain what changed in main.json.

Verify: main.json reflects the SKU you currently have in main.bicep.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Nothing was deployed to Azure in this lab, so there is nothing billable to clean up. If you created rg-bicep-lab for the validate step, delete it: az group delete --name rg-bicep-lab --yes. Your main.bicep and main.json files remain in Cloud Shell storage — keep main.bicep, you will deploy it on Day 23.

G. Practical Assignment

Modify your main.bicep: add a second parameter 'skuName' with default 'Standard_LRS', and use it in the resource's sku block instead of the hard-coded value. Rebuild with 'az bicep build' and confirm the JSON changed. (No deployment — this stays free.)

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What does 'declarative' mean in Infrastructure as Code?
    • A) You declare each step in order, one command at a time
    • B) You describe the desired end state and let the engine figure out the steps
    • C) You declare your budget before deploying anything
    • D) It means the code is written in a compiled language like C#
  2. Q2. When you deploy a Bicep file, what does it get compiled into behind the scenes?
    • A) Python bytecode
    • B) A SQL database script
    • C) A standard ARM template in JSON
    • D) A Docker container image
  3. Q3. In a Bicep file, what is the difference between a param and a var?
    • A) Parameters are secret and variables are public
    • B) Parameters are supplied at deployment time; variables are computed inside the file
    • C) They are identical — two names for the same thing
    • D) Variables can only hold numbers
  4. Q4. What does 'az bicep build --file main.bicep' do?
    • A) It deploys the file to Azure immediately
    • B) It deletes the resource group
    • C) It uploads the file to a storage account
    • D) It compiles the .bicep file to ARM JSON locally without touching Azure
  5. Q5. A teammate sends you this Bicep snippet: param storageName string resource st 'Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts@2023-05-01' = { name: storageName location: 'eastus' sku: { name: 'Standard_LRS' } kind: 'StorageV2' } In your own words: what resource type does it declare, which value must you supply at deployment time, and how many resources will exist in Azure after it deploys?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Declarative IaC describes the desired end state ('I want a storage account with these settings') and lets the engine figure out the steps. Imperative scripts list the steps ('run this, then that'). Bicep and ARM templates are declarative; CLI scripts are imperative.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. Bicep files compile (transpile) to standard ARM template JSON, which is what Azure Resource Manager actually executes. Bicep is the friendlier authoring language on top of the same engine.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. A parameter is supplied at deployment time (with defaults and descriptions); a variable is computed inside the file from other values. Parameters make templates reusable across environments; variables keep the file tidy.
  4. Q4 — Answer: D. 'az bicep build' only compiles the .bicep file to .json locally — it creates nothing in Azure. It is the safe, zero-cost way to check your syntax before you ever deploy.
  5. Q5 — Answer: The answer names the resource type 'Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts', the parameter storageName (or notes that storageName is the parameter controlling the name), and states that the deployment creates exactly one storage account in the target resource group — nothing else.. Reading a template means identifying three things: which resource types it declares, which values are parameterized, and what will exist after deployment. That is the skill this practical question checks.

Day 23 — Deploy Azure Resources Using a Bicep Template60–90 minutes

A. Day 23 — Deploy Azure Resources Using a Bicep Template

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain what a deployment is and what the what-if operation previews
  • Deploy a Bicep file to a resource group with az deployment group create
  • Pass values to parameters and read deployment outputs
  • Redeploy an edited template and explain why the resource is updated, not duplicated
  • Describe Incremental vs Complete deployment modes and why this workshop uses Incremental

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Day 22 completed — main.bicep exists in Cloud Shell ~/bicep-lab

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Yesterday you wrote the blueprint and checked it without building anything. Today you hand the blueprint to the factory. A deployment is one execution of a template against Azure: Azure Resource Manager reads your Bicep file (compiled to an ARM template), compares it with what already exists, and makes reality match the file.

Before any real deployment, professionals run a rehearsal called what-if. It lists every change the deployment would make — which resources would be created, modified, or deleted — without changing anything. Think of it as the 'preview changes' button before you hit 'publish'. If the what-if shows a Delete you did not expect, you stop and fix the template.

Deployments are idempotent: running the same template twice does not create two of everything. ARM identifies each resource by its type and name, so the second run simply confirms 'already correct' and changes nothing. That is why editing a template and redeploying is the normal way to update infrastructure — you never hand-edit the resource in the portal and hope it matches the file.

Deployments run in a mode. The default is Incremental: create or update what is in the template, leave everything else in the resource group alone. Complete mode deletes anything in the resource group that is not in the template — powerful, and dangerous if you did not mean it. This workshop uses Incremental only.

The lab deploys one storage account — the same file from Day 22 — and deletes it at the end. Portal screens, prices, and CLI output formats can change over time; the commands and concepts stay the same.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Fundamentals of Bicep learning pathMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/fundamentals-bicep/ VerifiedDeeper Bicep skills after today: parameters, conditions, loops, modules60 min
Quickstart: Deploy a container instance in Azure using BicepDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-instances/container-instances-quickstart-bicep VerifiedA second real example of the full Bicep workflow: review file, deploy with CLI, verify, clean up20 min
Bicep documentationDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/bicep/ VerifiedReference for deployment options: Azure CLI, PowerShell, Cloud Shell, and what-if15 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Preview with what-if, deploy a Bicep template that creates a storage account, verify outputs in the portal, redeploy a change, and delete the resource group.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. main.bicep from Day 22 in Cloud Shell (~/bicep-lab). If you lost it, recreate it from the file printed in step 2.

Cost note: Tiny. A Standard_LRS StorageV2 account existing for under an hour costs a fraction of a cent, covered by the free credit. Deleted in cleanup.

Step 1. Open Cloud Shell in Bash and confirm your context: run 'az account show' and check the subscription name. Then run 'cd ~/bicep-lab && ls' and confirm main.bicep is there.

Expected result: You see your subscription details, and the prompt returns to ~/bicep-lab with main.bicep present.

Verify: 'az account show' names your free subscription and main.bicep is listed.

Troubleshooting: If main.bicep is missing, recreate it with 'code main.bicep' using the file from Day 22, step 4 (two params, one storage account resource, one output).

Step 2. Create the deployment target: az group create --name rg-bicep-deploy --location eastus. Deployments at 'group' scope always need a resource group to deploy into.

Expected result: JSON output with provisioningState 'Succeeded' for the empty group.

Verify: The group appears in 'az group list --output table'.

Step 3. Rehearse first: az deployment group what-if --resource-group rg-bicep-deploy --template-file main.bicep. Read the summary carefully — every change should be a Create, nothing should be a Delete.

Expected result: A what-if summary listing one planned change: Create Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/<your-name>. No Delete operations.

Verify: The what-if output shows exactly one Create and zero Deletes.

Troubleshooting: If what-if reports errors, fix main.bicep and rebuild with 'az bicep build' first (Day 22, step 5).

Step 4. Deploy for real: az deployment group create --name day23-deploy --resource-group rg-bicep-deploy --template-file main.bicep. The --name flag names the deployment record (visible in the portal under Deployments).

Expected result: Long JSON output ending with 'provisioningState': 'Succeeded'.

Verify: provisioningState is 'Succeeded' in the output.

Troubleshooting: 'StorageAccountAlreadyTaken': the generated name collided — edit the storageAccountName default to add digits, rebuild, and retry. 'InvalidTemplateDeployment': read the inner error message, it names the bad property.

Step 5. Find the outputs: in the deployment JSON, search for 'outputs'. Note the storageAccountId value — this is the output block from your Bicep file coming back to you.

Expected result: A storage account ID path like /subscriptions/.../storageAccounts/st....

Verify: You can copy the storage account ID from the deployment output.

Troubleshooting: If outputs are empty, your Bicep output block may be missing — add 'output storageAccountId string = storageAccount.id', rebuild, redeploy.

Step 6. Verify in the portal: search 'Resource groups' → open rg-bicep-deploy → select the storage account. Confirm location, SKU, and kind match what the template declared.

Expected result: The storage account page shows East US, Standard_LRS, StorageV2 kind — matching your Bicep file exactly.

Verify: Portal values match the Bicep file's declared values.

Step 7. In rg-bicep-deploy, select 'Deployments' in the left menu and open 'day23-deploy'. This is Azure's receipt: template used, parameters, duration, and outputs.

Expected result: The deployment appears with status Succeeded and timestamp.

Verify: You can open the deployment and see its inputs and outputs.

Step 8. Make a safe change: in main.bicep, add a tags block to the storage account resource: tags: { environment: 'learning' }. Rebuild ('az bicep build --file main.bicep'), run what-if again — confirm it shows a Modify — then redeploy with the same create command.

Expected result: What-if shows Modify (not Create, not Delete) on the storage account. The redeploy succeeds.

Verify: The portal storage account now shows the tag environment=learning, and there is still exactly one storage account.

Troubleshooting: If what-if shows Delete, stop: you likely changed the resource name. Restore the name and retry.

Step 9. Cleanup: delete the whole lab with one command: az group delete --name rg-bicep-deploy --yes. Wait for the prompt to return, then verify with 'az group list --output table'.

Expected result: The resource group disappears from the portal list within a few minutes.

Verify: rg-bicep-deploy no longer appears in the group list.

Troubleshooting: Deletion takes a minute or two. Do not start Day 24's Git work until the group is confirmed gone.

Cleanup (prevent charges): In Cloud Shell Bash, run: az group delete --name rg-bicep-deploy --yes. Wait for the prompt to return, then run 'az group list --output table' and confirm rg-bicep-deploy is gone. The storage account, the deployments history, and everything else are deleted with the group. Keep main.bicep — you will commit it to Git on Day 24.

G. Practical Assignment

Add a second resource to main.bicep: a blob container is a child resource and slightly advanced, so instead add tags to the storage account (tags: { environment: 'learning' }). Run what-if first to confirm it shows an update (not a delete), then redeploy and verify the tag in the portal.

  1. Q1. What does 'az deployment group what-if' do?
    • A) It deletes the resource group as a test
    • B) It deploys the template to a temporary sandbox subscription
    • C) It previews which resources would be created, modified, or deleted — without changing anything
    • D) It estimates the monthly cost of the deployment
  2. Q2. You deploy main.bicep twice in a row with no changes. What happens on the second deployment?
    • A) A second storage account is created with a new name
    • B) The existing storage account is updated in place; no duplicate is created
    • C) The deployment fails because the resource already exists
    • D) The resource group is deleted and recreated
  3. Q3. By default, in which mode does 'az deployment group create' run?
    • A) Complete mode — everything not in the template is deleted
    • B) Incremental mode — template resources are created or updated, and anything else in the group is left alone
    • C) Audit mode — the deployment only reports compliance
    • D) Deny mode — the deployment is blocked
  4. Q4. Your deployment fails with 'StorageAccountAlreadyTaken'. What is the cause and the fix?
    • A) The name is already taken by someone else — choose a different globally unique name
    • B) Your subscription is out of storage quota — request a quota increase
    • C) Bicep is not installed — run az bicep install
    • D) The resource group is in the wrong region
  5. Q5. Write the three commands, in order, that (1) preview what main.bicep would change in rg-bicep-deploy, (2) actually deploy it, and (3) delete everything the lab created.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: C. What-if previews the create/update/delete operations a deployment would perform, without changing anything. It is the safe rehearsal before the real deployment — always run it when a template changes.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. ARM matches resources by type and name. Redeploying updates the existing resource in place instead of duplicating it — this idempotency is what makes IaC safe to re-run.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. The default mode is Incremental: resources in the template are created or updated, and anything already in the group that is not in the template is left alone. (Complete mode would delete those extras — never use it by accident.)
  4. Q4 — Answer: A. Storage account names are globally unique across all of Azure. 'StorageAccountAlreadyTaken' means another customer owns that name — change the name (adding digits or using uniqueString) and redeploy.
  5. Q5 — Answer: az deployment group what-if --resource-group rg-bicep-deploy --template-file main.bicep → review the planned changes. az deployment group create --name day23-deploy --resource-group rg-bicep-deploy --template-file main.bicep → deploy. az group delete --name rg-bicep-deploy --yes → delete everything.. What-if first, deploy second, delete the resource group last. The group deletion is the cleanup that makes the whole lab cost essentially nothing.

Day 24 — Git and GitHub Fundamentals for Azure Learners60–90 minutes

A. Day 24 — Git and GitHub Fundamentals for Azure Learners

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain the difference between Git (local tool) and GitHub (hosting platform)
  • Configure Git identity and initialize a local repository
  • Stage and commit files with clear messages, and read git log
  • Use .gitignore to keep secrets and local files out of version control
  • Push a local repository to GitHub and pull a remote change back down

C. Prerequisites

  • Completion of Days 21–23 — main.bicep from Day 22, deployed on Day 23
  • A free GitHub account (the lab walks you through using it; create one at github.com beforehand if needed)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Think of Git as save points in a video game, plus a shared cloud save. Every commit is a save point: a complete snapshot of your project with a message explaining what changed. You can return to any save point, compare two of them, or hand them to a teammate. A project under Git is a repository (often shortened to 'repo') — a folder whose entire history Git tracks.

Git and GitHub are different things, and confusing them is the most common beginner mistake. Git is free software that runs on your computer — no account, no internet needed. GitHub is a website (owned by Microsoft) that hosts Git repositories online, so you get a backup, sharing, and collaboration features like pull requests — a page where proposed changes are reviewed before being merged into the main branch. A copy of a repository on another machine is called a remote, conventionally named origin. You push commits up to the remote and pull other people's commits down; clone downloads a fresh copy of a repository.

Why does an Azure learner care? Everything you made in the last three days — CLI commands, PowerShell scripts, and your main.bicep — is code. Code belongs in Git: version-controlled, backed up, reviewable. Professional Azure teams keep their Bicep and scripts in GitHub and deploy from there. Today you will put your Day 23 Bicep file into your first GitHub repository.

One security rule, non-negotiable: never commit secrets — no passwords, no storage keys, no connection strings. A file named .gitignore lists files Git should skip (like local notes and credential files). And if a secret ever does get pushed, deleting it in the next commit is not enough — it still lives in the history. Treat it as compromised and rotate it immediately. GitHub setup screens change over time; if a button's label differs slightly from this lab, the flow is the same.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Getting started with GitDocumentationhttps://docs.github.com/en/get-started/learning-to-code/getting-started-with-git?search-overlay-open=true VerifiedGitHub's own walkthrough of repositories, branches, commits, and pull requests25 min
Connecting to GitHubDocumentationhttps://docs.github.com/en/get-started/using-github/connecting-to-github VerifiedThe ways to work with GitHub repositories: browser, editor, desktop app, or command line10 min
Git documentation and Pro Git bookDocumentationhttps://git-scm.com/doc Needs verificationThe official Git reference and the free Pro Git book for deeper study30 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Install/check Git, configure your identity, create a local repository containing your Bicep file, ignore a secrets file with .gitignore, push to a new GitHub repository, and pull a web edit back down.

Prerequisites: Azure free account not needed for this lab. You need a free GitHub account (create one at github.com if you don't have one) and a terminal — Cloud Shell Bash works and already has Git installed.

Cost note: $0. Git is free software; a GitHub personal account with public and private repositories is free.

Step 1. Open Cloud Shell in Bash (or your local terminal). Run 'git --version'. Cloud Shell includes Git. On your own computer, if Git is missing, install it from the official downloads page for your operating system and re-run the command.

Expected result: A version line like 'git version 2.4x.x'.

Verify: A git version number prints.

Troubleshooting: Local Windows users: install 'Git for Windows' and use its Git Bash terminal. Do not skip configuration in step 2 — commits will fail without an identity.

Step 2. Tell Git who you are (one-time setup): git config --global user.name 'Your Name' and git config --global user.email 'you@example.com'. Use the email on your GitHub account so commits are attributed to you.

Expected result: No output — Git accepts the settings silently.

Verify: 'git config --global --list' shows user.name and user.email.

Troubleshooting: The quotes matter — use single quotes. Run 'git config --global --list' to check both values were saved.

Step 3. Create the project folder: mkdir ~/azure-git-lab && cd ~/azure-git-lab. Initialize Git: git init. Copy your Bicep file in: cp ~/bicep-lab/main.bicep . (If Day 22/23 files are elsewhere, adjust the path.)

Expected result: 'Initialized empty Git repository' message, and 'ls -a' shows a hidden .git folder.

Verify: git status prints 'On branch ... No commits yet' and lists main.bicep as an untracked file.

Troubleshooting: If 'cp' fails with 'no such file', find your file with 'find ~ -name main.bicep' and copy from that path.

Step 4. Create a README: use the editor ('code README.md') and type one line: '# Azure Workshop Files — Bicep templates and scripts from the 30-Day Azure Learning Workshop.' Save the file.

Expected result: A README.md file exists in the folder.

Verify: 'ls' shows README.md and main.bicep.

Step 5. Create a .gitignore so secrets never get committed: run 'code .gitignore' and add two lines: '.env' and 'secrets.txt'. Save. Then create a dummy secrets file to prove it works: echo 'this-is-not-a-real-secret' > .env.

Expected result: git status no longer lists .env — it is invisible to Git now.

Verify: 'git status' lists .gitignore, README.md, and main.bicep — but NOT .env.

Troubleshooting: The file must be named exactly '.gitignore' (leading dot, no extension). If Git still lists .env, check for a typo in the filename.

Step 6. Make your first commit: git add main.bicep README.md .gitignore, then git commit -m 'Add Day 23 Bicep template'. The add step stages files; the commit saves the snapshot with your message.

Expected result: git status reports 'nothing to commit, working tree clean'.

Verify: 'git log --oneline' shows one commit with your message.

Troubleshooting: If commit fails with 'Please tell me who you are', repeat step 2 — the identity config was not saved.

Step 7. Create the GitHub home: sign in to github.com → '+' (top right) → 'New repository'. Name it 'azure-workshop'. Leave it Public or Private (your choice). Do NOT tick 'Add a README file' — your local repo already has commits. Select 'Create repository'.

Expected result: An empty repository page showing a '…push an existing repository from the command line' section with a URL ending in .git.

Verify: You see the repository URL: https://github.com/<your-username>/azure-workshop.

Troubleshooting: If you accidentally added a README on GitHub, the push in the next step will be rejected — delete that repository and recreate it empty.

Step 8. Connect and push: git remote add origin https://github.com/<your-username>/azure-workshop.git, then git branch -M main, then git push -u origin main. Replace <your-username> with your GitHub username. If GitHub asks you to sign in, complete the browser sign-in prompt (Git Credential Manager) — never type your GitHub password into a random script or share it.

Expected result: Git prints 'branch main set up to track origin/main' and the push completes.

Verify: Refresh the GitHub repository page — main.bicep, README.md, and .gitignore are visible.

Troubleshooting: 'Permission denied' almost always means you typed the wrong username in the URL. Check with 'git remote -v' and fix with 'git remote set-url origin <correct-url>'.

Step 9. Make a web edit: on GitHub, open README.md → pencil icon → add a second line: 'Deployed with Azure CLI and Bicep.' → 'Commit changes'. You just committed directly on the remote.

Expected result: The web commit appears in the repository's commit list.

Verify: The README on GitHub shows both lines.

Step 10. Pull the web change down: back in your terminal, run 'git pull'. Watch it download and apply the README change from origin.

Expected result: git log shows two commits, with the web edit on top. Your local README.md now has both lines (check with 'cat README.md').

Verify: 'cat README.md' shows the line you added on the web.

Troubleshooting: If pull complains about local changes, you edited the file locally too — in this controlled lab, run 'git status' and commit your local change first, then pull again.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Nothing billable exists to clean up — Git and a free GitHub account cost $0. Keep the repository: it is the home for your workshop files for the rest of the course. If you ever want to remove it: GitHub → repository → Settings → Danger Zone → Delete this repository. Locally, your files stay in ~/azure-git-lab unless you delete that folder.

G. Practical Assignment

Create a second branch in your azure-workshop repository (git checkout -b add-readme-notes), add one line to README.md describing what the workshop is, commit it, push the branch, and open a pull request on GitHub. Read (do not merge yet) the pull request page and describe in your journal what it shows.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is the difference between Git and GitHub?
    • A) Git is the version control tool on your computer; GitHub is a website that hosts Git repositories
    • B) GitHub is the tool; Git is the website
    • C) They are two competing products from the same company
    • D) Git only works inside GitHub
  2. Q2. In the everyday Git workflow, what is the correct order?
    • A) commit → add → push
    • B) add → commit → push
    • C) push → commit → add
    • D) clone → push → commit
  3. Q3. What is a commit?
    • A) A backup copy of your whole hard drive
    • B) A comment on someone else's code
    • C) A snapshot of your project files together with a message describing the change
    • D) A folder you can safely delete at any time
  4. Q4. You realize you committed and pushed an Azure storage access key to GitHub last month. What is the correct first action?
    • A) Make a new commit that deletes the secret file — the problem is fixed
    • B) Revoke or rotate the credential immediately; it must be treated as compromised
    • C) Nothing — private repositories are safe places for credentials
    • D) Just rename the file in the next commit
  5. Q5. Starting from a folder on your computer that contains main.bicep and README.md, write the command sequence to create a local Git repository, commit those files with the message 'Add Day 23 Bicep template', connect it to an empty GitHub repository you already created, and push.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: A. Git runs locally on your machine and works with no internet connection. GitHub is the website/platform that hosts Git repositories online (owned by Microsoft) for backup, sharing, and pull-request reviews.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Files flow: edit → stage with 'git add' → snapshot with 'git commit' → upload with 'git push'. Reversing stages (push before commit) uploads nothing.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. A commit is a snapshot with a message. The commit itself — carrying all changes, metadata, and history position — is what lets you (and teammates) compare versions and roll back.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. Secrets already pushed must be treated as breached: revoke/rotate them first, then scrub history with a tool like BFG and force-push. A removal commit alone leaves the secret in history forever.
  5. Q5 — Answer: git init → git add main.bicep README.md → git commit -m 'Add Day 23 Bicep template' → git remote add origin https://github.com/<username>/azure-workshop.git → git branch -M main → git push -u origin main. The answer should include all five stages in this order (username placeholder acceptable).. This is the exact Day 24 workflow. Init creates the repo, add stages, commit snapshots, remote connects GitHub, push uploads.

Day 25 — Deployment Workflows and Your First Look at Azure DevOps60–90 minutes

A. Day 25 — Deployment Workflows and Your First Look at Azure DevOps

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain CI (Continuous Integration) and CD (Continuous Delivery) in your own words.
  • Describe what Azure DevOps is and name its five main services.
  • Connect a GitHub repository to an Azure App Service so code deploys automatically.
  • Trigger a real CI/CD redeploy by editing code in GitHub and watching the change go live.

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Days 1-24 — you can create resource groups and navigate the portal
  • A free GitHub account (create one at github.com if you do not have one; it takes about five minutes)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Think of the difference between cooking dinner for one friend and running a restaurant kitchen. Cooking for a friend, you improvise: a pinch of this, taste, adjust, serve. A restaurant cannot work that way. It needs a printed recipe, the same ingredients in the same order, the same timing, every single time — otherwise one customer's meal is perfect and the next is a disaster. Deploying software works the same way. Doing it by hand (copying files to a server yourself) is the home-cooking approach: slow, error-prone, and different every time. An automated deployment workflow is the restaurant kitchen: a fixed recipe that runs the same way on every order.

CI (Continuous Integration) means developers merge their code changes into a shared repository often — usually daily — and every merge automatically triggers a build and a set of tests. Problems surface in minutes, not weeks. CD has two meanings you should know: Continuous Delivery means the software is always in a releasable state and a human presses the final release button; Continuous Deployment goes one step further and releases to users automatically with no human button-press. In this workshop we use the automatic flavor.

A few words you will see everywhere: a pipeline is the automated recipe itself — a sequence of steps (build, test, deploy). An artifact is the packaged output of the build step, the thing that actually gets deployed. A stage (also called an environment) is one stop on the journey, such as dev, test, or production. A deployment slot is a second copy of your web app living beside the live one, so you can test the new version on a staging URL and swap it into production with zero downtime.

Azure DevOps is Microsoft's suite of tools for running this whole process. It has five main services: Boards (plan and track work), Repos (host Git code), Pipelines (build, test, and deploy with CI/CD), Artifacts (store packages), and Test Plans (manual and exploratory testing). You do not need all five today. The one that matters for deployment is Pipelines. Its main rival is GitHub Actions, which does the same CI/CD job from inside GitHub — and that is what we will use in today's lab, because App Service's Deployment Center can wire it up for you in a few clicks.

Two honest notes before we start. First: a budget alert in Azure is an alert, not a spending cap. It sends you an email when spending crosses a threshold, but it does not stop the spending, and the alert itself can be delayed. Set budgets anyway — early warning beats no warning. Second: portal screens, prices, and free-tier limits change over time. If a button has moved since this was written, search the portal for the feature name and you will find it.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Overview of Azure App ServiceDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/app-service/overview VerifiedOfficial overview of the App Service platform you will deploy to today, including its CI/CD options15 min
Microsoft Learn Azure training hubMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/azure/ VerifiedFree Microsoft modules on Azure fundamentals if you want a second explanation of any concept20 min

Azure-Samples Node.js hello-world appToolhttps://github.com/Azure-Samples/nodejs-docs-hello-world Needs verificationThe small sample web app you will fork on GitHub and deploy in the lab5 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Deploy a web app from GitHub to Azure App Service with automatic redeploys on every code change.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; free GitHub account.

Cost note: Free. The App Service uses the Free (F1) tier, GitHub is free, and the Deployment Center costs nothing. Remember: budget alerts warn you but do not cap spending — Day 27 shows you how to set one up.

Step 1. Sign in to GitHub (create a free account first if needed). Open the Azure-Samples Node.js hello-world repository (linked in today's resources) and click Fork (top-right), then Create fork. You now own a personal copy of the sample app.

Expected result: A new repository appears under your GitHub username with the same files as the sample.

Verify: Your GitHub profile → Repositories shows the forked repo.

Troubleshooting: No Fork button visible means you are not signed in — sign in and try again.

Step 2. In the Azure portal, click Create a resource, search for 'Web App', and click Create. Fill in: Subscription = your free subscription; Resource group = Create new, name it rg-deploy-day25; Name = a globally unique name like myapp-yourinitials-25; Runtime stack = Node 20 LTS; Operating system = Linux; Region = East US (or the region nearest you).

Expected result: The form accepts your entries; the app name shows a green checkmark.

Verify: Green checkmark next to the web app name means it is unique and valid.

Troubleshooting: Name rejected as taken: add more initials or numbers until the checkmark turns green.

Step 3. Under App Service Plan, click Create new. Name it asp-day25, then change the Pricing plan: click Change size (or Explore pricing plans), open the Dev/Test tab, select F1 (Free), and click Select/Apply.

Expected result: The plan summary shows Pricing plan: Free (F1).

Verify: The SKU reads F1 — this keeps the lab free.

Troubleshooting: F1 missing from the list: your subscription may already use its free quota in that region — try a different region.

Step 4. Click Review + create, then Create. Wait for 'Your deployment is complete', then click Go to resource.

Expected result: The web app's Overview blade opens.

Verify: Overview shows the app name, URL, and App Service Plan asp-day25.

Troubleshooting: Deployment failed: click the error, usually a name or region issue — delete the failed attempt and retry step 2.

Step 5. In the left menu under Deployment, click Deployment Center. Set Source to GitHub, then click Authorize and sign in to GitHub when asked, granting Azure App Service access to your repositories.

Expected result: A GitHub authorization window appears; after approving, the portal shows your GitHub account connected.

Verify: The Source dropdown area shows your GitHub username as connected.

Troubleshooting: Popup blocked by the browser: allow popups for portal.azure.com and click Authorize again.

Step 6. Still in Deployment Center: Organization = your GitHub username, Repository = your forked hello-world repo, Branch = main (or master — match whatever branch your fork has). Set Build provider to GitHub Actions, then click Save at the top.

Expected result: A banner says the deployment configuration is being saved; a workflow file is added to your repo.

Verify: In your GitHub fork, check the Actions tab — a workflow run should start within a minute.

Troubleshooting: No workflow appears: confirm the branch name matches your fork's default branch exactly.

Step 7. Watch the workflow: in GitHub, open your repo → Actions tab → click the running workflow. Wait until every step shows a green checkmark (2-5 minutes).

Expected result: All workflow steps succeed (build, deploy).

Verify: Green checkmarks on every step; the deploy step log mentions your app name.

Troubleshooting: A step fails: open its log, read the red error lines — most often a Node version mismatch, fixed by re-saving the Deployment Center settings.

Step 8. Back in the portal, open your web app → Overview → click Browse (or visit the URL). You should see the sample app's welcome page.

Expected result: The hello-world sample page loads in your browser.

Verify: The page title/content matches the sample app, not the Azure default landing page.

Troubleshooting: Still seeing the Azure default page: the deploy may still be finishing — wait 2 minutes and refresh, or restart the app (Overview → Restart).

Step 9. Now the CI/CD magic. In your GitHub fork, open the main page file (for example views/index or the HTML/JS file rendering the title), click the pencil Edit icon, change the heading text to include your name, and click Commit changes.

Expected result: GitHub saves the commit; the Actions tab starts a brand-new workflow run automatically.

Verify: Actions tab shows a new run triggered by your commit — nobody clicked anything in Azure.

Troubleshooting: No new run: make sure you committed to the same branch the Deployment Center is watching.

Step 10. Wait 2-4 minutes for the workflow to finish, then refresh your app URL. Your edited heading should now be live.

Expected result: The live site shows your custom heading text.

Verify: Your name/text appears on the public URL. You just did Continuous Deployment: code change → automatic build → automatic release.

Troubleshooting: Change not visible: hard-refresh (Ctrl+Shift+R), the browser may be showing a cached copy.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete everything this lab created: Portal → search 'Resource groups' → open rg-deploy-day25 → Delete resource group → type rg-deploy-day25 to confirm → Delete. This removes the web app, the App Service plan, and the deployment configuration. Confirm the resource group no longer appears in the list.

G. Practical Assignment

On paper, draw today's deployment flow as boxes and arrows: GitHub repo → GitHub Actions → App Service → browser. Label exactly where CI happens (build + test) and where CD happens (release to users). Keep it — you will reuse this diagram on Day 28.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is the practical difference between Continuous Integration (CI) and Continuous Deployment (CD)?
    • A) CI deploys to users; CD only runs tests
    • B) CI merges and tests code changes frequently and automatically; CD automatically releases every passing change to users
    • C) They are two names for the exact same thing
    • D) CI is manual and CD is automatic
  2. Q2. In a deployment pipeline, what is an artifact?
    • A) A bug found during testing
    • B) The packaged output of the build step that actually gets deployed
    • C) A GitHub username
    • D) A type of Azure region
  3. Q3. Which Azure DevOps service handles CI/CD pipelines?
    • A) Boards
    • B) Repos
    • C) Pipelines
    • D) Test Plans
  4. Q4. What is a deployment slot in App Service?
    • A) A second copy of your app (e.g., staging) that you can test and then swap into production with zero downtime
    • B) A time window when deployments are allowed
    • C) A GitHub Actions billing tier
    • D) A firewall rule for deployments
  5. Q5. Practical: without touching the Azure portal, how would you prove to a colleague that your Day 25 setup is true CI/CD?
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. CI is about integrating and validating code early and often; CD takes validated code all the way to users automatically.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. The artifact is the build's packaged result (e.g., a zip of the app) — the pipeline deploys the artifact, not raw source files.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. Pipelines builds, tests, and deploys. Boards tracks work, Repos hosts code, Artifacts stores packages, Test Plans manages testing.
  4. Q4 — Answer: A. Slots let you validate the new version on a staging URL first, then swap it live — the safe way to release.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Make a small text edit to the app in GitHub and commit it, then show that a deployment starts and goes live on its own — the portal is never opened during the release.. The defining test of CI/CD is that a code commit alone triggers the whole build-test-deploy chain with no manual steps.

Day 26 — Troubleshooting with Logs and Metrics60–90 minutes

A. Day 26 — Troubleshooting with Logs and Metrics

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Explain the difference between metrics and logs in your own words.
  • Describe what Azure Monitor, Log Analytics, and Application Insights do.
  • Read a metrics chart and run a basic KQL query against real log data.
  • Create an alert rule that emails you when your app starts failing.

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Days 1-25 — you have deployed a web app before and can create resource groups

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Imagine you are a doctor and your patient is your application. The doctor checks two kinds of information. First, vitals: temperature, blood pressure, heart rate — numbers measured over time that show trends. In Azure, those are metrics: CPU percentage, request counts, failed requests, response times. Second, the doctor reads the patient's diary: timestamped notes like 'felt dizzy at 3pm after lunch.' In Azure, those are logs: individual events with timestamps, such as 'request to /checkout failed with status 500 at 14:32.' Metrics tell you something is wrong; logs tell you what exactly happened. Good troubleshooting uses both.

Azure Monitor is the umbrella service that collects both. Think of it as the hospital's monitoring department. Under it: Log Analytics is the filing cabinet where log data is stored in workspaces, and you search it with KQL (Kusto Query Language) — the librarian's search language, a simple pipeline-style query syntax you will try today. Application Insights is an APM (Application Performance Monitoring) feature of Azure Monitor built for live web apps: it tracks request rates, response times, failure rates, and dependencies automatically.

An alert rule is a smoke detector. It has two parts: a condition (the smoke — e.g., 'more than zero HTTP 500 errors in 5 minutes') and an action group (the alarm bell — e.g., 'email me'). Without alert rules, you only learn about outages from angry users. With them, Azure taps you on the shoulder first.

Professional incident investigation follows the same loop every time: Detect (alert fires or user reports) → Triage (how bad, who is affected, right now) → Investigate (metrics for scope, logs for cause) → Mitigate (stop the bleeding first — restart, roll back, scale) → Learn (blameless review: what broke, what would catch it sooner next time). Memorize this loop; it is the same one used by on-call engineers at every large company.

Cost note: Log Analytics ingestion includes a free allowance (5 GB per month at the time of writing — verify current limits, as pricing and free-tier limits change over time). Today's lab stays inside it. Portal screens may also have moved since this was written; search the portal for the feature name if a button is not where described.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure Monitor overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/overview VerifiedOfficial overview of Azure Monitor: metrics, logs, Log Analytics workspaces, and Application Insights20 min
Microsoft Learn Azure training hubMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/azure/ VerifiedFree modules covering monitoring concepts if you want extra practice beyond the lab20 min
Azure pricing calculatorToolhttps://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/ VerifiedEstimate what Log Analytics ingestion would cost at higher volumes so you understand the free allowance10 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Send a web app's logs to Log Analytics, investigate them with KQL, and create an email alert for failures.

Prerequisites: Azure free account. You will recreate a small web app (Day 25's was deleted in cleanup).

Cost note: Free tier. App Service F1 is free; Log Analytics has a free monthly ingestion allowance (5 GB/month at time of writing). Diagnostic settings themselves cost nothing.

Step 1. Create a resource group: Portal → search 'Resource groups' → + Create → name it rg-monitor-day26 → pick your usual region → Review + create → Create.

Expected result: The resource group is created.

Verify: It appears in the resource groups list.

Step 2. Create the Log Analytics workspace: Portal → search 'Log Analytics workspaces' → + Create → resource group rg-monitor-day26, name law-day26, region matching your resource group → Review + create → Create. (Pricing tier defaults are fine — you will stay inside the free ingestion allowance.)

Expected result: Deployment completes; the workspace opens.

Verify: Overview shows the workspace name and region.

Troubleshooting: Name rejected: workspace names must be unique-ish — add your initials.

Step 3. Create a web app in rg-monitor-day26 exactly as on Day 25 (Create a resource → Web App → Node 20 LTS, Linux, new Free F1 plan named asp-day26). You do NOT need the GitHub deployment wiring today — the default app is enough to generate logs.

Expected result: Web app created and running.

Verify: Overview → Browse shows the default app page.

Troubleshooting: Browse shows an error page right after creation: wait 1-2 minutes and refresh; new apps need a moment.

Step 4. Send the app's logs to your workspace: open the web app → left menu under Monitoring → Diagnostic settings → + Add diagnostic setting. Name it send-to-law. Under Logs, check AppServiceHTTPLogs and AppServiceAppLogs. Under Destination details, select 'Send to Log Analytics workspace' and pick law-day26. Click Save.

Expected result: The diagnostic setting is saved and listed.

Verify: Diagnostic settings shows send-to-law with the two log categories checked.

Troubleshooting: Save fails: make sure the workspace is in the same region as the app, then retry.

Step 5. Also turn on application logging: in the web app → left menu under Monitoring → App Service logs → Application logging (Filesystem) → On, Level = Information → Save.

Expected result: Setting saved.

Verify: The toggle reads On.

Step 6. Generate traffic: open the app URL in a browser, refresh 5-10 times, then visit a nonsense page like https://yourappname.azurewebsites.net/this-page-does-not-exist to create some 404 errors. (Replace yourappname with your app's name.)

Expected result: Pages load; the bad URL shows a 404.

Verify: You see normal pages and one 404 — that 404 is now evidence in your logs.

Step 7. Wait 5-10 minutes (log delivery is not instant), then open the web app → Monitoring → Metrics. Set Metric to 'Requests', Aggregation to Sum, and look at the chart. Try adding a second metric: 'Http 4xx'.

Expected result: The chart shows your recent requests as a line or bars, including the 404s under Http 4xx.

Verify: The chart is not flat/empty — it reflects the traffic you generated.

Troubleshooting: Chart empty: wait a few more minutes and widen the time range to 'Past 1 hour'.

Step 8. Now query the raw logs: web app → Monitoring → Logs → close any pop-up queries. In the query box, type exactly: AppServiceHTTPLogs | where TimeGenerated > ago(1h) | summarize Requests = count() by ScStatus | order by Requests desc Then click Run.

Expected result: A results table appears showing status codes (200, 404, ...) and their counts.

Verify: You see at least one row for 200 and one row for 404.

Troubleshooting: No results: the #1 cause is impatience — logs can take 10-15 minutes to arrive. Also confirm the time range (top-right) covers the last hour and that step 4's diagnostic setting saved.

Step 9. Create the smoke detector: web app → Monitoring → Alerts → + Create → Alert rule. Under Condition, click 'See all signals', search for and select 'Http 5xx'. Set: Aggregation = Total, Operator = Greater than, Threshold = 0, check period and window = 5 minutes. Click Next.

Expected result: The condition summary reads roughly 'Total Http 5xx > 0 over last 5 minutes'.

Verify: The condition preview shows your threshold logic.

Troubleshooting: Http 5xx signal missing: the app must have metrics flowing — revisit step 7; brand-new apps sometimes need traffic first.

Step 10. Under Actions: create an action group — click Create action group → name it ag-email-day26, short name agemail26 → Notification type = Email/SMS/Push/Voice → enter YOUR email → Review + create. Back in the rule wizard, name the alert rule alert-5xx-day26, set severity Sev 2, and Create alert rule.

Expected result: The alert rule is created and listed under Alerts → Alert rules.

Verify: Alert rules list shows alert-5xx-day26 as Enabled.

Troubleshooting: Email not received later: check spam; also note action-group emails can take several minutes.

Step 11. Optional but instructive: in the Logs blade, run this second query to see your slowest pages: AppServiceHTTPLogs | where TimeGenerated > ago(1h) | top 10 by TimeTaken desc | project TimeGenerated, CsUriStem, ScStatus, TimeTaken

Expected result: Up to 10 rows showing which requests took longest.

Verify: The TimeTaken column shows numbers — you are now reading real performance data.

Troubleshooting: TimeTaken column empty: some requests log it as 0 for tiny static responses — normal.

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete everything: Portal → Resource groups → open rg-monitor-day26 → Delete resource group → type rg-monitor-day26 → Delete. This removes the web app, the Log Analytics workspace, and the alert rule. Confirm the resource group is gone from the list.

Write the Detect → Triage → Investigate → Mitigate → Learn loop on an index card in your own words, with one concrete Azure action for each step (for example, Investigate = 'run a KQL query in Log Analytics'). Keep it at your desk for the rest of the workshop.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. A teammate says 'metrics and logs are basically the same thing.' What is the correct distinction?
    • A) Metrics are numbers measured over time (CPU %, request counts); logs are timestamped individual events ('request X failed at 14:32')
    • B) Metrics are free and logs always cost money
    • C) Logs are numbers and metrics are text
    • D) There is no difference
  2. Q2. What is KQL used for in Azure?
    • A) Writing web app code
    • B) Querying log data stored in Log Analytics workspaces
    • C) Creating virtual networks
    • D) Sending alert emails
  3. Q3. Application Insights is best described as:
    • A) A billing report
    • B) An APM (Application Performance Monitoring) feature of Azure Monitor for live web apps — request rates, response times, failures
    • C) A code editor
    • D) A backup service
  4. Q4. An alert rule has two essential parts. They are:
    • A) A condition (what counts as a problem) and an action group (who gets notified / what happens)
    • B) A username and a password
    • C) A region and a resource group
    • D) A dashboard and a workbook
  5. Q5. Practical: write a KQL query that shows the 10 slowest requests to your app in the last hour, including the page URL and status code.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: A. Metrics = aggregated numbers over time (trends, charts). Logs = discrete timestamped events (the details behind a spike).
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. KQL (Kusto Query Language) is the query language for searching and analyzing log data in Log Analytics.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Application Insights gives automatic performance and failure telemetry for running applications.
  4. Q4 — Answer: A. Condition = the smoke (e.g., Http 5xx > 0); action group = the alarm bell (e.g., email you).
  5. Q5 — Answer: AppServiceHTTPLogs | where TimeGenerated > ago(1h) | top 10 by TimeTaken desc | project TimeGenerated, CsUriStem, ScStatus, TimeTaken. Filter to the last hour, take the 10 highest TimeTaken values, and project only the useful columns.

Day 27 — Best Practices Review: Identity, Networking, Security, Operations60–90 minutes

A. Day 27 — Best Practices Review: Identity, Networking, Security, Operations

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Summarize identity best practices: Microsoft Entra ID, RBAC roles, least privilege, and managed identity.
  • Summarize networking and security best practices: NSGs, Key Vault, and keeping secrets out of code.
  • Apply operational best practices: naming conventions, tags, budget alerts, and Azure Advisor.
  • Run a best-practice audit on your own Azure resources and fix what you find.

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Days 1-26 — especially Day 25 (deployment) and Day 26 (monitoring)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Today is consolidation day — no brand-new service to learn. Instead, we do what professionals do before anything important ships: a checklist inspection. Think of a house inspection before you buy: the inspector does not build anything, she walks room to room checking locks, wiring, smoke detectors, and labels. Today you are the inspector, and your Azure subscription is the house. We review four rooms: identity, networking, security, and operations.

Room 1 — Identity. Microsoft Entra ID (formerly Azure AD) is the identity system: who you are. RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) is what you are allowed to do. Memorize the three big roles: Owner (full control including giving access to others), Contributor (manage resources but cannot grant access), Reader (look but do not touch). The golden rule is least privilege: give every person and every app only the permissions it actually needs — nothing more. And managed identity gives your app its own identity badge in Entra ID, so it can access other Azure resources without you ever storing a password or key in its settings.

Room 2 — Networking. Room 3 — Security. A VNet (virtual network) is your private network in the cloud; an NSG (Network Security Group) is the firewall rule list on its doors — default-deny, open only what you need. Key Vault is the safe: passwords, connection strings, and certificates live there, never in code, never in app settings as plain text, and never in a Git repository. If a secret is ever committed to Git, treat it as compromised and rotate it. Microsoft Defender for Cloud is the security guard that watches your subscription and grades your posture — free tier included.

Room 4 — Operations. Naming conventions (like rg-deploy-day25) let you tell what a resource is and when it can be deleted. Tags are labels on resources — Owner, Environment, CostCenter — that make cost reports and cleanup possible. Budget alerts email you before spending surprises you (remember: alerts, not caps). Azure Advisor is a free built-in consultant that scans your resources and recommends cost, security, reliability, and performance improvements. Professionals check Advisor the way pilots check instruments: routinely, not just in emergencies.

One meta-rule ties all four rooms together: never weaken security to make a lab work. If a tutorial tells you to disable a firewall, open a port to the world, or paste a key into code 'just to get it running,' that tutorial is teaching you a habit you will have to unlearn. There is always a secure way — managed identity, Key Vault references, scoped firewall rules — and this workshop only teaches those.

E. Learning Resources

ResourceTypeDirect URLPurposeEstimated Time
Azure Key Vault overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/general/overview VerifiedHow Key Vault stores secrets, keys, and certificates so they never live in code or app settings15 min
Azure pricing calculatorToolhttps://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/ VerifiedPractice estimating costs — a core operational habit before creating any resource10 min
AZ-900 cloud concepts learning pathMicrosoft Learnhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/microsoft-azure-fundamentals-describe-cloud-concepts/ VerifiedThe official fundamentals path — a good cross-check that your identity/security mental models are correct30 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Audit a live web app against the four-room best-practice checklist and fix every finding.

Prerequisites: Azure free account.

Cost note: Free tier. App Service F1 is free; tags, managed identity, budgets, and Advisor cost nothing. Pricing and free-tier limits change over time — verify before creating anything beyond F1.

Step 1. Create a resource group rg-review-day27 and a web app in it (Node 20 LTS, Linux, new Free F1 plan asp-day27) exactly as on Days 25-26. No GitHub wiring needed — this app is your inspection subject.

Expected result: Web app running; Browse shows the default page.

Verify: Overview shows the app and its URL.

Troubleshooting: Creation errors are almost always the app name being taken — add initials/numbers.

Step 2. Room 4 — Tags: open rg-review-day27 → left menu → Tags. Add: Owner = your name, Environment = Learning, DeleteAfter = 2026-11-01. Click Apply.

Expected result: Tags saved on the resource group.

Verify: Reopen Tags — your three tags are listed.

Troubleshooting: Tags not saving: you need write permission on the resource group — as subscription owner, you have it; retry.

Step 3. Room 1 — Managed identity: open the web app → left menu under Settings → Identity → System assigned tab → Status = On → Save → Yes to confirm.

Expected result: Status shows On with an Object (principal) ID.

Verify: An Object ID is displayed — your app now has its own Entra ID identity badge.

Troubleshooting: Save fails transiently: wait a minute and retry; identity provisioning occasionally needs a second attempt.

Step 4. Room 4 — Budget alert: Portal → search 'Cost Management' → Budgets → + Add. Scope = your subscription (or rg-review-day27), name budget-learning-20, amount $20 USD, monthly. Add an alert condition at 50% and 100% of budget, email = your address. Create.

Expected result: Budget appears in the budgets list.

Verify: Open it — alert conditions list your email at 50% and 100%.

Troubleshooting: Cannot create at subscription scope: scope it to the resource group instead — same protective effect for this workshop.

Step 5. Room 4 — Advisor: Portal → search 'Advisor' → open it. Review the recommendations under Cost, Security, Reliability, Operational Excellence for your resource group.

Expected result: A list of recommendations (possibly 'no recommendations' — that is also a result).

Verify: You can open at least one recommendation and read what it suggests.

Troubleshooting: Advisor empty: it can take time to analyze brand-new resources — note what you see and move on.

Step 6. Room 3 — Secrets check: web app → Settings → Configuration → Application settings. Confirm there are no passwords, keys, or connection strings stored here. (There should be none on a fresh app — the point is building the habit of looking.)

Expected result: Only default settings; no secrets present.

Verify: You can state where a real secret WOULD go: Key Vault, accessed via the managed identity you enabled in step 3.

Step 7. Room 1 — Access check: open rg-review-day27 → Access control (IAM) → Role assignments tab. Confirm only expected identities (you) hold roles here.

Expected result: Your user account listed, typically as Owner or Contributor; no strangers.

Verify: You can explain why Contributor would be enough for a teammate who only deploys code.

Step 8. Write down your audit findings: for each of the four rooms, one sentence on what you checked and whether it passed. Example: 'Identity: managed identity enabled — PASS.'

Expected result: Four sentences, one per room.

Verify: Any FAIL has a fix noted next to it (you already applied the fixes in steps 2-7).

Cleanup (prevent charges): Delete everything: Portal → Resource groups → rg-review-day27 → Delete resource group → type the name → Delete. Also delete the budget: Cost Management → Budgets → open budget-learning-20 → Delete. Confirm both are gone.

G. Practical Assignment

Pick one real-world analogy of your own (not the house inspection) that explains least privilege to a non-technical friend — for example, hotel key cards that only open your floor. Write it in three sentences.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What does 'least privilege' mean in practice?
    • A) Everyone gets Owner so nothing is ever blocked
    • B) Every person and app gets only the permissions it actually needs — nothing more
    • C) Nobody gets any access
    • D) Privileges are assigned alphabetically
  2. Q2. What problem does a managed identity solve?
    • A) It makes the app run faster
    • B) It gives the app its own Entra ID identity so it can access Azure resources without stored passwords or keys
    • C) It backs up the app automatically
    • D) It translates the app into other languages
  3. Q3. Where should a database connection string for your app be stored?
    • A) Pasted into the app's code and committed to GitHub
    • B) In Azure Key Vault, referenced by the app via its managed identity
    • C) In a sticky note on your monitor
    • D) Emailed to the team
  4. Q4. A classmate says 'I set a $20 budget, so Azure will stop my resources at $20.' What is wrong with this?
    • A) Nothing — that is exactly how budgets work
    • B) Budgets are alerts, not spending caps: you get an email, but spending continues, and alerts can be delayed
    • C) Budgets only work on weekends
    • D) The minimum budget is $200
  5. Q5. Practical: write the 5-line best-practice checklist you would tape to your monitor, one line each for identity, networking, security, cost, and operations.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. Least privilege minimizes damage from mistakes or compromised accounts by granting only necessary permissions.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. No more passwords in settings or code — Azure itself vouches for the app's identity.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Key Vault is the safe; managed identity is the key. Code and Git history are the worst places for secrets.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. This is the single most misunderstood cost control in Azure — budgets warn, they do not stop.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Example: 1) Least privilege — no Owner unless needed. 2) NSGs default-deny; open only required ports. 3) Secrets in Key Vault, never in code. 4) Budget alert on every subscription. 5) Name and tag every resource so it can be found and deleted.. A good checklist is short, specific, and covers all four rooms plus cost — the version above is one valid answer.

Day 28 — Final Project: Plan and Design60–90 minutes

A. Day 28 — Final Project: Plan and Design

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Write a one-page project plan with a goal, users, and measurable success criteria.
  • Draw an architecture diagram of the capstone solution.
  • List every Azure resource with its SKU/tier and estimate the monthly cost.
  • Define a test plan and a rollback plan before building anything.

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Days 1-27 — you will reuse deployment (Day 25), monitoring (Day 26), and best practices (Day 27)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Builders do not start with bricks; they start with a blueprint. A blueprint forces every hard decision up front — what gets built, what it costs, what 'done' looks like — while changes are still cheap eraser marks instead of expensive demolitions. Today you draw the blueprint for your capstone project. Tomorrow you build it. The day after, you demo it.

Here is the capstone specification — Capstone: Deploy and Operate a Monitored Web App on Azure. Mandatory: (1) a resource group with a clear naming convention; (2) a web app on App Service (Free F1 tier is fine) running the sample app; (3) automatic deployment from GitHub — a commit must redeploy with no manual steps; (4) a system-assigned managed identity enabled on the app; (5) diagnostic settings sending logs to a Log Analytics workspace; (6) an alert rule that emails you on HTTP 5xx errors; (7) tags on all resources; (8) a budget alert; (9) a README.md documenting everything. Optional stretch goals: store a secret in Key Vault and reference it from the app, add a deployment slot, or add a custom domain.

Your architecture diagram does not need fancy software — boxes and arrows on paper are how real architects start. Draw one box per Azure resource, arrows for how they connect (GitHub → App Service, App Service → Log Analytics, Alert rule → your email), and label each box with its SKU. Anyone reading it should be able to rebuild your system without asking you a single question.

Estimate cost with the Azure pricing calculator before you build — professionals never skip this. At Free F1 plus the free Log Analytics ingestion allowance, this project should total $0/month; write that number down and cite the calculator as your source. Finally, write two plans most beginners skip: a test plan (exactly which checks prove it works — page loads, commit redeploys, 404 appears in logs, alert rule exists) and a rollback plan (if a deploy breaks the app: revert the GitHub commit or redeploy the previous artifact — because Git keeps every version, 'undo' is always one commit away).

Reminder: portal UI, pricing, and free-tier limits change over time — if the calculator looks different from any screenshot you have seen, follow the labels, not your memory.
