F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Produce a complete written project plan: goal, architecture diagram, resource list, cost estimate, test plan, and rollback plan.

Prerequisites: Nothing but your notes from Days 1-27. No Azure portal work today.

Cost note: No cost. No Azure resources are created during planning — the estimate you produce should show $0/month.

Step 1. Re-read the capstone specification in today's concepts (the nine mandatory items). Copy them into a new document called PROJECT-PLAN.md on your computer — this is your requirements list.

Expected result: A document with the nine mandatory requirements listed.

Verify: Count them: resource group, web app, GitHub auto-deploy, managed identity, diagnostics, alert rule, tags, budget, README.

Step 2. Write the goal section: one paragraph stating what you are building and who it is for. Then write 4-5 measurable success criteria, e.g., 'A commit to GitHub is live on the site within 5 minutes with no portal clicks.'

Expected result: Goal paragraph plus numbered success criteria.

Verify: Each criterion is checkable with a yes/no — vague criteria like 'works well' are rewritten.

Troubleshooting: Stuck on criteria? Turn each mandatory requirement into one: 'Alert rule exists and is Enabled — yes/no.'

Step 3. Draw the architecture diagram on paper (or any diagram tool): one box per resource — GitHub repo, App Service plan (F1), Web App, Log Analytics workspace, Action group, Budget. Draw arrows: code flow, log flow, alert flow. Label each box with its SKU/tier.

Expected result: A diagram showing all resources and how they connect.

Verify: A friend could rebuild your system from the diagram alone.

Step 4. Build the resource table in your document: columns = Resource, Name (following your naming convention, e.g., rg-capstone), SKU/Tier, Estimated monthly cost. Fill one row per resource.

Expected result: A complete table, one row per resource.

Verify: Every box in your diagram appears as a row in the table — nothing extra, nothing missing.

Step 5. Open the Azure pricing calculator and price each row: App Service F1, Log Analytics (within free allowance). Write the total monthly estimate in your document and note the calculator as your source.

Expected result: A total monthly cost figure with the calculator cited.

Verify: The total is $0 (or a few dollars at most) — if it is higher, you picked a paid SKU somewhere; find it.

Troubleshooting: Calculator confusing: search each product name in its search box and pick the free/basic tier options.

Step 6. Write the test plan: list the exact checks you will run on Day 29, in order — (1) page loads, (2) commit redeploys automatically, (3) 404 appears in Log Analytics via KQL, (4) alert rule exists and is Enabled, (5) tags and budget in place.

Expected result: An ordered checklist of tests.

Verify: Each test maps to one success criterion from step 2.

Step 7. Write the rollback plan in two sentences: what you do if a deployment breaks the app (revert the commit in GitHub / redeploy the previous working version), and how long it should take.

Expected result: A short, concrete rollback procedure.

Verify: It names the exact mechanism (Git revert), not just 'fix it'.

Step 8. Final review: read your PROJECT-PLAN.md top to bottom against the nine mandatory requirements. Check every box or revise the plan until you can.

Expected result: All nine requirements traceable to a part of your plan.

Verify: For each requirement, you can point at the diagram row, table row, or test that covers it.

Troubleshooting: A requirement with no coverage: add the missing resource or test now, while it is still cheap.

Cleanup (prevent charges): No Azure resources were created today — nothing to delete. Save PROJECT-PLAN.md somewhere you will find it tomorrow.

Day 29 — Final Project: Build, Test, Document60–90 minutes

A. Day 29 — Final Project: Build, Test, Document

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Build the capstone solution in the correct order: foundation, app, wiring, monitoring.
  • Wire CI/CD so a GitHub commit deploys automatically.
  • Run smoke, functional, and security checks against the live project.
  • Write a README.md that lets a stranger rebuild and operate your project.

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Day 28 — you have PROJECT-PLAN.md with the architecture, resource table, test plan, and rollback plan
  • Your forked sample app repo on GitHub (from Day 25)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Today you build in foundation order: the things everything else depends on go first. Resource group, then the Log Analytics workspace, then the App Service plan and web app, then identity, then the deployment wiring, then monitoring, then tests, then documentation. Building out of order — say, wiring alerts before the app exists — creates the classic beginner failure: half-configured pieces that cannot be tested. Follow your plan's order; it exists for exactly this reason.

Test in layers, cheapest first. A smoke test asks 'does it turn on?' — the page loads. A functional test asks 'does it do the job?' — a commit redeploys automatically, a 404 shows up in the logs. A security check asks 'did I leave anything exposed?' — no secrets in app settings, managed identity enabled, HTTPS on. A resilience check asks 'does it survive a bump?' — restart the app and confirm it comes back. Run them in that order; there is no point load-testing a page that does not load.

Documentation is part of the product, not decoration. Your README.md is the document a stranger — or you, six months from now — uses to rebuild and operate this project. It needs: what the project is and why it exists; the architecture (your Day 28 diagram, described in words); prerequisites; step-by-step setup; how deployment works; how to monitor it (where the logs and alerts live); the monthly cost; and how to tear it all down. Write it as you build, not after — memory fades within hours.

Portal UI and free-tier limits change over time; if a blade has moved, search the portal for the feature name. Keep every resource on the free tier from your plan — if a creation blade defaults to a paid SKU, change it before clicking Create.

Overview of Azure App ServiceDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/app-service/overview VerifiedReference for App Service configuration while you build — deployment options, identity, diagnostics15 min
Azure Monitor overviewDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/overview VerifiedReference for diagnostic settings, Log Analytics, and alert rules while you wire monitoring15 min
Install the Azure CLIDocumentationhttps://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/install-azure-cli VerifiedOptional: install the CLI now so you can verify resources from the terminal as well as the portal10 min

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Build the full capstone project from your Day 28 plan, test it in layers, and document it in a README.

Prerequisites: Azure free account; PROJECT-PLAN.md; GitHub fork of the sample app.

Cost note: Free tier if you follow your plan (F1 App Service, Log Analytics within the free allowance). Double-check every SKU before creating — the portal sometimes defaults to paid tiers.

Step 1. Foundation: create resource group rg-capstone, then a Log Analytics workspace law-capstone inside it (same steps as Day 26).

Expected result: Both exist in rg-capstone.

Verify: Resource group lists the workspace.

Troubleshooting: Workspace name rejected: add initials.

Step 2. Create the App Service plan asp-capstone (Free F1) and the web app (Node 20 LTS, Linux) in rg-capstone, following your plan's naming.

Expected result: Web app running; Browse shows the default page.

Verify: Overview shows plan SKU F1.

Troubleshooting: If the SKU shows anything but F1, you accepted a paid default — delete the plan and recreate it on F1.

Step 3. Identity + tags: enable the system-assigned managed identity on the web app (Identity → System assigned → On → Save), then tag the resource group (Owner, Environment=Learning, Project=Capstone).

Expected result: Identity shows On with an Object ID; tags saved.

Verify: Object ID visible; Tags blade lists your three tags.

Step 4. Wiring — CI/CD: open the web app → Deployment Center → Source = GitHub → authorize → select your forked sample repo and branch → Build provider = GitHub Actions → Save. Watch the Actions tab until the workflow is green.

Expected result: Sample app deployed; Browse shows the sample page.

Verify: GitHub Actions run is green and the live URL shows the sample app.

Troubleshooting: Workflow fails: read the failing step's log (Day 25 troubleshooting) — usually a branch-name or Node-version mismatch.

Step 5. Prove CD works: edit one line of text in your GitHub fork, commit, wait for the Actions run to finish, and refresh the live URL.

Expected result: Your edited text is live with zero portal clicks during the release.

Verify: Change visible on the public URL. Tick off the matching success criterion in PROJECT-PLAN.md.

Troubleshooting: Stale page: hard-refresh (Ctrl+Shift+R).

Step 6. Monitoring — logs: web app → Diagnostic settings → add send-to-law sending AppServiceHTTPLogs and AppServiceAppLogs to law-capstone. Also turn on Application logging (Filesystem) = Information under App Service logs.

Expected result: Diagnostic setting saved; filesystem logging On.

Verify: Both blades show the saved settings.

Troubleshooting: Save errors: workspace and app must be in the same region.

Step 7. Monitoring — alert: create alert rule alert-5xx-capstone on signal 'Http 5xx' (Total > 0 over 5 min), with an action group emailing you (same pattern as Day 26).

Expected result: Alert rule listed as Enabled.

Verify: Alerts → Alert rules shows alert-5xx-capstone Enabled.

Step 8. Cost guard: create budget budget-capstone-20 ($20/month, alerts at 50% and 100% to your email) scoped to rg-capstone.

Expected result: Budget listed in Cost Management.

Verify: Open it — your email is on both alert conditions.

Step 9. Smoke + functional tests: (1) Browse the app — page loads. (2) Restart the app (Overview → Restart) — it comes back (resilience). (3) Visit a bad URL for a 404, wait ~10 minutes, run the Day 26 KQL status-code query in Logs — the 404 appears (functional).

Expected result: All three checks pass.

Verify: KQL results include your 404; the app survived the restart.

Troubleshooting: 404 not in logs yet: log delivery lags — wait longer, then re-run the query.

Step 10. Security check: web app → Configuration → Application settings — confirm no secrets stored. Confirm managed identity is still On and the site loads over HTTPS.

Expected result: No secrets; identity On; https URL works.

Verify: You can state where a secret would go instead (Key Vault + managed identity).

Step 11. Documentation: in your GitHub fork, click Add file → Create new file, name it README.md, and write: project description, architecture summary, prerequisites, setup steps, how deployment works, how to monitor (logs query + alert name), monthly cost, and teardown steps. Commit it.

Expected result: README.md renders on your repo's front page.

Verify: Open the repo in a private/incognito window — the README alone explains the project.

Step 12. Final plan check: open PROJECT-PLAN.md and tick every success criterion against the live project. Anything unticked is tomorrow's first job.

Expected result: Most or all criteria ticked.

Verify: Each mandatory requirement (Day 28's nine) is visibly satisfied in the portal or README.

Troubleshooting: Missing alert or diagnostics: go back to steps 6-7 now — Day 30's demo needs them.

Cleanup (prevent charges): DO NOT delete anything yet — you need this entire project for your Day 30 demo. AFTER the demo, delete it all: Portal → Resource groups → rg-capstone → Delete resource group → type rg-capstone → Delete. Also delete budget-capstone-20 under Cost Management → Budgets.

G. Practical Assignment

Break something safely: stop your web app (Overview → Stop), wait one minute, then start it again and watch the metrics dip and recover. Write two sentences on what you observed — this is the story you will tell in your demo.

H. Knowledge Assessment

  1. Q1. What is the correct build order for the capstone, and why?
    • A) Alerts first, then the app, then the resource group
    • B) Foundation first (resource group, workspace, app, identity), then wiring (CI/CD), then monitoring, then tests, then docs — each layer depends on the previous one
    • C) Documentation first, everything else in any order
    • D) Order does not matter in the cloud
  2. Q2. What does a smoke test verify?
    • A) The app handles 10,000 users
    • B) The basics work — the page loads, the app starts ('does it turn on?')
    • C) The code has no spelling mistakes
    • D) The budget alert fires
  3. Q3. A deployment just broke the live app. Per your rollback plan, what do you do first?
    • A) Delete the resource group and start over
    • B) Revert the bad commit in GitHub (or redeploy the previous working version) so the pipeline restores the last good state
    • C) Email Microsoft support
    • D) Wait and hope it fixes itself
  4. Q4. Which README section matters most to someone who inherits your project?
    • A) Your favorite color scheme
    • B) Step-by-step setup and teardown plus how deployment and monitoring work — everything needed to rebuild and operate it
    • C) A long apology for bugs
    • D) Your biography
  5. Q5. Practical: write the 'How to monitor this project' section of your README in 4-6 lines.
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. You cannot wire monitoring for an app that does not exist or test a deployment that was never configured — dependencies dictate order.
  2. Q2 — Answer: B. Smoke tests are the cheapest, fastest checks: if the page does not load, deeper testing is pointless.
  3. Q3 — Answer: B. Git keeps every version, so reverting the commit re-runs the pipeline with the last known-good code — the fastest safe recovery.
  4. Q4 — Answer: B. A README's job is reproducibility and operability: setup, deploy, monitor, cost, teardown.
  5. Q5 — Answer: Example: Logs: web app → Monitoring → Logs (law-capstone workspace); query AppServiceHTTPLogs for status codes. Metrics: Monitoring → Metrics → Requests / Http 5xx. Alerts: alert-5xx-capstone emails me when 5xx > 0 in 5 min. Budget: budget-capstone-20 warns at 50%/100% of $20.. A good section names the exact blades, workspace, queries, and alert so a stranger can find everything without asking.

Day 30 — Demo Day, Final Review, and Your Learning Roadmap60–90 minutes

A. Day 30 — Demo Day, Final Review, and Your Learning Roadmap

Estimated duration: 60–90 minutes

B. Learning Objectives

  • Deliver a 10-minute live demo of your capstone project.
  • Complete a final troubleshooting review of the most common failure modes.
  • Assess your own skills against the workshop's learning goals.
  • Build a personal 90-day learning roadmap starting with AZ-900.

C. Prerequisites

  • Azure free account (created on Day 1)
  • Completed Days 1-29 — your capstone project is built, tested, and documented (rg-capstone still exists)

D. Concepts Explained Simply

Today you do what professionals do at the end of every project: demonstrate it, review what broke along the way, and plan what is next. A good demo tells a story in five beats: Hook (the problem — 'deploying by hand is slow and risky') → Architecture (your diagram, 60 seconds) → Live tour (the site, a commit redeploying itself, the logs query, the alert rule) → Lessons (one thing that broke and how you fixed it — audiences trust demos with scars) → Close (what you would add next). Ten minutes, no more. Rehearse out loud once; everything feels longer on stage than in your head. And keep backup screenshots — live demos fail at the worst moments, and a screenshot of it working is a perfectly professional save.

Troubleshooting review — the greatest hits from 30 days, and their fixes: (1) App shows the default page / old version: deployment still running or browser cache — wait, restart the app, hard-refresh. (2) GitHub Actions run is red: open the failing step's log — usually a branch-name or runtime-version mismatch in the Deployment Center settings. (3) No data in Log Analytics: the #1 cause is impatience (10-15 min delay) or the diagnostic setting never saved / wrong region. (4) Alert never fires: condition threshold misread (e.g., 'greater than 5' when you only generated 2 errors) or the action group's email went to spam. (5) Resource creation fails: name already taken, wrong region for the SKU, or a paid default you did not change. (6) Costs surprise you: you skipped the pricing calculator or left a paid SKU running — budgets warn, they do not stop spending.

Your roadmap. The natural next step is AZ-900: Microsoft Certified: Azure Fundamentals — the entry-level certification covering cloud concepts, Azure architecture and services, and governance. You have already learned a large part of it in this workshop; the official learning path (linked below) fills the gaps. After AZ-900, pick a lane: AZ-104 (Azure Administrator) if you like operating infrastructure, AZ-204 (Azure Developer) if you like building apps, AI-900 if AI services excite you. This workshop is not affiliated with, endorsed, or certified by Microsoft — it is your preparation, not their program.

Keep three habits and you will keep growing: build weekly (the free tier and the Learn sandbox are your gym), read the docs (one docs page per week compounds fast), and write it down (your journal habit from this workshop is a career asset). Thirty days ago the portal was a foreign country. Today you can plan, deploy, monitor, secure, and demo a real cloud system. That is not beginner luck — that is a skill set. Go use it.

F. Hands-On Workshop

Objective: Run the final demo checklist, rehearse your demo, do a last troubleshooting pass, then tear everything down.

Prerequisites: Capstone project live (rg-capstone); PROJECT-PLAN.md; README.md.

Cost note: Free tier. This lab ends with the final teardown — after today, nothing from the workshop should remain in your subscription.

Step 1. Final validation — work through this demo checklist against your live project, ticking each: (1) site loads over HTTPS; (2) managed identity is On; (3) tags on the resource group; (4) budget-capstone-20 exists; (5) alert-5xx-capstone is Enabled; (6) README.md renders on your repo.

Expected result: All six ticked.

Verify: Each item confirmed in the portal or on GitHub — no item taken on faith.

Troubleshooting: Anything unticked: fix it now using the Day 29 steps — the demo needs all six.

Step 2. Live CI/CD proof: make one final tiny commit in GitHub (e.g., update the README's 'last demoed' date), watch the Actions workflow go green, and refresh the live site.

Expected result: The change deploys itself; the site reflects it.

Verify: Zero portal clicks between commit and the live change.

Troubleshooting: Workflow slow: GitHub-hosted runners occasionally queue — wait up to 10 minutes.

Step 3. Monitoring proof: open the web app → Monitoring → Metrics and show the Requests chart; then open Logs and run your status-code KQL query live.

Expected result: Chart shows traffic; query returns rows.

Verify: Both work on the first try — if not, note the fix; honesty beats perfection in a demo.

Troubleshooting: Query slow: narrow the time range to the last hour.

Step 4. Take backup screenshots now: the live site, the green Actions run, the metrics chart, the KQL results, and the alert rule blade. Save them in a folder called demo-backup.

Expected result: Five screenshots saved.

Verify: Open each image — it is readable and clearly labeled by its filename.

Step 5. Rehearse your 10-minute demo out loud, following the five beats (Hook → Architecture → Live tour → Lessons → Close). Time yourself with a phone timer.

Expected result: You finish between 8 and 12 minutes.

Verify: If you ran long, cut the architecture section first — the live tour is the star.

Troubleshooting: Nervous about the live parts: that is what the screenshots are for — narrate over them if anything fails.

Step 6. Troubleshooting pass: deliberately pick ONE scenario from today's concepts (e.g., stop the app, then start it) and walk through detecting and fixing it using metrics and logs, narrating as you go.

Expected result: You detect the issue in metrics/logs and recover the app.

Verify: You can explain each step of the Detect → Triage → Investigate → Mitigate → Learn loop as you did it.

Step 7. Self-assessment: grade yourself honestly on the four workshop pillars — deploy (can you CI/CD a change?), monitor (can you query logs and read metrics?), secure (managed identity, Key Vault thinking, least privilege?), operate (tags, budgets, Advisor, docs?). Write one sentence per pillar on what is solid and what needs work.

Expected result: Four honest sentences.

Verify: Each sentence names something specific, not 'I need to learn more.'

Step 8. Write your 90-day roadmap: weeks 1-4 finish the AZ-900 learning path; weeks 5-8 practice exam + book the exam; weeks 9-12 pick your lane (AZ-104 / AZ-204 / AI-900) and start its path. Save it as ROADMAP.md next to PROJECT-PLAN.md.

Expected result: ROADMAP.md with dated milestones.

Verify: Each milestone has a concrete deliverable (e.g., 'complete path', 'book exam').

Step 9. Final teardown: Portal → Resource groups → delete rg-capstone (type the name to confirm). Then Cost Management → Budgets → delete budget-capstone-20. Then scan the full resource-group list for any leftover rg-deploy-day25, rg-monitor-day26, rg-review-day27, or rg-capstone entries and delete stragglers.

Expected result: No workshop resource groups remain; no workshop budgets remain.

Verify: Resource groups list shows none of the four names; Budgets list is empty of workshop budgets.

Troubleshooting: Delete blocked by a lock or a still-provisioning resource: wait 5 minutes and retry; locks are removed under the resource group's Settings → Locks.

Cleanup (prevent charges): This lab IS the cleanup: every workshop resource group (rg-deploy-day25, rg-monitor-day26, rg-review-day27, rg-capstone) and every workshop budget is deleted in the steps above. Verify the resource-group list is clear — your subscription should look the way it did on Day 1, plus your knowledge.

G. Practical Assignment

Deliver your demo to a real human — friend, family member, colleague, or record it on your phone. Afterwards, write down the three questions they asked (or the three you asked yourself). Those questions are your personalized study guide for AZ-900.

Final Project — Capstone: Deploy and Operate a Monitored Web App on Azure

Build a complete, real-world slice of an Azure workload: a web app running on Azure App Service, wired to Application Insights for monitoring, reading its secrets from Azure Key Vault through a managed identity, guarded by a budget alert, and then fully cleaned up. You will operate it for 3 to 5 days like a real service owner: checking health, reading metrics, and investigating one alert. Everything is designed for the free tier and free account credit. Note: portal screens, pricing, and free-tier limits change over time, so treat exact menu labels as guidance and read the current labels on screen. Estimated effort: 4 to 6 hours of building, plus a few minutes a day of operating.

Mandatory components

  • One resource group holding every resource for this project, in a region close to you
  • An Azure App Service web app on the Free (F1) tier with a simple sample app deployed and reachable over HTTPS
  • Application Insights connected to the web app, collecting requests, with one custom event or one availability test configured
  • One secret stored in Azure Key Vault, read by the web app through a system-assigned managed identity (no passwords, keys, or connection strings in code or app settings)
  • A Cost Management budget with an alert threshold (for example, 50 percent and 100 percent of a small amount) so you see how budget alerts behave
  • One metric alert rule (for example, HTTP server errors or response time) that notifies you by email via an action group
  • A project README describing the architecture, the build steps you followed, and what you learned
  • Full cleanup at the end: delete the resource group and confirm in Cost Management that no new charges appear

Optional enhancements

  • A staging deployment slot with a swap to production
  • A GitHub Actions workflow that builds and deploys the app on every push
  • A custom domain name with a free managed TLS certificate
  • An Azure Function backend called by the web app (for example, a health-check or queue-processing endpoint)
  • A KQL query pinned to a dashboard showing request volume and failures over the operating period

Build steps

  1. Plan on paper first: sketch the architecture (web app, App Insights, Key Vault, budget, alert), pick a region, and write down the resource names you will use. Naming convention example: capstone-rg, capstone-web-<yourname>, capstone-kv-<yourname>.
  2. Create the resource group: Portal - Search Resource groups - Create, select your subscription and region, name it, and create. Everything in this project lives here so one delete removes everything.
  3. Create the App Service plan (Free F1 tier) and the Web App: Portal - Create a resource - Web App, select your resource group, give the app a globally unique name, choose a runtime you know (for example, Node or Python), select the Free F1 plan, and create.
  4. Deploy a simple sample app: use the App Service quickstart sample or deploy from VS Code with the Azure App Service extension. Browse to https://<yourapp>.azurewebsites.net and confirm the sample page loads.
  5. Enable Application Insights on the web app and open it. Generate traffic by refreshing the page, then check the Application Insights overview for incoming requests. This confirms telemetry is flowing.
  6. Create a Key Vault in the same resource group and add one secret (for example, a fake API key named DemoApiKey). On the web app, turn on the system-assigned managed identity, then grant that identity the Key Vault Secrets User role on the vault. Update the app to read the secret from Key Vault at runtime and verify it works. Confirm there is no secret value in your code or app settings.
  7. Create a budget: Portal - Cost Management - Budgets - Add, scope it to your resource group, set a small amount and alert thresholds at 50 percent and 100 percent of actual cost, and add your email as the recipient. Remember: this sends alerts; it does not stop spending.
  8. Create a metric alert rule: on the web app, go to Alerts - Create alert rule, pick a signal such as Http5xx, set a threshold, create an action group with your email, and save. Trigger a test alert if the portal offers a way to do so, and confirm the email arrives.
  9. Operate the app for 3 to 5 days: each day, open Application Insights, note request counts and any failures, and run one KQL query in Logs (for example: requests | where success == false | take 20). Write down what you observe.
  10. Run one controlled experiment: restart the web app from the portal, then watch the availability test and the metrics recover. Note how long the gap in telemetry lasts.
  11. Write the README: architecture summary with a simple text diagram, the exact steps you followed, how you verified monitoring and alerts, the security approach (managed identity, no secrets in code), and lessons learned or what you would improve.
  12. Clean up: delete the resource group (Portal - Resource groups - select it - Delete resource group, type the name to confirm). Then open Cost Management - Cost analysis and confirm the project resources show no further spend.

Evaluation rubric (100 points)

CriterionPointsWhat good looks like
Working web app deployed20App Service web app on the Free tier is reachable over HTTPS and serves the deployed sample app. Deployment method is documented.
Monitoring and alerting20Application Insights collects request telemetry; one custom event or availability test exists; one metric alert rule with an email action group is configured and was tested.
Secure secret handling20Secret lives in Key Vault and is read through the system-assigned managed identity. No secret values appear in code, app settings, or the README.
Cost control and cleanup15A budget with alert thresholds is configured and understood (alerts, not caps). The resource group was deleted at the end and Cost Management confirms no ongoing spend.
README quality15README explains the architecture, lists the build steps, shows how monitoring and alerts were verified, documents the security approach, and includes lessons learned.
Operations evidence10Notes or screenshots from the 3 to 5 day operating period: daily metric observations, one KQL query with results, and the restart experiment outcome.

Glossary of Azure Terminology

TermDefinition
ARM (Azure Resource Manager)The deployment and management layer of Azure; every resource you create is processed through it.
Availability ZoneA physically separate datacenter within a region, with independent power, cooling, and networking.
Azure App ServicePaaS for hosting web apps, APIs, and mobile backends without managing servers.
Azure ArcExtends Azure management (policy, monitoring) to on-premises servers and other clouds.
Azure BackupManaged backup service for VMs, databases, and file shares, stored in a Recovery Services vault.
Azure CLICommand-line tool (az commands) for creating and managing Azure resources from a terminal.
Azure FunctionsServerless compute that runs small pieces of code in response to events called triggers.
Azure Key VaultManaged store for secrets, encryption keys, and certificates, with full audit logging.
Azure MonitorThe platform monitoring service that collects metrics and logs from Azure resources.
Azure PolicyEnforces organizational rules (allowed regions, required tags) and reports compliance at scale.
BicepMicrosoft's concise declarative language for infrastructure as code; compiles to ARM templates.
Blob StorageObject storage for unstructured data such as images, videos, and backups.
BudgetA Cost Management spending threshold that sends alerts when crossed; it does not stop spending.
CapEx (Capital Expenditure)Upfront spending on physical assets, such as buying servers; the traditional on-premises model.
CIDR (Classless Inter-Domain Routing)Notation like 10.0.0.0/16 that defines a range of IP addresses.
Cold StartThe delay when a serverless function runs for the first time after being idle.
ContributorRBAC role that can create and manage resources but cannot grant access to others.
DNS (Domain Name System)Translates domain names (example.com) into IP addresses.
Entra ID (Microsoft Entra ID)Microsoft's cloud identity service for sign-in, MFA, and access management (formerly Azure AD).
GRS (Geo-Redundant Storage)Storage replication to a paired secondary region, for protection against regional outages.
Hybrid CloudCombining on-premises or private cloud infrastructure with public cloud services.
IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service)Rented virtual hardware (VMs, networks, disks); you manage the OS and applications.
IaC (Infrastructure as Code)Defining infrastructure in version-controlled files (Bicep, ARM, Terraform) instead of clicking.
KQL (Kusto Query Language)Read-only query language for analyzing logs in Log Analytics and Application Insights.
LRS (Locally Redundant Storage)Three copies of data within a single datacenter; the cheapest redundancy option.
Load BalancerDistributes incoming network traffic across multiple backend resources.
Log AnalyticsThe Azure Monitor workspace where logs are stored and queried with KQL.
Managed IdentityAn Entra ID identity attached to an Azure resource, so apps authenticate without stored credentials.
Management GroupA container for subscriptions; used to apply policy and RBAC across many subscriptions at once.
MetricA numeric measurement over time (CPU percent, request count) used in dashboards and alerts.
MFA (Multi-Factor Authentication)Sign-in that requires two or more verification factors, such as password plus phone approval.
NSG (Network Security Group)A set of allow/deny firewall rules for traffic to and from subnets or network interfaces.
OpEx (Operational Expenditure)Pay-as-you-go spending on services as you consume them; the cloud pricing model.
PaaS (Platform as a Service)A managed platform (App Service, SQL Database) where you bring code and data, not servers.
Private EndpointA private IP inside your VNet for a PaaS service, keeping traffic off the public internet.
RBAC (Role-Based Access Control)Assigns roles (Owner, Contributor, Reader) to identities at a scope to control who can do what.
ReaderRBAC role that can view resources but cannot create, change, or delete them.
RegionA geographic area containing one or more Azure datacenters.
ResourceAny manageable item in Azure: a VM, storage account, web app, database, and so on.
Resource GroupA logical container for related resources that share a lifecycle and are managed together.
SaaS (Software as a Service)Complete software delivered over the internet, such as Microsoft 365; you just use it.
Scale (horizontal / vertical)Horizontal (scale out): add more instances. Vertical (scale up): use a bigger instance.
SecretA sensitive value such as a password or API key, stored in Key Vault rather than in code.
SLA (Service Level Agreement)Microsoft's published uptime commitment for a service, such as 99.95 percent.
SSH (Secure Shell)Encrypted protocol for remote command-line administration of Linux machines.
Storage AccountThe container that provides the namespace for Blob, Files, Queue, and Table storage.
SubnetA range of IP addresses carved out of a virtual network to group resources.
SubscriptionThe billing and access boundary in Azure; resource groups live inside it.
TagA name/value label on resources (for example, env=prod) used for organization and cost tracking.
TenantA dedicated instance of Microsoft Entra ID representing one organization.
VNet (Virtual Network)Your isolated private network in Azure, where resources communicate securely.
VNet PeeringConnects two virtual networks so their resources communicate privately over the Microsoft backbone.
VM (Virtual Machine)An emulated computer running in the cloud; you manage its operating system.
ZRS (Zone-Redundant Storage)Storage copies spread across availability zones within a single region.

Troubleshooting Guide — Common Beginner Errors

Error / SymptomLikely CauseFix
Portal: subscription is missing or shows 'You do not have access'You are signed in to the wrong Microsoft Entra directory (tenant), or your access was never granted in this directory.In the portal, click the gear icon (Settings) - Directories + subscriptions, and switch to the directory that holds your subscription. If it is still missing, ask the subscription owner to grant you access.
Free trial subscription shows as DisabledThe 30-day period ended or the $200 credit was fully used.Upgrade to a pay-as-you-go subscription in the portal (Subscriptions - select it - Upgrade). Set a budget alert immediately so spending stays visible. Delete anything you do not need first.
az login opens nothing, hangs, or fails in the terminalThe browser-based sign-in flow cannot complete in your environment.Run: az login --use-device-code. Open the shown URL on any device, enter the code, and complete sign-in there. Then confirm with: az account list --output table.
Deployment fails with 'Resource provider not registered' (for example, Microsoft.Compute)The subscription has never used that resource type, so its provider is not registered.Register it once per subscription: az provider register --namespace Microsoft.Compute. Or in the portal: Subscriptions - your subscription - Resource providers - find it - Register. Wait a few minutes and retry.
VM deployment fails: quota or 'Operation could not be completed as it results in exceeding quota'Your subscription has a limit on vCPUs for that VM family in that region (new accounts start low).Try a smaller VM size or a different region first. For a lasting fix, request a quota increase: Portal - search 'Quotas' - request more vCPUs for the VM family and region.
AuthorizationFailed / 403 error when creating or changing a resourceYour identity lacks the RBAC permission for that action at that scope.Check your role: open the resource group - Access control (IAM) - View my access. Ask an Owner to grant you Contributor (or a narrower role) at the right scope. Never work around this by sharing someone else's account.
Storage account creation fails: name is already in useStorage account names must be globally unique across all of Azure, 3 to 24 characters, lowercase letters and numbers only.Pick a more unique name, for example adding your initials and a number: mystoreabc123. Validate: az storage account check-name --name mystoreabc123.
Cannot SSH into a Linux VM (or RDP into Windows) - connection times outThe network security group does not allow your inbound traffic on port 22 (SSH) or 3389 (RDP).Open the VM - Networking - add an inbound rule allowing TCP 22 (or 3389) from your public IP only, not from Any. Confirm the VM has a public IP and is running, then retry.
Web app on the Free tier returns 403/503 or 'quota exceeded'The Free (F1) plan has strict daily limits (CPU minutes, bandwidth). Hitting them pauses the app until the quota resets.Check the Quotas page on the web app to confirm. Reduce load or wait for the daily reset. For real workloads, scale the App Service plan to Basic or higher.
Key Vault: 'Access denied' when the app or you try to read a secretThe identity (you or the app's managed identity) has no permission on the vault.Grant the Key Vault Secrets User role to that identity on the vault: Key Vault - Access control (IAM) - Add role assignment. If the vault uses the older access-policy model, add an access policy instead. Then retry.
Budget alert never arrived even though spending passed the thresholdBudgets are evaluated on cost data that can lag 8 to 24 hours, and alert emails can take another hour. Also, alerts do not stop spending.Wait a day and check Cost Management - Budgets - your budget for the evaluated spend. Confirm the alert recipients list has the right email. Treat budgets as delayed notifications, not real-time guards.
Cannot delete a resource group: deletion fails or hangsA resource lock (CanNotDelete) is protecting something inside, or a resource has dependencies that block deletion.Check for locks: Resource groups - your group - Locks. Delete the lock, then delete the group again. If it still fails, delete the blocking resources individually first.
VM's public DNS name does not resolve (for example, myvm.eastus.cloudapp.azure.com)The public IP has no DNS label configured, so the name does not exist.Open the public IP resource - Configuration - set a DNS name label (must be unique in the region), save, and wait a few minutes for DNS to propagate.
'The location is not available for this subscription' when creating a resourceSome regions or resource types are restricted for new or trial subscriptions.Choose a major region such as East US or West Europe and retry. If you must use that region, request access through a support request.
az: command not found, or a command fails on an old CLI versionThe Azure CLI is not installed, is outdated, or the needed extension is missing.Check the version: az version. Upgrade following the official install guide. For missing commands, install the extension: az extension add --name <extension-name>.
CLI commands act on the wrong subscriptionThe CLI is logged in with several subscriptions and the default is not the one you want.List them: az account list --output table. Then set the right one: az account set --subscription <subscription-id-or-name>. Re-run the command.
Unexpected charges on the billA resource was left running (VMs and disks bill even when the VM is only stopped, not deallocated) or an experiment was never deleted.Open Cost Management - Cost analysis, group by Resource, and find the expensive one. Stop (deallocate) or delete what you do not need. Set a budget alert so this cannot surprise you again.
Azure Function is slow on first run or times out on the Consumption planCold start: after idle time the function host must start before running your code. The Consumption plan also has execution time limits.This is expected on Consumption. For latency-sensitive or long-running functions, move to the Flex Consumption or Premium plan, which keeps instances warm.

Final Self-Assessment

Final check before you call yourself Azure-ready. Fifteen questions covering all three weeks: cloud models, resource management, identity, networking, storage, security, monitoring, and cost. Answer honestly, then use the scoring guide to plan your next step.

  1. Q1. With an Azure Virtual Machine, who is responsible for patching the operating system?
    • A) Microsoft, always
    • B) You, the customer
    • C) Nobody - VMs are never patched
    • D) The internet service provider
  2. Q2. What happens when you delete a resource group?
    • A) Nothing - resource groups cannot be deleted
    • B) Only the group metadata is removed
    • C) All resources inside the group are deleted
    • D) The subscription is cancelled
  3. Q3. A developer needs to deploy apps in a resource group but must NOT change access. Which role do you assign?
    • A) Owner
    • B) Reader
    • C) Contributor
    • D) Global Administrator
  4. Q4. What is the recommended way for a web app to read a database password?
    • A) Hardcode it in the application code
    • B) Put it in a public configuration file
    • C) Store it in Key Vault and read it using a managed identity
    • D) Email it to the team whenever it changes
  5. Q5. By default, can two subnets in the SAME virtual network communicate with each other?
    • A) No, never
    • B) Yes, traffic between subnets is allowed by default; NSGs can restrict it
    • C) Only if they have the same name
    • D) Only after buying a VPN gateway
  6. Q6. What does GRS give you that LRS does not?
    • A) Faster disk performance
    • B) A copy of your data in a secondary region
    • C) Free storage
    • D) Larger file sizes
  7. Q7. Availability Zones are mainly used to protect against:
    • A) Accidental file deletion
    • B) Datacenter-level failures within a region
    • C) Stolen passwords
    • D) High prices
  8. Q8. An App Service plan primarily defines:
    • A) Your custom domain name
    • B) The pricing tier and compute resources shared by apps on the plan
    • C) The language your app is written in
    • D) Your backup retention
  9. Q9. In Azure Functions, a trigger is:
    • A) A security alert
    • B) The event that starts the function (HTTP request, timer, new blob, queue message)
    • C) A billing threshold
    • D) A type of storage account
  10. Q10. In the KQL query 'requests | where success == false | take 10', what does 'take 10' do?
    • A) Deletes 10 rows
    • B) Returns the first 10 rows of the result
    • C) Waits 10 seconds
    • D) Counts 10 servers
  11. Q11. How do metrics and logs differ in Azure Monitor?
    • A) They are the same thing
    • B) Metrics are numeric time-series values; logs are detailed event records you query with KQL
    • C) Logs are numbers; metrics are text
    • D) Metrics can only be viewed in the CLI
  12. Q12. What is the difference between Azure Policy and RBAC?
    • A) They are identical
    • B) RBAC controls who can perform actions; Policy controls what configurations resources are allowed to have
    • C) Policy is only for billing
    • D) RBAC is only for on-premises servers
  13. Q13. Which Cost Management statement is TRUE?
    • A) A budget stops resources when spending reaches the limit
    • B) A budget sends alerts when thresholds are crossed, but does not cap spending
    • C) Tags cannot be used in cost reports
    • D) Cost data appears in real time, second by second
  14. Q14. Which pair of parameters is REQUIRED for 'az group create'?
    • A) --name and --location
    • B) --subscription and --tag
    • C) --owner and --budget
    • D) --vm-size and --image
  15. Q15. You see an unexpected charge. What is the best FIRST step?
    • A) Delete your subscription immediately
    • B) Open Cost Management - Cost analysis, group by Resource, identify what is spending, then shut down or delete what you do not need
    • C) Ignore it - small charges always reverse themselves
    • D) Create a new account
Answer key & explanations (open after you finish)
  1. Q1 — Answer: B. VMs are IaaS: Microsoft manages the physical host; you own the guest OS, patching, and configuration. PaaS services like App Service shift patching to Microsoft.
  2. Q2 — Answer: C. Deleting a resource group deletes everything inside it. That is why one group per project is such a powerful cleanup pattern.
  3. Q3 — Answer: C. Contributor deploys and manages resources but cannot assign roles. Owner would over-grant; Reader cannot change anything.
  4. Q4 — Answer: C. Managed identity plus Key Vault means no secret exists in code or settings, access is audited, and there is nothing to leak or rotate manually.
  5. Q5 — Answer: B. Azure routes traffic between subnets in a VNet automatically. You add NSG rules when you want to restrict that traffic.
  6. Q6 — Answer: B. GRS (geo-redundant storage) asynchronously replicates to a paired region. LRS keeps all copies inside one datacenter.
  7. Q7 — Answer: B. Zones are separate datacenters in a region. Spreading an app across zones keeps it running if one datacenter fails.
  8. Q8 — Answer: B. You pay for the plan, not the apps. Tier, size, and instance count on the plan determine cost and performance.
  9. Q9 — Answer: B. Every function has exactly one trigger. Bindings then connect the running function to input/output data sources.
  10. Q10 — Answer: B. take limits the number of rows returned - useful when exploring large tables before writing a bigger query.
  11. Q11 — Answer: B. Use metrics for dashboards and fast threshold alerts. Use logs for investigation, correlation, and detailed analysis.
  12. Q12 — Answer: B. RBAC = identity permissions. Policy = resource rules (allowed regions, SKUs, required tags) plus compliance reporting.
  13. Q13 — Answer: B. Budgets notify; they do not enforce a hard stop. Cost data typically lags hours, which is another reason alerts are early warnings, not guards.
  14. Q14 — Answer: A. az group create --name myRg --location eastus is the minimal form. Every resource group needs a name and a location for its metadata.
  15. Q15 — Answer: B. Investigate first: find the resource driving the cost, then deallocate or delete it. Deleting the subscription destroys your work and proof of what happened.

Scoring guide

13 to 15 correct: Strong foundation. You are ready for the AZ-900 exam and for intermediate topics: deeper networking, Bicep and Terraform, and containers. 9 to 12: Solid base. Review the weekly review for each question you missed, redo one or two labs from those days, then attempt the capstone. 5 to 8: The concepts are forming but the hands-on muscle is missing. Repeat the labs (not just the reading) for your weakest week and retake this assessment in a week. 0 to 4: Restart the workshop at a slower pace, one day at a time, and do every lab step in the portal or CLI - Azure is learned by building, not by reading.
